## 📚 Paper List

- **[SimVLA: Attributing Gains in VLA Models Through Controlled Ablation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18224)**  `arXiv:2602.18224`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuankai Luo, Woping Chen, Tong Liang, Zhenguo Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models often bundle architectural changes with different pretraining data, backbone scales, and optimization recipes, obscuring what actually drives progress. We introduce SimVLA, a deliberately minimal VLA, a standard vision-language backbone with a lightweight continuous-action head, as a controlled testbed for this attribution problem; our contribution is methodological and empirical (a controlled attribution study and a minimal-baseline calibration), not a new technique. Across LIBERO, CALVIN, WidowX, and Google Robot, one-knob-at-a-time ablations show that training dynamics are the largest measured driver, producing performance swings of $\Delta$$\approx$54--94\%, while task configuration and architecture have smaller effects under matched settings. Despite using a small backbone and no additional large-scale robot-data VLA pretraining before benchmark-specific training, SimVLA reaches 98.6\% average success on LIBERO, remains competitive across the other benchmarks, and transfers to held-out real-robot scenes. These results suggest that simple, carefully controlled VLA baselines remain underexplored and provide a calibration point for future architectural claims within the VLM-encoder plus lightweight-head family. Website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[UniVEP: Unifying Velocity and Endpoint Prediction for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26103)**  `arXiv:2611.26103`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengtu Liang, Qiwei Liang, Tao Lin, Shihang Weng, Boyang Cai, Xiangyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching robot policies usually use one prediction target throughout action generation, even though recovering actions from noise and polishing nearly clean actions bring different challenges. We systematically compare velocity prediction (V), endpoint prediction (X), and velocity-weighted endpoint prediction (X--V) across multiple robot policies, benchmarks, and inference budgets. On StarVLA and LiLa-WAM, X--V works best for few-step generation, while V gains more from iterative refinement. We further uncover their complementary strengths across noise levels: clean actions have concentrated covariance spectra, endpoint predictions stay more stable under high noise, and velocity predictions offer more accurate corrections near clean actions. Inspired by these findings, we introduce UniVEP, which learns both prediction targets together with consistency supervision and switches from endpoint recovery to velocity refinement as noise drops. Each step only needs one network run, and joint training also boosts single-step endpoint generation. Tests on simulated and real-world manipulation show better success rates and efficiency. On RoboTwin, UniVEP hits 61.36% success with one StarVLA run, beating the best single-target baseline at sixteen runs (59.96%). With LiLa-WAM, two runs outperform the best sixteen-step single-target baseline and cut inference latency by 77.1%.
  </details>

- **[Learning What Actions Change: Action Effect Matching for Dynamics-Faithful World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25980)**  `arXiv:2611.25980`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _John Won, Yonghoon Dong, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models aim to predict environment dynamics under actions, enabling the prediction of action consequences without executing them in the real world. Yet despite producing increasingly realistic videos, current world models often fail to capture how small action changes affect future observations. This limits their use in tasks such as robotic policy evaluation and model-based planning, where nearly identical actions can lead to qualitatively different outcomes. To address this, we propose AFFECT, an auxiliary training objective for learning the distribution of visual changes induced by action perturbations. Specifically, we inject temporally correlated noise into the actions of a policy trained on task-agnostic human teleoperation. We then train the world model so that its generated videos respond to this noise as the recorded videos do, without requiring repeated executions from the same initial state. Across OGBench simulation and real-world DROID and ALOHA-2 setups, AFFECT makes world-model rollouts more faithful to real outcomes. On DROID, the Pearson correlation between policy success rates in the world model and on the real robot rises from 0.47 to 0.79, alongside up to 23% lower FVD. These gains carry over to planning, where selecting actions with AFFECT-trained world models improves task success by up to 8.3%.
  </details>

- **[Inhabitant: An Agent Framework for Long-Horizon Human-Scene Interaction with Spatial Control and Quantitative Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25005)**  `arXiv:2611.25005`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yao Zhang, Zhuchenyang Liu, Yu Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on human-scene interaction (HSI) generation moves from single interactions to long-horizon interaction, where a language-model agent plans and executes a sequence of interactions in a 3D scene under one instruction. However, existing agents struggle in challenging everyday scenarios (e.g., picking up a toy and throwing it into a basket) because they lack spatial control over the motion and quantitative feedback on its execution, and they often fail to complete the interaction sequence. To address these gaps, we present Inhabitant, a training-free agent framework in which the agent specifies each motion through positions, paths and poses, measures how the executed interaction deviates from its intent, and revises the control specification accordingly. For an extensive evaluation in such scenarios, we build Inhabit, a benchmark of 75 household tasks in 31 furniture layouts of an apartment, in which every task chains one to three goals on movable objects and is carried out by the whole body, excluding fingers. On Inhabit, Inhabitant completes 98.7\% of the tasks with Claude Opus 5.5, and 93.3\% and 82.7\% with Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Gemini 4 Argon, respectively. Ablations confirm that without spatial control the agent fails most tasks, and without quantitative feedback its motion becomes less precise. Code is available on the project page.
  </details>

- **[AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26613)**  `arXiv:2611.26613`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yiyang Du, Yuxuan Kuang, Qitao Zhao, Yixuan Wang, Zhanqiu Guo, Yi Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.
  </details>

- **[FlowDrive: moderated flow matching with data balancing for trajectory planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21961)**  `arXiv:2509.21961`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lingguang Wang, Ömer Şahin Taş, Marlon Steiner, Nick Le Large, Christoph Stiller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning-based planners are sensitive to the long-tailed distribution of driving data. Common maneuvers dominate datasets, while dangerous or rare scenarios are sparse. This imbalance can bias models toward the frequent cases and degrade performance on critical scenarios. To tackle this problem, we compare balancing strategies for sampling training data and find reweighting by trajectory pattern an effective approach. We then present FlowDrive, a flow-matching trajectory planner that learns a conditional rectified flow to map noise directly to trajectory distributions with few flow-matching steps. We further introduce moderated, in-the-loop guidance that injects small perturbation between flow steps to systematically increase trajectory diversity while remaining scene-consistent. On nuPlan and the interaction-focused interPlan benchmarks, FlowDrive achieves state-of-the-art results among learning-based planners and approaches methods with rule-based refinements. After adding moderated guidance and light post-processing (FlowDrive*), it achieves overall state-of-the-art performance across nearly all benchmark splits. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[On Agentic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892)**  `arXiv:2611.09892`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. Seethis https URLfor code and videos.
  </details>

- **[Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26444)**  `arXiv:2611.26444`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuzhi Huang, Jie Wu, Fanding Huang, Junchao Lin, Weichen Zhang, Ziyi Xiong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.
  </details>

- **[Fast-JEPA: Advancing the Success-Latency Frontier for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25927)**  `arXiv:2611.25927`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Zhang, Bin Yu, Shijie Lian, Haishan Liu, Changti Wu, Yu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models predict the outcomes of action sequences, but comparing candidate plans through recursive rollout remains expensive at test time. We introduce Fast-JEPA, a latent world model planning framework that advances the success-latency Pareto frontier of predictive search. Fast-JEPA reorganizes predictive computation to support effective action-sequence search at low planning cost. It constructs a goal-directed plan to guide search and directly predicts the outcomes of complete candidates for efficient comparison, avoiding recursive state rollout during refinement. Across PushT, Reacher, Cube, and TwoRoom, Fast-JEPA achieves 96.50% average success rate with 2.41x faster planning than INTACT. Search-budget sweeps show that its competitive success and low latency extend across candidate counts and refinement depths. We further introduce DynaGoal, a benchmark for evaluating world-model planning under dynamic out-of-distribution conditions, execution deadlines, and controlled response delays. Fast-JEPA achieves average success rates of 73.83% on DynaGoal-Shift and 87.67% on DynaGoal-Deadline, demonstrating the practical value of low-latency predictive planning under changing control conditions and execution-time constraints. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TipDex-VTLA: Learning When to Refine Actions with Touch for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25683)**  `arXiv:2611.25683`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xudi Ge, Yepeng Liu, Haifeng Liu, Enze Shi, Liangqi Zhang, Yongchao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Changes in illumination can induce action drift in dexterous manipulation even when the physical state remains unchanged. Although existing vision--tactile--language--action (VTLA) policies incorporate tactile feedback, when and how touch refines actions under changing visual conditions remains challenging. We introduce \textbf{TipDex-VTLA}, a VTLA policy that combines estimated visual action reliability and tactile forecast verification to regulate execution-time refinement. Specifically, Visual Action Sensitivity Estimation (VASE) learns visual reliability from a frozen reference policy's action sensitivity to illumination perturbations. Tactile Anticipation and Correction during Execution (TACE) compares predicted and measured touch at matched times and combines the resulting discrepancy with visual reliability to gate bounded feature corrections for unexecuted actions. This effectively helps to refine actions under challenging unseen visual conditions. We evaluate TipDex-VTLA on four real-world dexterous manipulation tasks and the UniVTAC simulation benchmark. TipDex-VTLA trained on data collected under normal visual conditions, achieves average full-task success rates of 87.5% (resp. 73.8%) under normal (resp. challenging unseen) lighting in real-world tasks, significantly outperforming some state-of-the-art methods. We will release the collected data and source code.
  </details>

- **[VG-Steer: Visual-Geometric Policy Steering from In-Context Aligned Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25199)**  `arXiv:2611.25199`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Arthur Bucker, Pramod Anantharam, Jonathan Francis, Jean Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting robot policies to out-of-distribution (OOD) settings with limited real-world data remains a key bottleneck to generalist robots. The multimodality and scarcity of robotics data lead end-to-end approaches to overfit and break under small distribution shifts. Test-time adaptation can alleviate this by correcting frozen policies at inference, but most methods ground their correction in a signal learned implicitly from robotic datasets, making it susceptible to the same shifts as the policy it corrects. Recent methods that instead exploit explicit spatial information are typically limited to coarse corrections, missing the nuanced, task-relevant motion most manipulation tasks require. We propose VG-Steer, which corrects existing policies by transferring the nuanced motion of successful in-distribution rollouts, captured along meaningful geometric axes, known here as object-relational distance pairs. VG-Steer leverages the semantic understanding of Large Vision Models (LVMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reasoning, which combines the task-relevant entity pairs and their object-relational distance projections into a single tailored visual-geometric guidance signal. In a simulation benchmark, it efficiently corrects flow-matching policies under pose and task-goal shifts; on a real robot experiment, it widens the region of the workspace the frozen policy covers, more than doubling its success rate on cable placements one grid step outside the training positions, and steers a single-task policy toward objects it was never trained on while nearly eliminating object confusion. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PhyDEX: A Benchmark for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language-Action Models via Dynamic Extrinsic Dexterity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26565)**  `arXiv:2611.26565`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hao Vo, Taisei Hanyu, Yuki Ikebe, Pham Tri Quang, Sreevenkata Anjani Tishita Godavarthi, Nghi D. Q. Bui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have advanced rapidly in language-conditioned robotic manipulation, yet existing benchmarks mainly evaluate gripper--object contact and largely ignore how objects interact with the environment through gravity, friction, inertia, and mass distribution. As a result, it remains unclear whether current policies truly understand physics or merely interpolate from their training data. We address this gap through the lens of dynamic extrinsic dexterity and introduce PhyDEX, a throwing benchmark in which a robot must infer an object's mass, center of mass, and friction to send it to a destination beyond its kinematic workspace. PhyDEX contains 35K trajectories (143 hours) across nine tasks and 100 scenes, covering uniform, irregular, and dynamic-mass objects with static, multiple, and moving destinations. All data are generated by a scalable, fully automatic pipeline that combines grasp generation, motion planning, and closed-loop throw optimization under randomized physical properties. We evaluate three representative VLAs, GR00T N1.7, DynamicVLA, and ForceVLA, and find that all achieve success rates below 3\%. Our analysis reveals two key bottlenecks: limited robustness of pretrained models, which causes most failures before the throw, and physical blindness, as providing ground-truth physical properties roughly doubles success. Experiments on a humanoid robot and in the real world show the same failure patterns. We hope PhyDEX will drive the development of physically grounded robot policies.
  </details>

- **[ACE-Brain-0.7: Learning Execution-Grounded Reasoning for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26519)**  `arXiv:2611.26519`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yinhe Wang, Shijun Shi, Feng Pan, Tao Tao, Wei Mao, Jiangning Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable reasoning for long-horizon embodied tasks requires predictions grounded in the observed execution state. We present ACE-Brain-0.7, a 9B vision--language model that combines spatial grounding, task understanding, and progress estimation. To train the model, we curate 3.7 million question--answer pairs, drawing execution supervision from egocentric human videos, robot episodes, and simulation. These questions span different stages of an execution and connect task-level judgments with observed progress and outcomes. We then fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B on this collection together with additional task, spatial-grounding, and progress-estimation data. Across six public task-reasoning benchmarks, ACE-Brain-0.7 achieves an average score of 76.5, ahead of GPT-6 Astra at 72.7 and Claude Opus 5.5 at 73.5. Our model also leads the evaluated models in spatial grounding, averaging 75.90 across seven benchmarks and reaching 91.25 Acc@0.5 on RefCOCOg. Controlled evaluations further indicate that our task-only model makes more effective use of visual history and more accurately distinguishes execution states. Together, these results show that ACE-Brain-0.7 effectively combines spatial perception with temporal understanding, delivering strong execution-grounded reasoning and comparing favorably with closed-source models.
  </details>

- **[Alignment Drives Reuse: Learning Generalizable Robot-Use Agents from Heterogeneous Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26376)**  `arXiv:2611.26376`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ziyue Wang, Yaopeng Yang, Liyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) demonstrate broad generalization across tasks and domains. However, comparable generalization in robot manipulation remains challenging. Heterogeneity in robot embodiments, task formulations, geometric conventions, and action interfaces fragments interaction experience across benchmarks and hardware. We rethink robot skill learning through alignment: Can heterogeneous interactions across benchmarks and embodiments jointly improve one shared skillset for robot manipulation? We propose UniCraft, a framework for learning robot manipulation skills by aligning heterogeneous settings. Specifically, UniCraft represents observed scene geometry using consistent coordinate conventions and aligns sensing and control interfaces across robots. It organizes tasks from different benchmarks in a canonical format, allowing interaction feedback to jointly refine shared skills and the orchestration policy that guides their selection and composition, without any demonstrations or model weight updates. We interleave tasks from 3 benchmarks and 6 embodiments, collecting 2,700 learning rollouts per agent model. During training, UniCraft improves pooled task success on unseen scenarios and tasks by 9-28 percentage points on LIBERO-PRO, 10.8-13.3 on RoboTwin 2.0, and 3.1-14.4 on RoboCasa365 (Epochs 1-3). After training, with the learned skills frozen, UniCraft demonstrates zero-shot transfer across 4 new robot embodiments and 5 additional agent models on robosuite and RoboDojo.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning on Call: Efficient Agentic Robot Control for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26341)**  `arXiv:2611.26341`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zilang Cen, Chao Tang, Fangcheng Liu, Weishi Mi, Wei Hao, JiaYu Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models can support long-horizon robot manipulation, but frequent model calls incur substantial latency and token costs. The central challenge is determining when agent assistance or intervention is needed and whether errors require correcting robot execution, subtask planning, or both. To address this problem, we propose RoC (Reasoning on Call), an efficient framework that coordinates a low-level policy and a high-level reasoning agent through an online task-state monitor. The monitor combines temporal visual observations with ordered interaction memory to jointly estimate progress within the active subtask and predict its successor. These predictions guide event-triggered agent review for coordinated execution repair and plan revision. Verified outcomes govern memory updates and subtask transitions, allowing routine execution to remain with the policy while reasoning focuses on decisions requiring additional assessment. On RMBench, RoC improves task success rate by 24.0 percentage points and reduces token consumption by 65.3% compared with the strongest agent-based baseline. It also outperforms the strongest policy-based baseline by 15.1 percentage points in task success rate. Real-world experiments further validate its effectiveness across three long-horizon manipulation tasks. Overall, RoC supports efficient agent intervention and coordinated recovery across execution and planning for long-horizon robot manipulation.
  </details>

- **[LocoMuJoCo: A Comprehensive Imitation Learning Benchmark for Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26293)**  `arXiv:2611.26293`  `cs.RO`  
  _Firas Al-Hafez, Davide Tateo, Oleg Kaidanov, Aditya Bhatt, Artur Zelik, Zhengyi Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LocoMuJoCo is an open-source benchmark for imitation learning (IL) and reinforcement learning (RL) on whole-body control. The library couples MuJoCo (CPU) with MJX and the MjWarp backend (GPU) under a single API, exposes 9 humanoid, 3 (musculo-)skeletal, and 4 quadruped embodiments, and ships a curated native motion-capture dataset for every humanoid, to which it can also retarget AMASS and LAFAN1. A modular plug-in architecture makes observations, rewards, control modes, terrains, and domain randomization fully interchangeable, while compact JAX implementations of many RL and IL algorithms provide JIT-compiled end-to-end training, and the companion loco-mujoco-real repository provides sim-to-real deployment. We describe the design, the SMPL-to-robot and robot-to-robot retargeting pipeline, and a usage example. LocoMuJoCo is released under MIT atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[One View, One Action: Spatially Generalizable VLAs through Observation-Aligned Experts and Cross Graph Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26161)**  `arXiv:2611.26161`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jinxi Liu, Ruifeng Zhai, Hao Liu, Guangrun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-view vision-language-action (VLA) models still face challenges in spatial generalization, particularly under changes in camera viewpoints and robot initial states. These models typically receive action supervision only in the robot base frame, requiring them to implicitly learn the spatial correspondence between observations from each view and base-frame actions. We propose One View, One Action (OVOA-VLA), which learns to predict actions in each view's camera frame, allowing the same physical motion to provide diverse, observation-aligned supervision across views. OVOA-VLA jointly trains a base-frame expert and view-specific action experts, whose robot-state inputs and action targets are expressed in their respective camera frames. Two complementary directed attention graphs model both directions of interaction between the base and view-specific experts, jointly leveraging view-specific action supervision to improve base-frame prediction. Cross Graph Consistency further encourages agreement between the two graphs' base-frame predictions for the same sample. With the same multi-view inputs, the model supports both full and base-only inference. Experiments across multiple simulation benchmarks demonstrate improved manipulation performance and robustness to camera-viewpoint and robot-initialization perturbations. Full and base-only inference achieve average success rates of 88.1% and 85.7% on LIBERO-Plus, respectively. In real-world evaluations, base-only inference raises the average success rates from $\pi_{0.5}$'s 28.3% to 58.3% across three tasks under spatial distribution shifts.
  </details>

- **[Cognitive-Horizon Action Chunking: Memory-Guided Adaptive Reasoning for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25587)**  `arXiv:2611.25587`  `cs.RO`  
  _Weile Gong, Zijian Lu, Jiahao Zheng, Bo Xu, Yiping Zuo, Jiajia Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal foundation models are advancing embodied navigation, but frequent inference incurs substantial computational cost. We present Cognitive-Horizon Action Chunking (CHAC), a training-free framework for memory-guided adaptive reasoning with a frozen navigation policy. Retained visual context, completion cues, and controller feedback determine the cognitive horizon, or how many cached waypoints the robot executes before refreshing its prediction. At the policy's stop request, local visitation history guides a bounded correction with at most one extra movement and no additional model calls. CHAC reduces vision-language model calls by 33.4% on HM3D-OVON and 64.7% on R2R-CE, with success-rate decreases of 0.63 and 10.82 percentage points, respectively. Success rate and success weighted by path length improve per model call on both benchmarks. Across four physical navigation tasks with 40 runs per method, CHAC achieves 18 successes versus 14 for native execution, reducing model calls by 54.9% and GPU inference time by 49.7%.
  </details>

- **[Old-School Planners Drive (Sometimes) Better Than Learned Ones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25138)**  `arXiv:2611.25138`  `cs.RO`  
  _Andreas Look_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end driving models learn to plan from data, and hand-written planners are widely treated as a thing of the past. We test whether this is justified: we implement sixteen textbook planners, each combining a path source (reference line, A*, hybrid A*, RRT*) with a trajectory method (Frenet lattice, nonlinear optimization, $s$--$t$ search, dynamic window), and evaluate them on three benchmarks: open loop on nuScenes, closed loop on nuPlan with ground-truth perception, and photorealistic closed loop on HUGSIM. On HUGSIM, they replace only the planner of the end-to-end model UniAD and read its frozen perception, without an HD map or other privileged information. On nuScenes and nuPlan, the best textbook planners are competitive (L2 error of 0.735\,m against 0.947\,m for UniAD's planner; a nuPlan score of 79.3 against 76.1 for the rule-based PDM-Closed on Test14-hard with reactive agents). On HUGSIM, nine of the sixteen score above UniAD's learned planner on the same perception,. The best, a hybrid A* path followed by nonlinear trajectory optimization, raises HUGSIM's driving score (HD-Score) from 32.0 to 41.8. The gain is largest with standing and oncoming cars; against cars that steer at the ego, there is none. In 31 of the 182 runs in which UniAD's planner hits a car, the best textbook planner completes the route on the same perception: these crashes are due to the planner, not the perception.
  </details>

- **[CoT-WAM: Collaborative Training of Vision-Language Models and World-Action Models via Plan Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23803)**  `arXiv:2611.23803`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jinhao Zhang, Yushen Zuo, Ke Rui, Weitao Zhou, Haibao Yu, Honglong Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General robot manipulation requires both semantic competence and physical foresight. World action models (WAMs) acquire an understanding of and predictive capability over the physical world by jointly modeling visual foresight and action distributions. However, existing WAMs still lack semantic competence: they have limited ability to perform open-world scene understanding, semantic reasoning, and long-horizon planning. Although vision-language models (VLMs) provide these capabilities, effectively integrating their semantic priors into WAM prediction and control remains challenging. To address this challenge, we propose CoT-WAM, a jointly trained WAM-VLM framework. CoT-WAM uses a learnable embedding to exchange information between the VLM and the WAM, which is supervised by textual and goal-image signals during training to encode rich intent information. A tailored two-stage training strategy enables joint optimization and more effective collaboration between the VLM and WAM. We evaluate CoT-WAM on six benchmarks covering control performance, zero-shot generalization, and long-horizon planning. CoT-WAM achieves SOTA performance on LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboCasa-Kitchen, and demonstrates stronger zero-shot generalization on LIBERO-Plus and LIBERO-Pro without fine-tuning. On RoboCasa365, it further improves long-horizon planning over baselines with and without external planners. Real-world experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in practical deployments. Code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Rollout then Recycle: 3,718 Labelled Real-Robot Episodes from Benchmarking 7 Policies on 12 Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03168)**  `arXiv:2611.03168`  `cs.RO`  
  _Praveen Selvaraj, Lorenzo Uttini, Ville Kuosmanen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We release all 3,718 episodes from the ArmnetBench v0.1 evaluation (arXiv:2607.24481), which contains 600 teleoperated demonstrations and 3,118 rollouts of seven imitation-learning and vision-language-action policies on 12 tabletop tasks, recorded on low-cost SO-101 arms; 73% of the rollouts are failures. Every policy for a task was trained or fine-tuned on the same 50 demonstrations and rolled out on the same cell, so the corpus contains many policies' failures on shared tasks. An operator labelled every rollout successful, suboptimal or failure. Each episode has three camera views, joint states and actions and is available on the Hugging Face Hub in LeRobot v3.0 and a reward-model format. This note documents how the episodes were collected and the limitations that could affect their use.
  </details>

- **[SuperMimic: Scaling Humanoid Whole-Body Control with One Differentiable World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28480)**  `arXiv:2610.28480`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qiayuan Liao, Yiyang Shao, Junfeng Long, Kevin Zakka, Roberto Horowitz, Pieter Abbeel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking large human-motion datasets has become the dominant route to general, expressive humanoid whole-body control, but scaling across many motions is expensive. Current pipelines use model-free reinforcement learning, either merging per-motion specialists through lossy distillation, or brute-forcing a single policy with cluster-scale compute. SuperMimic amortizes learning across all motions with one differentiable world model of their shared dynamics, fit from logged transitions of a standard rigid-body simulator. The world model and the tracking policy train together in one loop, and backpropagation-through-time over short imagined rollouts gives the policy analytic gradients. Fast imagined rollouts and analytic gradients make policy learning efficient. In 3 hours on a single GPU, SuperMimic surpasses the return of a state-of-the-art motion tracker baseline trained for 7 days on 128 GPUs. It reduces GPU-hours by more than $1000\times$ and fails on only 25 of 129,514 benchmark motions, versus 179 failures for the baseline. After 9.8 hours on two GPUs, SuperMimic achieves zero failures. From 1 to 264 hours of motion, the world model's prediction error stays stable, and GPU-hours to a target return grow slowly. Our policy transfers to a physical Unitree G1 across diverse and acrobatic motions. SuperMimic removes compute as the bottleneck to scaling humanoid whole-body control, making it practical to measure motion-tracking scaling laws.
  </details>

- **[Evo-DexBench: Benchmarking Generalization across Related Tasks for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21180)**  `arXiv:2610.21180`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bing Cheng, Yiran Mao, Zewei Ye, Yifan Jin, Xinyi Chen, Ruifeng Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose dexterous manipulation requires policies to transfer knowledge from related tasks to new tasks with only a few target demonstrations. Existing benchmarks often provide too many target demonstrations and leave the differences between source and target tasks uncontrolled. As a result, they cannot clearly distinguish knowledge transfer from task memorization. Our key insight is the Core-Gen principle. Core tasks are related tasks that share a functional interaction and provide source knowledge, while a Gen task is a novel target within the same functional family that has only a few demonstrations. This setup tests whether a policy can reuse functional interaction knowledge. Based on this principle, we propose Evo-DexBench, which controls both source-task relatedness and target-task exposure. It comprises 30 tasks in six functional families, three everyday layouts per task, and 1,248 multimodal demonstrations collected via VR teleoperation with simulated Shadow Hands equipped with 123 taxels. We first compare few-shot adaptation strategies and find that joint Core + Gen training makes the most effective use of Core knowledge. Under this setting, we evaluate six representative policies. The best-performing policy, $\pi_{0.5}$, achieves a mean success rate of only 24.4%, revealing the limitations of existing methods in transferring functional interaction knowledge. Further analysis shows that on the tested Gen targets, functionally matched Core data yields larger success-rate gains than mismatched data, and that with matched Core data, 8 Gen demonstrations reach the performance of training on 16--32 Gen demonstrations alone. These results indicate that Evo-DexBench separates knowledge transfer from task memorization under controlled conditions and effectively evaluates the generalization of dexterous manipulation policies across related tasks.
  </details>

- **[Video2SwimFish: An Automated Pipeline for Reconstructing Controllable Fish Models and Biological Locomotion from Real Fish Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38966)**  `arXiv:2609.38966`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hangong Chen, Linfeng Cheng, Tahsin Zaman Jilan, Jiaye Wu, Lee Caesar, Ian Fuller Yantian Zha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Video2SwimFish, an automated pipeline and benchmark for building controllable fish assets from real-fish videos for underwater embodied AI. Given synchronized multi-view videos of an individual fish, the pipeline generates a metrically scaled deformable mesh from a VLM-selected canonical frame, builds internal articulation adapted to that individual's morphology through a VLM actor-critic loop, and extracts a Biological Locomotion Manifold (BLM). The BLM provides a low-dimensional action space bounded by real-fish motion. Compared with learning directly in joint space, it lets the individual swimming policy of each reconstructed fish converge faster and reach a higher task success rate. We release two paired datasets: synchronized top- and front-view recordings of 120 individual fish across 6 species, and the controllable assets and individual swimming policies derived from them. Because every asset is tied to the animal it came from, the dataset supports a benchmark that evaluates locomotion learning not only on task success but on fidelity to that individual's locomotion behavior. We evaluate on two tasks, 3D trajectory following and target reaching, and a case study in which a simulated BlueROV underwater robot captures the assets. We further validate biological realism with a human study in which fish experts rate the simulated fish, as a complement to the automated metrics. We find that task success and locomotion fidelity do not necessarily improve together, and we identify faithful reproduction of individual animal locomotion as an open challenge for the community. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[A Multimodal Label Forecasting Method for Aperiodic Visuo-Motor Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07930)**  `arXiv:2609.07930`  `cs.RO`  
  _Borui He, Garrett E Katz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models have been increasingly applied to Time Series Forecasting (TSF) in recent years. Transformer-based and MLP-based models have both been used effectively on many real-world TSF regression benchmarks, and there is ongoing debate as to which family of methods is best. While these benchmarks have drawn much attention, it is also worth noting that many current datasets and methods assume approximate periodicity in the time series. In this work, we focus on a new TSF task without periodicity: anticipating falls during humanoid locomotion, on the basis of egocentric vision and proprioception. When the locomotion trajectories are sufficiently diverse, periodicity is violated. We contribute two new benchmark datasets (one from simulation, one from real hardware), showing that periodicity is violated and recent deep TSF methods struggle on these benchmarks. We also propose a novel deep learning architecture that exploits both endogenous and exogenous variables and a training process that rigorously enforces i.i.d sampling of training examples. Our results show statistically significant improvement over prior art in multiple experimental conditions, by 12.73% or more on the real data and 10.40% or more on the simulation data. Code and datasets will be available upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[A Physics-Consistent Benchmark for Contact-Rich Human-Robot Interaction in Assistive Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02402)**  `arXiv:2609.02402`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chengxiao He, Shenghai Yuan, Liuqun Fan, Shenzhe Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional task-level evaluation asks whether a robot policy completes a specified action, but can miss failures that emerge only during physical human contact. This limitation is critical in contact-rich assistive tasks, where meaningful evaluation requires a physically responsive human, interaction-quality assessment beyond task success, and a leak-free observer-scorer protocol. We introduce a physics-consistent benchmark for contact-rich human-robot interaction, instantiated in robot-assisted bathing. The benchmark combines a deformable, passively responding human, physics-aware scores alongside task-level success, and a frozen vision-only / scorer-only evaluation protocol. To establish physical validity, region-wise simulated responses are calibrated against force-indentation measurements from Franka impedance pushes on a medical-care manikin. Under a frozen T1-T7 protocol with 140 runs per method, an LLM-augmented state machine (State Machine) achieves 72.9% task success but drops to 56.4% after correct-region and force-safety screening; VoxPoser produces lighter and more stable contact but completes only 27.9% of trials; and zero-shot pi0.5 achieves 0.7% task success with no correct-region or safety-gated successes. These results show that task completion alone does not imply physically valid contact and motivate physics-aware screening before deployment of contact-rich assistive robot policies.
  </details>

- **[Flatness Preserves Instruction Following in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23641)**  `arXiv:2606.23641`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haochen Zhang, Yonatan Bisk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models have the potential for open-world generalization by leveraging pretrained vision-language representations, yet downstream finetuning on limited robot data often degrades these representations. This leads to brittle action policies that ignore language instructions in favor of visual shortcuts, despite the base VLM having this knowledge, which is a failure mode we term instruction blindness. We hypothesize that standard finetuning with limited data applies gradients to a sparse set of points, which manifests as a sharp loss landscape with high-curvature minima. We analyze this effect, then show that regularization through explicit flatness-preserving optimization is a simple yet effective technique while finetuning on the exact same data. Specifically, we demonstrate that applying sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) during VLA finetuning significantly improves instruction following by over 55% across multiple simulation and real-world benchmarks without additional data, architectural modification, or pretraining. Explicitly preserving flatness better preserves pretrained knowledge compared to other regularization techniques that simply anchor model weights or constrain learning. We further analyze the effect of selective sharpness, quantify its effects, and show that such approaches are complementary to existing guidance techniques.
  </details>

- **[MyoChallenge 2025: A New Benchmark for Human Athletic Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15650)**  `arXiv:2605.15650`  `cs.RO`  
  _Cheryl Wang, Chun Kwang Tan, Balint K. Hodossy, Eric Lyu, Jun Guo, Wentao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Athletic performance represents the pinnacle of human motor intelligence, demanding rapid choices, precise control, agility, and coordinated physical execution. Replicating this seamless combination of capabilities remains elusive in current artificial intelligence and robotic systems. Concurrently, understanding the biological mastery of these movements is hindered because complex muscle coordination is rarely measured in vivo due to the limitations of physical equipment. To bridge this fundamental gap in understanding, MyoChallenge at NeurIPS 2025 established a pioneering benchmark for motor control intelligence in sports, leveraging high-fidelity musculoskeletal models within physics simulation combined with machine learning-driven algorithms. The competition introduces two distinct tracks emphasizing either upper or lower limbs control: a table tennis rally task utilizing a biomechanic upper limb composed of an arm with a hand and a trunk; and a soccer penalty kick using a biomechanic model of legs and a trunk. Marking the fourth iteration of the MyoChallenge series, this event attracted almost 70 teams and over 560 submissions globally, uniting a diverse community ranging from physicians and neuroscientists to machine learning experts. The competition facilitated the development of several state-of-the-art control algorithms for a musculoskeletal system capable of sports agility, leveraging techniques such as physics-based motion planners, on-policy behaviour cloning, hierarchical planning, and muscle synergies. By integrating standardized tasks and physiologically realistic models into the open-source framework of MyoSuite, MyoChallenge'25 serves as a reproducible and reusable testbed to accelerate interdisciplinary research across machine learning, biomechanics, sports science, and neuroscience. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SixthSense: Proprioception-Only Whole-Body Wrench Estimation for Humanoids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01427)**  `arXiv:2605.01427`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xingzhou Chen, Xiayan Xu, Yan Ning, Jiyu Yu, Yizheng Zhang, Siyi Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid robots are entering the physical world at scale, yet are often criticized as oversized toys---good at dancing, but lacking the force-interaction capabilities for practical tasks. Reliable whole-body contact perception is difficult because floating-base dynamics make proprioceptive wrench recovery ambiguous, while model-based estimators require accurate dynamics and support-force inputs. We propose SixthSense, which uses conditional flow matching to model sparse spatiotemporal fields of link-wise contact gates and 6-D wrenches conditioned on proprioceptive and IMU histories. We train one estimator on pooled data from lying, standing, walking, and motion tracking. Across extensive simulation experiments, SixthSense outperforms learned and model-based baselines in overall contact estimation and remains robust to observation noise. We further establish a real-robot benchmark with our newly collected Unitree G1 dataset, where SixthSense achieves the strongest overall performance among learned estimators and requires approximately 50\,ms per inference. Controlled ambiguity experiments show that its samples recover multiple dynamically plausible contact modes with improved local physical consistency. We finally deploy SixthSense for collision detection and contact-triggered physical human--robot interaction.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Centric Observation Adaptation for Robust Visual Control under Dynamic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24661)**  `arXiv:2604.24661`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhengru Fang, Yu Guo, Huanhuan Lou, Fei Liu, Yuang Zhang, Yihang Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual control maps visual streams to actions in reinforcement learning and vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Changing weather, noise, blur, and compression can obscure different task cues within one episode. Existing control benchmarks mainly test scene distractions, while generic restorers optimize whole-image fidelity. We introduce the Visual Degraded Control Suite (VDCS) to evaluate seven degradations under Markov switching. We propose Agent-Centric Observations with Mixture-of-Experts (ACO-MoE), which routes each frame to a repair expert, restores RGB content, and composes task-relevant foregrounds. On eight VDCS tasks, corruption lowers DreamerV3's mean return from 769 to 562. ACO-MoE raises it to 730, recovering 81.2% of the lost return. It also improves returns on DMC-GB and RoboSuite and retains 70% to 97% of clean-input mean return under six unseen perturbation families. We train ACO-MoE on robot demonstrations and deploy it with a 3B-parameter VLA policy on a Piper arm for cube placement in blue and white boxes and cup placement on a coaster. On the blue-box and cup tasks, motion blur and physical low light with added noise reduce success from 100% to at most 10%. ACO-MoE raises success to 75% to 95% over 20 trials per task and condition.
  </details>

- **[GridAnchor: Specialized Multi-Agent AI for Safeguarding Autonomous Energy Systems Against Cyber-Physical Hallucinations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25158)**  `arXiv:2611.25158`  `cs.MA`  
  _Abderaouf Bahi, Hasan Masrur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous energy systems increasingly chain specialized learned agents, such as a forecaster, a state estimator and a dispatcher that may be a language model. These agents can emit confident outputs that contradict physics, and downstream agents may act on them. We define such cyber-physical hallucinations and their cascades, organize them in eight types, and build CPH-Bench, a generator of labeled episodes on SimBench feeders. We propose GridAnchor, a verification layer whose Physics-Residual Graph Transformer scores every claim from residuals between the claims, the measurements and a linearized grid model. Mondrian conformal p-values with the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure turn the scores into decisions, a root head attributes cascades, and a quadratic program repairs unsafe actions with a voltage margin calibrated at repaired steps. On a simulated rural medium-voltage feeder, GridAnchor reaches a message-level area under the precision-recall curve of 0.846, against 0.837 for the strongest of six baselines, a difference within the confidence interval. It leads on estimates and actions, whereas a self-consistency check that knows the label tolerances reaches 0.874 by leading on forecasts. The false-alarm rate stays within 0.17 percentage points of the nominal level on steps without injected hallucinations. In closed loop, GridAnchor leaves no bus outside the voltage band on the test days, against 0.0700% of the bus-steps without protection, and simpler detectors with the same mitigation come close. Stale but coherent messages and single-bus forecast errors remain mostly undetected.
  </details>

- **[Anchor and Perturb: Lazy Agent Remediation by Exploration Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27365)**  `arXiv:2609.27365`  `cs.MA`  
  _Chengxi Zhong, Yongzhe Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anchor and Perturb (AnP) is a lightweight framework that resolves multi-agent coordination failures by decoupling exploratory variance injection from recurrent manifold stability. Existing remediation strategies predominantly alter mixing network architectures or enforce simultaneous exploration across the collective, which inevitably precipitates severe temporal-difference penalties in non-monotonic reward spaces. Specifically, AnP isolates underperforming lazy agents and injects an asymmetric exploratory pulse into targeted coordinates whilst anchoring converged teammates to nominal greedy exploitation. Empirical telemetry benchmarks demonstrate that AnP successfully rescues collapsed joint policies (recovering from a 5% evaluation win rate nadir back to 85%) and facilitates escape from suboptimal coordination plateaus, sustaining peak win rates of 90% without requiring structural network modifications.
  </details>

- **[SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26296)**  `arXiv:2611.26296`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Maximilian Seeth, Chengzhi Martin Hu, Marie Kempkes, Paul Röttger, Leo Schwinn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducingthis http URL: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. We show thatthis http URLsignificantly outperforms state-of-the-art textual skills optimized using frontier LLMs in 9 of 16 settings (across 4 VLMs and 4 benchmarks) while requiring only 64 to 256 image tokens instead of roughly 900 to 2000 text tokens at inference time. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble.this http URLthus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Invertible Transport Between Sample-Only Distributions via Projected Characteristic Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26187)**  `arXiv:2611.26187`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Juan Gutiérrez, Victor Gutiérrez-García, José Luis Blanco-Murillo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many useful latent distributions can be sampled but do not have a tractable density. Such distributions may be represented by empirical collections of real-world observations or generated by scientific simulators and black-box generative models. These sample-only priors cannot be used directly in likelihood-based normalizing flows, which require evaluating the prior density. We propose Projected Characteristic-Function matching (PCF), a density-free framework for learning an invertible map between a data distribution and a prior specified only through samples.Our method aligns distributions using projected empirical characteristic functions, eliminating the need for density estimation, adversarial training, or paired observations. To address the non-uniqueness of the transport map, we introduce an optional transport regularizer that selects lower-displacement transformations while preserving endpoint alignment.We benchmark PCF on controlled transports, sample-efficiency studies, and high-dimensional alignment tasks. PCF matches the accuracy of kernel MMD on the controlled benchmark and on raw-pixel MNIST--USPS at a cost linear in the batch size, and it is more sample-efficient, achieving lower endpoint error than MMD in 13 of 15 paired runs across library sizes. By avoiding pairwise kernel evaluation, it reduces discrepancy memory by 29.8% and trains at batch sizes where kernel MMD exceeds GPU memory. These results show that PCF provides accurate and efficient invertible transport, particularly in sample- and memory-limited settings.
  </details>

- **[Long-Range Video Presentation Attack Detection: A Three-Class Benchmark Across 1-100 m and Two Sensor Types](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25108)**  `arXiv:2611.25108`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Lambert Igene, Mohammad Zahir Uddin Chowdhury, Afzal Hossain, Sandip Purnapatra, Peter Johnson, Stephanie Schuckers_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Face presentation attack detection (PAD) systems deployed in border control and airport surveillance are constrained by a critical assumption: subjects are captured at close range, typically under one meter. No existing benchmark systematically addresses multi-distance capture, diverse environmental conditions, and concurrent spoofing and occlusion-based attacks, leaving deployed models underprepared for real-world scenarios. We introduce a video-based PAD dataset of 374,000+ frames from 261 unique identities collected across five standoff distances (1 m-100 m) under indoor and outdoor conditions using a DSLR camera (C) and a mobile sensor (M), with three-class labels: live, spoof, and props (occlusions); models are trained and evaluated on video clips rather than single frames. We fine-tune and benchmark four CNN architectures: MobileNetV3, MobileNet, EfficientNet, and ResNet-50. The higher-resolution DSLR maintains near-perfect spoof classification rate across all distances, while the lower-resolution mobile sensor degrades sharply beyond 50 m, with props classification rate dropping to 55% at 75 m for MobileNetV3. EfficientNet achieves the best overall performance (98.48% accuracy, ACER 2.01%). Generalization tests on unseen attack datasets yield 89-94% accuracy, validating cross-domain robustness. These results establish capture distance and sensor resolution as critical but underexplored axes of PAD difficulty. The dataset will be publicly released under an End-User License Agreement to support reproducible long-range PAD research.
  </details>

- **[FG-GraphBench: Benchmarking and Enhancing Fine-Grained Visual Understanding with Multimodal Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25408)**  `arXiv:2611.25408`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Sicheng Liu, Xunkai Li, Ziyu Han, Xingnan Zhang, Ru Zhang, Rong-Hua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained visual understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to recognize small visual entities and understand their relationships. Scene graphs offer a natural way to represent this information, organizing visual entities as nodes and their relationships as edges. However, we identify two challenges: existing scene graph benchmarks provide insufficient coverage of fine-grained entities and the relationships among them, and existing methods for fine-grained visual understanding have yet to effectively leverage such graph-structured information in MLLMs. To address these challenges, we introduce Fine-Grained Multimodal Scene Graph Benchmark (FG-GraphBench) and FiGRA. FG-GraphBench is a benchmark comprising three complementary tasks: fine-grained visual question answering (FG-QA), image captioning (FG-Caption), and image description generation (FG-Description). We develop a two-stage construction pipeline that discovers and grounds fine-grained entities and establishes their relations to construct the fine-grained multimodal scene graphs (MSGs) in FG-GraphBench. Building on these MSGs, we propose FiGRA, a Fine-grained Graph Representation Augmentation method for MLLMs. FiGRA adopts a two-stage training strategy. It encodes multimodal entity and relation features as continuous graph tokens, and integrates them into the MLLM. Experiments show that FiGRA significantly outperforms strong baselines across three tasks of FG-GraphBench. Further analyses confirm the effectiveness of FG-GraphBench and highlight the importance of preserving fine-grained entity and relation information for visual understanding.
  </details>

- **[Game-Theoretic Data Allocation for Expert Specialization and Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25167)**  `arXiv:2611.25167`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Mark Lovett, Denis Lim, Soroush Vosoughi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can the allocation used to train experts also predict which expert will perform best on a prompt? Such a connection would make the intended division of labor inspectable before training and allow the allocation itself to guide inference. We introduce InflEns, a mixture of LoRA experts whose training weights and inference routes come from the same location game in a fixed prompt-trait space. Players choose positions to maximize their expected shares of the prompt distribution, without using expert losses. We utilize the game's kernel-general theoretical criterion for when co-located positions become locally unstable to separation. We evaluate InflEns on a corpus assembled from seven safety-related benchmarks, which demonstrates that the highest-share expert has the lowest held-out response negative log-likelihood (NLL) on approximately 90% of prompts across four kernels under a sharpened simplex representation, compared with approximately 15% after permuting allocations across prompts. We demonstrate that the method performs comparably to published learned gates without having to train an explicit gate on the experts. Because the router is fixed, we can trace harmful behavior to a single expert and retrain only that expert, which lowers HarmBench attack success from 0.51 to 0.02 while keeping instruction following. The game also gives us several controls over how the experts specialize, including the kernel choice and its parameters, the trait-space geometry, and the number of experts. Importantly, InflEns is a general framework that can be extended to any encoder and data distribution.
  </details>

- **[PosteriorBench: Evaluating Behavior Posterior Sampling in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25735)**  `arXiv:2611.25735`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhiyang Xun, Daniel D. Johnson, Sarah Schwettmann, Neil Chowdhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare language model behaviors can be difficult to study because obtaining them through ordinary sampling can require a very large number of samples. This motivates the behavior posterior sampling problem: given a language model and a behavior of interest, is there an algorithm that efficiently samples from the model's distribution over transcripts that exhibit this behavior? We introduce PosteriorBench, a benchmark for comparing algorithms by their compute cost and by how close their samples are to the true posterior in forward and reverse KL divergence. PosteriorBench contains 21 tasks on Qwen3.5-0.8B with behavior probabilities from $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-1}$, each with exact posterior samples from a corpus of one billion labeled responses. We find that no method we evaluate is both efficient and faithful: simple methods like prompting are cheap but plateau at a high KL divergence from the posterior, fine-tuning on posterior samples is often cost-prohibitive, and several training methods from prior work do not perform better. Building a posterior sampler that can cheaply approximate the true posterior remains an open problem, and we hope PosteriorBench can measure progress towards this objective. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SynthDCB: World Distillation for Scalable Device Control Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18639)**  `arXiv:2611.18639`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both interactive worlds and the tasks agents must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation (WD). World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis. SynthDCB instantiates Home, Car, and Phone with 114 device classes, 1,166 functions, and 95 events, supporting five task families with 32 subtypes. Explicit states and tool-call traces support task validation and agent evaluation. We evaluate 22 models on 2,560 test tasks; the best achieves 88.8% mean Pass@1. Construction experiments demonstrate token efficiency and expansion of task coverage. At the richest knowledge level, WD fully covers 22/23 properties, 19/29 functionalities, and 30/39 event requirements specified in a real RobotVacuum protocol. Furthermore, the 1,000 sampled tasks semantically cover 81.6% of the 854 reference instructions in the real-world corpus. SynthDCB connects an expanding space of real-to-sim executable capabilities to verifiable agent evaluation.
  </details>

- **[AVSD: Adaptive-View Self-Distillation by Balancing Consensus and Teacher-Specific Privileged Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20643)**  `arXiv:2605.20643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Duy Nguyen, Hanqi Xiao, Archiki Prasad, Zaid Khan, Anirban Das, Shi-Xiong Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-distillation enables language models to learn on-policy from their own trajectories by using the same model as both student and teacher, with the teacher being conditioned on privileged information unavailable to the student. Such information can come in different types or views, such as solutions, demonstrations, feedback, or final answers. This setup provides dense token-level feedback without relying on a separate external model, but creates a fundamental asymmetry: the teacher may rely on view-specific information that the student cannot access at inference time. Moreover, the best type of privileged information is often task-dependent, making it difficult to choose a single teacher view. In this work, we address both these challenges jointly by introducing AVSD (Adaptive-View Self-Distillation), a novel method of self-distillation with multiple privileged-information views, which reconstructs token-level supervision by separating stable cross-view consensus from view-specific residual signals. AVSD identifies the consensus signal shared across views, which provides a reliable update direction, and then selectively adds the view-specific residual signal to adjust the update magnitude when it both aligns with the consensus direction and remains proportionate to the consensus signal. Experiments on math competition benchmarks (AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25) show that AVSD consistently outperforms both single-view self-distillation baselines and GRPO, achieving average Avg@8 gains of 3.1% and 2.2% over the strongest baselines on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B, respectively. Moreover, on code-generation benchmarks (Codeforces, LiveCodeBench v6) using Qwen3-8B, AVSD outperforms the single-view self-distillation baseline by 2.4% on average.
  </details>

- **[Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26598)**  `arXiv:2611.26598`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.
  </details>

- **[The Stability Gap in Continual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26252)**  `arXiv:2611.26252`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sebastian Pusch, Matthia Sabatelli, Gido M. van de Ven_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) aims to enable agents to adapt to changing tasks while preserving previously acquired capabilities. Evaluation often relies on final aggregate metrics that can hide temporary performance degradation during adaptation. This work introduces a simple CRL benchmark that varies observation representations while preserving environment dynamics and rewards, supporting proof-of-concept demonstrations, inexpensive experimentation, and easily reproducible sanity checks. Using this benchmark, we demonstrate the stability gap in CRL: a substantial drop in prior-task performance after the introduction of new tasks, followed by partial or complete recovery. This pattern occurs despite the agent having complete access to past tasks.
  </details>

- **[Channel-Dependent State Space Model for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36453)**  `arXiv:2609.36453`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yu-Cheng Wu, Fan-Keng Sun, Li-Chun Lu, Duane S. Boning_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) is critical across many real-world domains. Existing deep learning approaches fall into two paradigms with distinct limitations: channel-independent (CI) methods ignore cross-variable dependencies and model only temporal dynamics, while channel-dependent (CD) methods consider both but typically rely on architectural compromises to mitigate overfitting and computational overhead. We therefore propose Chameleon, a CD state space model (SSM) that enables data-dependent, fine-grained interactions across variables while scaling linearly with the number of variables. By connecting selective SSMs with the Kalman filter, we adapt the latter's measurement-update formulation for cross-variable modeling while preserving the SSM backbone for robust temporal modeling. We further identify favorable inductive biases of GatedDeltaNet for time series, adapt it as our backbone, and improve generalization through additional techniques, including a previously unexplored stochastic perturbation of reversible instance normalization. On strongly dependent ODE and PEMS datasets, Chameleon achieves the best MSE and MAE across all settings, while its CI ablation and prior CD methods incur 61-178% higher MSE on average. Across 28 standard benchmark settings, Chameleon also achieves better MSE and MAE than each baseline in at least 27 and 22 cases, respectively. Training-time, FLOP, and peak-memory analyses on Traffic and ETT further demonstrate competitive efficiency and favorable scalability across different variable counts.
  </details>

- **[EMA Policy Gradient: Taming Reinforcement Learning for LLMs with EMA Anchor and Top-k KL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04417)**  `arXiv:2602.04417`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lunjun Zhang, Jimmy Ba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) has enabled Large Language Models (LLMs) to acquire increasingly complex reasoning and agentic behaviors. In this work, we propose two simple techniques to improve policy gradient algorithms for LLMs. First, we replace the fixed anchor policy during RL with an Exponential Moving Average (EMA), similar to a target network in deep Q-learning. Second, we introduce Top-$k$ KL estimator, which allows for flexible interpolation between exact KL and sampled KL. We derive the stability conditions for using EMA anchor; moreover, we show that our Top-$k$ KL estimator yields both unbiased KL values and unbiased gradients at any $k$, while bringing the benefits of exact KL. When combined with GRPO, the two techniques (EMA-PG) lead to a significant performance boost. On math reasoning, it allows R1-distilled Qwen-1.5B to reach 53.9% on OlympiadBench compared to 50.8% by GRPO. On agentic RL domains, with Qwen-3B base, EMA-PG improves GRPO by an average of 35.6% across 7 datasets of Q&A with search engines, including 29.7% $\rightarrow$ 45.8% on HotpotQA, 27.4% $\rightarrow$ 40.5% on 2WikiMultiHopQA. Overall, we show that EMA-PG is a simple, principled, and powerful approach to scaling RL for LLMs. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Bio-PM-Guided Synthetic IMU Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26607)**  `arXiv:2611.26607`  `cs.LG`  
  _Deepali Singh, Deepesh Parpani, Aviral Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collecting and labeling inertial measurement unit (IMU) data for human activity recognition (HAR) is expensive, and models trained on one group of subjects often fail to generalize to unseen individuals. Even with balanced data, classifiers struggle to distinguish dynamically similar activity classes and benefit from targeted augmentation. We explore whether synthetic IMU data generated in the representation space of a pretrained biosignal encoder, Bio-PM \cite{b1}, can serve as a practical augmentation source. Rather than synthesizing raw signals, we leverage Bio-PM's frozen encoder to convert 10-second IMU windows into token embedding sequences and train class-conditional decoders in this space: (1) an autoregressive transformer decoder that predicts tokens sequentially, and (2) a diffusion decoder that learns the token distribution through a denoising objective. We analyze generation realism through distributional metrics and embedding-space visualizations, evaluate downstream utility through leave-one-subject-out HAR classification, and present a PatchDecoder for approximate raw waveform reconstruction. Our diffusion decoder achieves a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real (TSTR) macro-F1 of 0.68, demonstrating that generated tokens are realistic enough to train a classifier that transfers to real data. Combining synthetic-token augmentation with a 320-dimensional gravity-aware feature representation raises LOSO macro-F1 from 0.729 to 0.809 over a real-only baseline across 51 subjects on the WISDM benchmark, with statistically significant gains concentrated in the most challenging activity classes. These results demonstrate that high-fidelity generation in a pretrained encoder's representation space is achievable and practically useful for cross-subject HAR augmentation.
  </details>

- **[FEA solver integration of a composite World Model with a Spiking Neural Network memory Block for computationally efficient modelling of defects and variabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26419)**  `arXiv:2611.26419`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bassam El Said, Adam Sobey_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Each fibre-reinforced composite structure has a unique material architecture. The manufacturing methods used and the presence of defects lead to local variations which require material-scale models that can resolve the response in real time at every location in the structure. Concurrent multi-scale schemes provide this capability, but at a prohibitive computational cost for large structures as the number of integration points and time increments scales with size. Surrogates have been studied extensively as replacements for material-scale models to mitigate these computational limitations. A surrogate must represent an arbitrary local architecture including defects, track the irreversible material state over an unknown number of increments, remain stable when driven recursively by its own predictions, and fit within the memory available at every integration point. Here, a Composites World Model based on a Multi-Modal Spiking Transformer architecture is designed to meet these requirements. The proposed architecture combines a material encoder, a Spiking Neural Network memory block that tracks the long-term internal state of the material, and transformer blocks that learn the short-term applied strain state. The system is trained in three stages: material architecture encoding, base training on short time windows, and recursive fine-tuning with scheduled sampling to suppress cumulative error drift. The trained model is disassembled at inference and coupled to a non-linear FE solver, enabling structural scale simulation of composites containing embedded defects. Benchmarks against experimental results for IM7/8552 laminates under tension, compression and transverse shear, for pristine and wrinkled composites, show that the SNN memory block successfully tracks the material internal state and results in prediction errors between 0.4% and 4.8%.
  </details>

- **[Evidence from a Multi-Regime Causal Discovery Benchmark: UCMT Algorithms, Comparative Performance, and Controlled Ablation Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26383)**  `arXiv:2611.26383`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mahyar Eslami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an internal, pre-registered evaluation of the UCMT causal discovery algorithm family alongside established alternatives across heterogeneous benchmark regimes, complemented by a separately pre-registered, controlled study of UCMT v2 Global components. The contribution is both empirical and methodological: a family-stratified comparison of three actively developed algorithm configurations and targeted evidence about which internal stages affect their outcomes.
