In the main benchmark, UCMT v2 Global has higher directed F1 than NOTEARS and DAGMA in selected real, semi-synthetic and Gaussian-network comparisons at the paired-run level, while trailing leading permutation-search methods in other settings. The BOSS-based UCMT v4 produces a CSuite-specific directed-F1 improvement over BOSS (mean paired difference +0.279; pair-level Holm p < 0.001), but the dataset-level adjusted result is not significant (p = 0.188). In a separate fixed-skeleton ablation, UCMT v2 Global exceeds an adapted R^2-SortnRegress orientation baseline on standardized iSCM data (directed F1 0.537 vs 0.468, dataset-level Holm p = 0.031); absolute orientation accuracy is 0.563 and there is no established broad advantage over RESIT, ANM or DirectLiNGAM. Controlled ablations show that sparsity and BIC-based refinement materially affect some outcomes, whereas the examined arbitration and automatic regime-selection mechanisms do not yield confirmed general improvements. We report performance shortcomings, multiple-testing correction, runtime costs, evaluation limitations and reproducibility checks. The results support UCMT as an implemented and empirically investigated algorithm family, not a claim of overall superiority, independent validation or demonstrated commercial impact.
  </details>

- **[From Benchmarks to the Bench: Preference, Correspondence and Selectivity in Bidirectional Drug-Target Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26369)**  `arXiv:2611.26369`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiangjie Qiu, Chengrui Xu, Xiaonan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying active molecules for known targets and potential targets for existing drugs is central to drug discovery and repurposing. Drug-target interaction (DTI) models can rank vast numbers of drug-target pairs, but limited experimental capacity restricts testing to a small number of candidates. We compared the bidirectional recommendations of 13 released systems across 711 molecular structures of FDA-approved drugs and 614 human proteins. For the same target or drug, pairs of models produced completely disjoint Top-10 molecule and protein lists in 77.8% and 70.1% of comparisons, respectively. Even where recommendations overlapped, agreement between some systems largely reflected recurring preferences for the same candidates. Published inhibition measurements for 79 molecules and 65 kinases revealed that some systems enriched active candidates, yet their hit yields changed little when recommendations were matched to different drugs or targets, whereas other systems lost substantially more hits. Some of the former systems also rarely ranked both molecules and kinases correctly when two drugs favoured different kinases. We further retrained the same architecture on the same data, with the training objective fixed and random seeds varied. Despite similar test performance, mean pairwise Top-10 overlap on a separate screening catalogue was about two candidates. Our study moves DTI model evaluation beyond benchmark performance to assess whether model predictions can guide meaningful experimental decisions in drug discovery and target identification.
  </details>

- **[Mamba Flow Matching Neural Processes: Linear-Time Inference for Irregularly Observed Spatial Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26355)**  `arXiv:2611.26355`  `cs.LG`  
  _Cosmo Santoni, Giovanni Charles, Timothy James Hitge, Oliver Watson, Elizaveta Semenova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpolating a spatial field, such as daily maximum temperature, from sparse, irregularly located observations recurs across the environmental and health sciences. Standard Gaussian-process kriging refits hyperparameters and solves an $O(N^3)$ system for every new field. When many related fields are observed, such as daily maps from one sensor network, neural processes (NPs) instead learn the interpolation map from past fields and predict a new field in one forward pass. Joint-predictive variants, which sample whole fields rather than independent per-location values, typically use self-attention over context and query points, costing $O(K^2)$ in the combined point count $K$ and limiting practical use to a few thousand points. State-space models offer a linear-time alternative, but a direct state-space adaptation compresses the context into one hidden state before reading queries, reducing what the decoder sees. We propose MambaFlowNP, a linear-compute joint-predictive NP trained with conditional flow matching. A multi-directional Mamba-2 scan reads context and query tokens in one shared sequence, so each query's velocity is read from its own scan position rather than a compressed context summary, and because queries only read the scan state, predictions are consistent across query sets. MambaFlowNP is competitive with the strongest attention-based joint-predictive baselines on synthetic Matérn Gaussian-process benchmarks, and on regional to continental-scale NOAA temperature interpolation it attains the lowest RMSE and CRPS and near-nominal coverage on every tier, while running 114--784$\times$ faster than the strongest joint-predictive baseline. A single forward pass scales to $10^6$ points on one NVIDIA L40S GPU in under a second. This makes joint prediction feasible for continental-scale, irregularly sampled sensor networks; predictive quality is evaluated up to $K=5{,}580$.
  </details>

- **[TRCF: Temporal-Reference Complementary Forecasting for Irregular Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26351)**  `arXiv:2611.26351`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xudong Zuo, Shusen Ma, Qirui Chen, Bing Fan, Yun-Bo Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular multivariate time series forecasting supports healthcare monitoring, environmental analysis, and activity recognition. Channel-independent (CI) approaches capture each variable's temporal patterns but do not explicitly use observations from other variables. Channel-dependent (CD) modeling exploits relationships among variables, yet asynchronous measurements make these relationships difficult to learn. Incorporating cross-variable information while preserving each variable's irregular history therefore remains a key challenge. We propose Temporal-Reference Complementary Forecasting (TRCF), a framework that augments a compact CI forecaster with a complementary CD branch. ANCHOR aggregates observed values and timestamps at shared temporal locations, retaining observation support to form compact CI features. BRIDGE constructs complementary features through history completion. It first estimates each variable's historical values from its own observations, then uses differences between other variables' observed and estimated values at nearby times to correct missing entries. The completed history preserves observed values and is encoded to supply context alongside the direct CI representation. Finally, the two representations are fused, combined with the query-time embedding, and passed to a shallow multilayer perceptron for forecasting. Experiments on four real-world datasets show that TRCF achieves the lowest mean squared error and mean absolute error in the main benchmark comparison, while reducing measured peak inference memory by 17.28% to 87.98% relative to APN.
  </details>

- **[NariQ: Sub-3-Bit Quantization for Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26157)**  `arXiv:2611.26157`  `cs.LG`  
  _Doyeob Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agentic tasks require a model to call tools over many steps at contexts of 100K tokens or more, yet many low-bit quantization methods are calibrated on short web text and judged on perplexity. This mismatch is costly: existing quantizations of Qwen3.8-27B at or below 3 bits per weight match BF16 on shorter tasks like AIME, yet in published results solve at most 72% as many tasks as BF16 on DeepSWE, a long-horizon agentic benchmark. We propose NariQ, a trellis-coded post-training quantization method that calibrates on tokens generated by the BF16 model in agent sessions. On Qwen3.8-27B, NariQ at 3.0 and 2.5 bits per weight stays level with BF16 on two long-horizon agentic benchmarks, DeepSWE and Terminal Bench, as well as on AIME and RealWorldQA. Our checkpoint at 2.0 bits per weight trails this performance. To our knowledge, these are the first quantizations at or below 3 bits per weight shown to match their full-precision version on long-horizon agentic tasks. We release the files as Qwen3.8-27B-NariQ-GGUF (this https URL), with inference available through ourthis http URLfork atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Iterative Scarcity-Guided Exploration: Bootstrapping Generative Auto-bidding from Narrow Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26060)**  `arXiv:2611.26060`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qingmao Yao, Guangzheng Hu, Yusen Huo, Zhilin Zhang, Chuan Yu, Jian Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auto-bidding is a core algorithmic component in online advertising auctions. In practical cold-start settings, iterative training is commonly used to compensate for low-quality, narrowly supported historical data. Unfortunately, in iterative training, representative diffusion-based AI-Generated Bidding (AIGB) methods fail to sustain extrapolation beyond the narrow data support and stagnate at a suboptimal level. In this paper, we theoretically attribute this stagnation to Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) collapse: the weak radial return signal is overwhelmed by a curvature-induced penalty. To break this stagnation, we propose \textbf{Iterative Scarcity-Guided Exploration (ISGE)}, which introduces a guidance handoff: as the return signal collapses, scarcity subsequently takes over as an exploratory signal to elevate the SNR above a critical threshold. Specifically, ISGE iteratively alternates between a \emph{Judger} that assigns scarcity scores to trajectories and an \emph{Explorer} that performs guided generation of high-scarcity and high-return trajectories, thereby bootstrapping from the narrow support with such self-generated trajectories. Extensive experiments on the industrial AuctionNet benchmark demonstrate that ISGE, starting from cold-start datasets, effectively surpasses the performance of the Full-Dataset baseline within three iterations. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Seeing Beyond the Obvious: Complementary Semantic Guidance for Salient Single-Positive Multi-Label Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25986)**  `arXiv:2611.25986`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tong Jin, Meng-Long Wang, Xingyu Zhao, Sheng-Jun Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Salient Single-Positive Multi-Label Learning (SalSPML) assigns each image its most salient positive label, leaving other classes unlabeled. Existing methods construct class-level Salient Prototypes from observed positives to guide the recognition of unobserved classes. However, since salient annotations favor visually prominent instances, these prototypes may not fully capture the visual diversity of hidden positives, resulting in insufficient positive supervision. To address this limitation, we propose CoST, a Coverage-guided Complementary Soft-Target Refinement framework that supplements Salient Prototypes with complementary class-semantic information. Specifically, CoST identifies semantically relevant candidates from unknown classes by leveraging task-learned category information and image-text semantic correspondence. The selected candidates are then aggregated into class-wise Coverage Prototypes to complement the semantic representations provided by Salient Prototypes. Furthermore, we introduce a ranking-based selective refinement strategy that identifies classes receiving stronger semantic support from Coverage Prototypes and selectively enhances their soft targets while preserving the base soft targets for the rest. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of CoST, with strong performance gains over existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[Gradient Boosting with Kolmogorov-Arnold Units for Interpretable Regression beyond Pairwise Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25942)**  `arXiv:2611.25942`  `cs.LG`  
  _Minjong Cheon, Hanseon Joo, Eunji Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpretable models for tabular regression, such as the explainable boosting machine (EBM), restrict a prediction to main effects and pairwise interactions so that every part of it can be plotted. When the target depends on three or more inputs jointly, this restriction costs accuracy, and a black-box model with post hoc explanations becomes the usual fallback. Here we present MJBoost, a gradient boosting method whose weak learner is a single Kolmogorov-Arnold unit $g(\sum_p \phi_p(x_p))$ with penalized spline inner and outer functions. Each unit is fitted in closed form by alternating penalized least squares, starting from principal Hessian directions of the residual, and categorical and missing inputs enter the units directly. Because a unit depends on each input only through one inner function, the fitted model has an exact functional ANOVA decomposition into main effects, pair effects and a higher-order remainder, which one-dimensional convolutions compute. On synthetic targets of interaction order three to six, the test error of MJBoost is up to 13 times lower than that of EBM, and its decomposition recovers the true variance share of every order to within 0.04. On the 35 regression tasks of the OpenML-CTR23 benchmark, it has a lower test RMSE than EBM on 21 tasks and a higher one on 2 (geometric-mean RMSE -29.1%). On these tasks the main and pair effects of the fitted model explain a median 0.992 of its variance, so the model reads as plots where the data allow, and the decomposition measures the higher-order part where they do not. With Newton steps the same algorithm classifies: on 32 binary tasks of OpenML-CC18 its test log-loss is on par with that of EBM, and on 11 multiclass tasks its mean log-loss is lower than that of EBM on 8 and than that of tuned LightGBM on 7.
  </details>

- **[DTS: Delta-Based Tile Scoring for Efficient Long-Context Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25938)**  `arXiv:2611.25938`  `cs.LG`  
  _Emre Uğur, Jiawen Qi, Qinyu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly need to process long documents and codebases, making long-context attention a major prefill bottleneck. Block sparse attention reduces computation by skipping unimportant blocks of the attention matrix, but scoring blocks accurately can itself be expensive. We propose Delta-Based Tile Scoring (DTS), a training-free, content-adaptive tile scoring method for sparse block attention. DTS selects query and key anchors according to local vector changes, using fewer representatives in redundant regions while retaining more detail in heterogeneous regions. To accelerate anchor selection, parallel delta packing partitions the query and key sequences into tile-aligned chunks and performs bounded local scans over these chunks concurrently. The selected tiles are then computed using the original query, key, and value vectors. On LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, DTS keeps average RULER and LongBench scores within 0.10 percentage points of full attention, with RULER evaluated up to 128K tokens. At 128K tokens, DTS reduces measured scoring-pipeline latency by 38% compared with XAttention. On four NVIDIA H100 GPUs, it achieves a 3.3$\times$ prefill speedup over a PyTorch full-attention baseline using FlashAttention-2 at 512K tokens.
  </details>

- **[Explainability of genotype embedding models through observed cohort paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25681)**  `arXiv:2611.25681`  `cs.LG`  
  _Amashi Niwarthana, Aloysius Tan, Jagath C. Rajapakse_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models that combine genotypes with neuroimaging predict neurological diseases more accurately than either modality alone. Their clinical use, however, requires identifying which genetic variants a prediction is based on. Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients address this by integrating the model's gradient from a baseline to the input. These methods assume the model accepts any point along that path as a valid input. Genotype models violate this assumption. They map the three discrete allele dosages \{0,1,2\} to vectors through a learned embedding defined only at those values, so a straight line passes through fractional genotypes at which no embedding, and therefore no gradient, exists. Attribution must therefore proceed in embedding space where the choice of path can affect the resulting attribution. We show that the commonly used per-variant estimator does not generally satisfy completeness. Additive separability guarantees completeness for this estimator, but interactions between variants generally break that property. Instead, we integrate along piecewise-linear embedding-space paths connecting genotype waypoints observed in the training cohort. Each path satisfies completeness in the continuum although the interiors of its segments are not necessarily observed. Our method improves faithfulness over Integrated Gradients and discretized Integrated Gradients under both perturbation protocols, achieves the strongest results for Parkinson's disease classification under support-preserving perturbations, and is competitive with a support-background Shapley baseline under per-variant perturbations. It also shows higher seed stability and better recovery of planted non-additive effects in a synthetic benchmark.
  </details>

- **[PSTO: A PDE-structured transport operator with shared implicit updates for multiscale CDR systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25607)**  `arXiv:2611.25607`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiwen Li, Dezhi Zhou, Kun Wu, Xingcai Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiscale convection-diffusion-reaction (CDR) systems couple spatial transport with local stiff reaction kinetics, making their numerical simulation computationally demanding. Conventional neural surrogates require costly coupled CDR training data, and their parameter counts scale with the number of transported variables, limiting scalability to high-dimensional CDR systems. Here we propose a PDE-Structured Transport Operator (PSTO) that learns convection--diffusion dynamics solely from reaction-free data. By embedding the PDE structure into the neural architecture, PSTO generalizes to full CDR systems at inference without retraining. A single transport operator block is shared across all transported variables and refined through relaxed fixed-point iterations, performing an implicit-like correction in latent space. This improves stability during recursive multi-step prediction without adding trainable parameters. We evaluate PSTO on three benchmarks: a canonical 2D CDR system, a multiscale Robertson counterflow flame, and a multiscale hydrogen planar jet flame. In every case, PSTO reduces the mean relative $L_2$ error after 100 coupled steps by at least 50% compared to the strongest neural baseline. Ablation studies on the fixed-point iterations and detailed error decomposition further confirm that PSTO achieves both high one-step accuracy and long-term stability in coupled rollouts. Time-step sensitivity analysis shows that PSTO maintains accuracy even with a tenfold larger time step, whereas baseline accuracies decay rapidly as the time step increases. This framework enables efficient, modular modeling of multiscale CDR dynamics by separating transport learning from the treatment of local kinetics.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement-Aligned Probabilistic Weather Forecasting with Discrete Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25570)**  `arXiv:2611.25570`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jinhan Li, Zhisheng Chen, Yuxuan Li, Yuan Gao, Fan Xu, Hao Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Global medium-range weather forecasting must not only generate ensembles efficiently to characterize uncertainty in future states, but also serve downstream applications such as extreme-event warnings and cross-sphere coupled simulations. We introduce WeatherCraft, a framework that unifies probabilistic generation and reinforcement learning alignment through discrete weather tokens, bringing the GPT-style pretraining--post-training paradigm to global medium-range weather forecasting. A multiscale tokenizer represents atmospheric states as a coarse-to-fine token hierarchy, while a Transformer models causal dependencies across time and scale and predicts tokens within each scale in parallel. Direct sampling generates diverse ensembles, and exact token log-probabilities enable Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to maximize downstream task rewards without differentiating through evaluators or coupled models. On the ERA5 benchmark, WeatherCraft's eight-member ensemble mean reduces day-15 normalized RMSE by 17.6% relative to the strongest evaluated deterministic baseline and, with KV caching, achieves an approximately $18\times$ inference speedup over GenCast. Direct fair-CRPS adaptation improves ensemble probabilistic skill, while reward-based post-training improves forecasts of persistent land temperature extremes and coupled ocean heat extremes. This work establishes discrete autoregressive modeling as a general foundation for scalable, task-alignable Earth system foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Protein Functional Semantic Alignment via Representation Rectification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25396)**  `arXiv:2611.25396`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chuyang Zhou, Bingxin Zhou, Andi Han, Chang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein language models (PLMs) are commonly pretrained with likelihood-based objectives such as masked language modeling or denoising. These objectives capture sequence-level regularities, but can produce representations in which functional signals are entangled with non-functional variation (e.g., sequence identity, compositional bias, and frequent local motifs), creating a readout bottleneck for downstream function prediction. We introduce Functional Semantic Alignment via Representation Rectification (SAVR), a contrastive post-training method using Pfam family co-membership as a homology-guided discrete semantic signal to reshape PLM representations. SAVR reduces directional dominance and redistributes variance across embedding dimensions, decreasing variance concentration, and increasing effective dimensionality of the embedding covariance spectrum. Without observing any task-specific labels during post-training, SAVR achieves state-of-the-art results on Enzyme Commission (EC) benchmarks and competitive results on Gene Ontology (GO) benchmarks under frozen-backbone evaluation. These results suggest that homology-guided contrastive post-training can rectify PLM representation geometry and improve sequence-based protein function prediction.
  </details>

- **[TuringSpace: Grounding Spatial Intelligence in Action with Interactive CAPTCHAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25387)**  `arXiv:2611.25387`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xuwei Ding, Skylar Zhai, Linxin Song, Jiarui Feng, Dongqi Fu, Huajun Xi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans act in the visual world by inferring the 3D layout behind a 2D view, predicting how an action changes it, and correcting themselves after seeing the result. Can multimodal large language models (MLLMs) do the same? CAPTCHAs offer a natural test, since humans solve them through this very loop, clicking and dragging on the screen and adjusting after each result. Yet existing CAPTCHA benchmarks are largely solved and rarely spatial, while spatial benchmarks rarely require action. We therefore present TuringSpace, a synthetic web environment of 125 interactive CAPTCHAs that span five spatial abilities and must be solved through clicks and drags on the screen. Every task is a program that generates new instances with a checker and an oracle, so the tasks also form a scalable training gym. Humans solve 94.1% of the tasks, whereas the best model, GPT-6 Astra, still fails 25.9% of them, and the best open-weight model, Kimi K3, fails 68.0%. Our further analysis shows that most failures come from misreading the screenshot, and that model reasoning rarely corrects a wrong belief but hallucinates evidence to defend it. To test whether such spatial abilities can be learned, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B on this gym. Its success rate rises from 1.6% to 33.1%, above Kimi K3, and it improves on all seven out-of-distribution benchmarks, including a 9.9-point gain on the real-world indoor videos of VSI-Bench. These results show that grounding spatial intelligence in screen actions exposes what MLLMs lack and offers a scalable way to teach it, with gains that transfer to real-world scenes. We will release TuringSpace to support further study.
  </details>

- **[Class-Conditional Conformal Triage for ICAO Face-Image Compliance under Class Imbalance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25342)**  `arXiv:2611.25342`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mustafa Vedat Yildirim, Inna Skarga-Bandurova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated ICAO/ISO face-image compliance analysers provide criterion-level pass/fail decisions, yet these verdicts do not quantify uncertainty when deciding whether to accept, reject, or retake a photograph. We investigate conformal prediction as a triage layer for BioGaze, evaluating 14 compliance criteria on the synthetic TONO benchmark. Without retraining the analyser, we fit per-criterion logistic classifiers and compare marginal and label-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction with conventional abstention rules using subject-disjoint splits. Marginal conformal prediction achieved approximately 0.90 overall coverage but substantially under-covered violations, with a ten-partition mean of 0.37 for one criterion. The corresponding Mondrian means ranged from 0.89 to 0.92 across criteria. However, most of the reduction in criterion-level false positives came from the logistic layer (0.145 to 0.048), while tuned abstention rules achieved lower false-positive rates than Mondrian at comparable retake rates. Bonferroni correction for joint coverage across 14 criteria required retaking nearly all images. These findings show that class-conditional conformal prediction can achieve near-target empirical coverage for minority violation classes and reveal trade-offs between statistical coverage, classification performance, and retake requirements in multi-criterion face-image compliance assessment.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Question Reliable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25292)**  `arXiv:2611.25292`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hector G. Rodriguez, Marcus Rohrbach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems operating near the limits of their capabilities can maximize utility while minimizing the risk of error by abstaining or deferring questions they are not confident about to a human or a stronger model. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been post-trained to estimate confidence in their own answers. To achieve this, methods reinforce confidence scores based on the correctness signal to each question-answer pair individually, with rewards that encourage assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect answers. However, estimating confidence scores is useful in that it enables selecting only the most confident answers, to maximize the share of solved questions while accommodating the user's risk tolerance. Assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect ones is theoretically optimal, but unattainable in practice and detrimental for selective prediction with a non-zero risk tolerance: confidences pushed to 0 and 1 carry no information about how answers compare to one another. We therefore introduce CARE (Cross-question Abstention Reward Estimation), which computes rewards over a set of question-answer pairs: At each of several risk tolerances, the confidences across the set induce an abstention threshold, and each confidence is rewarded for lying on the correct side of it. Since the threshold itself comes from the set, every verbalized confidence is reinforced relative to other questions' confidence, not against its own binary correctness label. On math, CARE increases the coverage at 5% risk (C@5) across MATH-500, GSM8K, and Big-Math-Digits from 0.8% to 42.4%, compared to existing pointwise correctness-matching rewards. When trained on multi-hop Wikipedia QA from HotpotQA, CARE improves C@5 in out-of-distribution general-knowledge, reasoning, and math benchmarks (including CommonsenseQA, GPQA, SimpleQA, and TriviaQA) from 12.3% to 23.6%.
  </details>

- **[Moving Generality from Model to Modeler: LLM Agents for PHM on a Deployment-Style Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25252)**  `arXiv:2611.25252`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenling Chen, Zhiguo Zeng, Anne Barros_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents built on large language models (LLMs) now help develop many models published as general solutions. Should generality instead reside in the agent that builds a model for each new task? We examine this question in prognostics and health management (PHM), where every model must work on unseen equipment. Our deployment-style benchmark, NextUnit-Bench, covers 24 public datasets split into 58 folds that hold out units or operating conditions; each agent has two hours to deliver an executable predictor, then run on the hidden units. First, frontier agents outperform AutoML, which reaches less than half the best agent's score, and faithful implementations of published methods on four datasets, of which only 6 of 30 beat the strongest agent's own design; published accuracies, where reproducible, rest on the papers' own conventions, and a single convention, capping the remaining life of test engines, can halve the reported error. Second, the agents' advantage is a design made on each machine's data; once made, a plain-text plan of a few hundred words carries most of the gap between two frontier agents, and given a weaker agent's plan, the strongest agent falls to that agent's level, often to identical predictions. Before touching the data, however, it matters little whose plan an agent carries out, and domain toolkits appear to help neither: what sets the strongest apart is how much it learns from the data. Third, such designs reach only implementers that can execute them: given the strongest agent's designs, a locally deployable open-weight model delivers half as often, even when they are lightened, and scores no higher than without them. Generality, we argue, is not disappearing but moving, from the model to the modeler.
  </details>

- **[ϕTD: Distributional Reinforcement Learning using Characteristic Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25208)**  `arXiv:2611.25208`  `cs.LG`  
  _Thomas Mousseau, Tyler Kastner, Davide Baldelli, Amir-massoud Farahmand_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributional Reinforcement Learning methods aim to learn the entire distribution of returns, yet current algorithmic approaches are limited to a narrow class of distributions. We propose $\varphi$TD, a method to overcome this limitation by computing the loss in the frequency domain. We demonstrate that this further unifies quantile and categorical approaches to distributional RL, and enables the learning of distributions that were previously impossible to capture, such as mixtures of continuous distributions. We empirically study $\varphi$TD, across synthetic benchmarks and large-scale deep RL environments and demonstrate that the flexibility obtained from wider parametric families can lead to improved performance.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian $κ$-Explorer for Minimax Model Estimation in MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25160)**  `arXiv:2611.25160`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xihe Gu, Urbashi Mitra, Tara Javidi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study active exploration for minimax transition estimation in unknown Markov decision processes (MDPs). Frequentist $\kappa$-Explorer~\citep{gu2026kappa} addresses this problem through a parameterized family of exploration objectives, but requires confidence-bound constructions and joint optimization over plausible transition kernels and occupancy measures, introducing a computational burden that limits its practical applicability. We develop Bayesian $\kappa$-Explorer, which simplifies this procedure by placing Dirichlet priors on the transition distributions. Under the posterior mean estimator, the posterior expected squared $\ell_2$ error equals the total posterior variance, providing a closed-form exploration signal. The algorithm plans under the posterior mean model, eliminating confidence-set optimization. Under the stated assumptions, this simpler planning procedure retains the $\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/3})$ surrogate convergence rate of frequentist $\kappa$-Explorer. We further bound the maximum posterior variance relative to the optimal asymptotic minimax allocation benchmark. Experiments on Pendulum, Mountain Car, and Taxi demonstrate lower worst-case transition-estimation errors than the tested baselines.
  </details>

- **[Rate-distortion Assessment for Hierarchical Content Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25151)**  `arXiv:2611.25151`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunye Gong, Indranil Sur, Jared Claypoole, Pritish Sahu, Ajay Divakaran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Powerful generative models including large language and vision models have demonstrated success across a wide range of content generation, editing and manipulation tasks via end-to-end prompt-based generation. However, current assessment of the quality of generative content manipulation remains subjective, limiting scientific understanding of model capabilities. Inspired by rate-distortion theory from Shannon information compression theory, in this work we formalize hierarchical content summarization as an information encoding problem. We design a principled framework to extract the semantic hierarchy of the source content to support quantitative characterization of the rate-distortion trade-off between compression efficiency (rate) against information loss (distortion) during generative content manipulation, which allows us to benchmark various models with respect to performance conditional on rate budget of interest. We demonstrate the use of the framework with empirical experiments on content summarization for long-context question-answering with state-of-the-art large language models, offering new insights into their efficiency and comprehension capabilities. We further demonstrate the use of the semantic hierarchy as guidance for performing effective retrieval augmented generation, showing improved rate-distortion characteristics against current baselines.
  </details>

- **[Federated Physics-Informed Thermal Field Prediction Across Unseen Geometries in Distributed Additive Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17794)**  `arXiv:2611.17794`  `cs.LG`  
  _Amirul Islam Saimon, Zhenyu James Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In additive manufacturing (AM), each change in part geometry requires a new thermal simulation, which makes design evaluation over many candidates costly. Geometry-aware models reduce this cost by predicting thermal fields on unseen parts with a single learned model, but they assume that diverse geometries are available centrally. This assumption is restrictive when each manufacturing site holds only a limited set of proprietary part designs that it cannot share. We propose Fed-PIGANO, a federated framework that trains physics-informed geometry-aware neural operator (PI-GANO) models at individual sites and aggregates their geometry encoders and prediction networks without sharing part geometries. We evaluate the framework on a 2D steady-state thermal benchmark for metal AM, with geometries from five polygonal families distributed across three sites under four scenarios that range from identical to strongly different family compositions. Over seven replications, Fed-PIGANO achieves mean relative L2 errors of 1.83% to 2.04% across all 12 scenario-site cases, compared with up to 20.46% for local-only training, and consistently yields lower PDE residuals. Its prediction accuracy is comparable to that of a centralized model trained on the pooled geometries of all sites. These results demonstrate that federated learning lets manufacturing sites benefit from each other's geometries without sharing them, enabling rapid thermal prediction on new part designs for tasks such as shape optimization, inverse design, and uncertainty quantification.
  </details>

- **[When Does Causality Help in Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15073)**  `arXiv:2611.15073`  `cs.LG`  
  _Long Zhang, Wei Huang, Aishui Rao, Jian Hu, Binxiang Wu, Jie Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causality-aware scoring is common in multivariate time-series anomaly detection, yet the literature rarely states when it helps. Holding one causality-guided autoencoder (DeCauAE) fixed, we vary graph quality, anomaly type and protocol against ground truth. Graph quality fixes the payoff: on a twenty-variable DAG the gain over a graph-free autoencoder decays as the graph is corrupted. Anomaly type fixes the target: the structural factor lifts window F1 from 0.800 to 0.976 where anomalies break conditional structure, and its advantage vanishes below a break severity s 0.3. Protocol fixes the ranking: point-adjusted F1 lets a random score match the full model, while range-aware VUS separates the autoencoder family from the classical baselines. On real benchmarks the causal module pays only where density makes a system eligible and cross-subsample reproducibility selects entities inside it; enabling it everywhere costs 0.012 VUS-ROC. The structural factor also transfers to classical detectors .
  </details>

- **[Generative Forecasting from Coarse Observations: Does Cascading Help?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00686)**  `arXiv:2611.00686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alexandra Spitzer, Giovanni Abati, Lorenzo Baldassari, Till Muser, Cheng Shi, Ivan Dokmanić_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study probabilistic forecasting of dynamical systems such as fluids and waves from coarse observations. Since the unseen fine scales influence the dynamics, the same coarse initial state can evolve into a whole distribution of futures. Generative forecasters may produce visually plausible samples, but it is hard to tell whether they sample the right conditional distribution, since trajectory data contain a single future for each initial state. We address this problem by generating reference data in a hierarchical way: for each coarse initial state, we generate many different fine-scale components and numerically evolve the full states. Using these conditional ensembles, we show that standard diffusion models and stochastic interpolants can make substantial distributional errors when trained over the full forecast horizon with finite data and model capacity, despite being exact in principle. We then show that cascading shorter forecasts greatly reduces these errors for the turbulent flows and waves we study, but we also find and characterize cases in which it hurts. Since the coarse process is not Markov, cascading discards information about the unresolved fine scales even with exact transitions. To understand this, we propose a solvable analytic model which shows that the optimal cascade depth decreases as the coupling between the unresolved and observed scales gets stronger, and increases as the dependence of the forecast on the observation becomes more oscillatory. Consistent with our experiments, cascading is detrimental when the direct forecast is "easy" to learn, for example Gaussian as in the passive scalar case. We release our datasets as a first benchmark for probabilistic forecasting with ground-truth conditional ensembles.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization: Unified Learning from Offline Demonstrations and On- and Off-Policy Rollouts via a Shared Regression Target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12830)**  `arXiv:2610.12830`  `cs.LG`  
  _Du Cheng, Junyu Lu, Jingfan Zhang, Haoxuan Jia, Shichao Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning post-training draws on offline demonstrations and policy-generated rollouts, yet conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) separates their objectives and limits continued reuse of collected responses. We propose \textbf{Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization} (CBPO), which makes these sources compatible with one regression loss through a fixed-reference target. Because the generating policy does not enter this target, positive and negative offline completions, reference responses, and current and historical rollouts can share a common reward-labeled record format and replay buffer. As new responses enter the buffer, optimization retains the same regression objective without coordinating separate SFT and RL objectives or requiring stored behavior-policy probabilities, importance weighting, or a learned critic. Restricting the available sources gives offline, on-policy, and off-policy variants, while CBPO-Unified combines them. Across two model scales and four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CBPO delivers leading \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} performance across benchmarks and data regimes. On Qwen3-1.7B, CBPO-Unified improves benchmark-averaged \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} over SFT DAPO by 2.33 and 4.44 percentage points, respectively. In the replay ablation, retaining historical rollouts improves these metrics by 1.87 and 3.72 percentage points over the no-history configuration.
  </details>

- **[Towards Better Exploration in Sequential Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39632)**  `arXiv:2609.39632`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joseph Rance, Fabio Pizzati, Juil Sock, Woody Bayliss, Marc Górriz Blanch, Philip Torr, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling improves language model reasoning by spending additional compute at inference. However, both classes of existing methods often fail to continue improving over long timescales. Parallel methods repeatedly sample independent answers from the model, scaling poorly on problems the model is unlikely to solve in a single attempt. In contrast, sequential methods build on previous answers to access new ideas, yet so far have not been shown to reach answers beyond those found by parallel scaling. First, we show that sequential scaling often stops improving because it becomes prematurely trapped in an attractor: a set of answers that prevents exploration of different answers once entered. Across 27 combinations of scaling methods, models, and benchmarks, we find that 53.8% of sequential scaling trajectories enter an attractor within four iterations. Second, we show that a simple model-mixing intervention helps escape attractors. This reduces the attractor hit rate by 21.2 percentage points on average, expands solution coverage beyond a compute-matched parallel baseline, and improves accuracy of recursive self-aggregation by at least 2.2 percentage points. Our results motivate refocusing long-horizon test-time scaling from parallel methods to sequential methods that improve previous answers.
  </details>

- **[From field-scale to large-scale spectral libraries: Tabular foundation models in soil spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00608)**  `arXiv:2608.00608`  `cs.LG`  
  _Viacheslav Barkov, Jonas Schmidinger, Robin Gebbers, Martin Atzmueller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visible and near-infrared (vis-NIR) and mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy enable rapid, cost-effective prediction of soil properties. Yet, translating high-dimensional, highly collinear spectra into accurate soil property predictions remains challenging, particularly when employing machine learning. We systematically investigated regression models and dimensionality reduction approaches for spectroscopic modeling across 85 soil property prediction tasks from open benchmark datasets in pedometrics, spanning field-scale digital soil mapping and a global soil spectral library. We compared an in-context learning tabular foundation model (TabPFN), a convolutional neural network (CNN), rule-based regression (Cubist), Random Forest, and partial least squares regression (PLSR) using full spectra as well as features derived from principal component analysis (PCA) and partial least squares (PLS) latent variables. TabPFN consistently delivered the best overall performance across spectral regions, properties, and scales, including large spectral library tasks with tens of thousands of soil samples. Notably, TabPFN applied directly to full spectra already surpassed all classical baselines, showing that explicit dimensionality reduction is not strictly required for strong performance. Further improvements were achieved through PLS, which proved to be an effective dimensionality reduction strategy for all models. Combining PLS latent variables with TabPFN yielded the best predictions overall, lowering the root mean squared error (RMSE) relative to the strongest classical baseline on 44 of the 46 large-scale spectral library tasks, by a median of 14%. Our findings provide evidence-based guidance for spectroscopic calibration model selection across operational scales, demonstrating that the long-standing advantages of PLSR and modern tabular foundation models complement each other in chemometrics.
  </details>

- **[A Byzantine-Robust Federated Learning with Nesterov's Momentum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02657)**  `arXiv:2511.02657`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lihan Xu, Xiaoyi Fan, Gang Wang, Runhao Zeng, Xiping Hu, Yanjie Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate robust federated learning, where a group of workers collaboratively train a shared model under the orchestration of a central server in the presence of Byzantine adversaries capable of arbitrary and potentially malicious behaviors. To improve communication efficiency while retaining resilience against such adversaries, we propose a Byzantine-resilient Nesterov-augmented federated learning (Byrd-NAFL) algorithm. Byrd-NAFL applies Nesterov's momentum at the server to the output of any soft $\gamma$-Byzantine resilient aggregation rule, while each worker only computes and uploads a stochastic gradient, which keeps the workers stateless, as desired for intermittently participating Internet of Things devices. For smooth non-convex losses, we establish a finite-time convergence guarantee for all momentum factors $\beta\in[0,1)$ without requiring the aggregated gradient to be unbiased or bounded; the bound reduces to that of Byzantine-resilient stochastic gradient descent at $\beta=0$ and is tighter than that of server-side heavy-ball momentum. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Sentiment140 show that Byrd-NAFL converges faster and attains higher accuracy than existing benchmarks in most i.i.d. and non-i.i.d. setups under diverse attacks, while its resilience is bounded by that of the underlying aggregation rule.
  </details>

- **[BrainPro: Process-conditioned Pretraining with Topographic Retrieval for Large EEG Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22050)**  `arXiv:2509.22050`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yi Ding, Muyun Jiang, Weibang Jiang, Shuailei Zhang, Xinliang Zhou, Chenyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EEG foundation models are pretrained on heterogeneous corpora, but two challenges remain underexplored: (1) experimental protocols provide mental-process cues that are rarely used in masked pretraining, and (2) electrode montages vary across datasets, making spatial representations montage-dependent. Existing methods typically learn a single general-purpose representation and model spatial structure implicitly, limiting their ability to exploit process-related information under montage heterogeneity. We introduce BrainPro, a process-conditioned self-supervised pretraining framework that couples topographic retrieval with shared and process-associated representation learning. BrainPro maps dataset-specific montages to a universal channel-region template and retrieves channel- and region-level spatial filters to form a topographically aligned spatial basis. Over this basis, BrainPro learns a shared encoder for general EEG structure and additional affective, motor-related, and auxiliary residual branches for process-associated variation. Protocol-derived mental-process cues condition branch activation and region-weighted masked reconstruction, incorporating topographic spatial priors and process information. Across nine public BCI benchmarks, BrainPro achieves strong performance among evaluated baselines. Ablations, channel-drop analysis, encoder-configuration studies, and spatial-filter visualizations suggest that topographic retrieval and process-conditioned representation learning jointly improve EEG decoding. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fast and Accurate Body Estimation in Pixels, World Space and Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25801)**  `arXiv:2611.25801`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Jinkun Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present FAB (Fast and Accurate Body estimation), a human body estimator that jointly improves inference efficiency and body-recovery fidelity. Rather than predicting only parametric mesh-model parameters, FAB directly predicts human skeleton joints and dense surface points. In images, it predicts dense 2D and 3D body points alongside a parametric Momentum Human Rig (MHR) head, then fits SMPL in one learned pass followed by two Gauss-Newton steps. Its ViT-L student, FAB-L, distilled from a fine-tuned SAM 3D Body, runs in real time for one person on an RTX 4070 Ti SUPER, from detection to a fitted body, at 4.6x the speed of SAM 3D Body's eager-PyTorch body-only path, while improving accuracy on most camera-space benchmarks. The same network predicts heel, toe, hand, and knee contacts, which connect the body to the scene. FAB also estimates world-space motion, fuses static-scene depth around the visible person, and aligns body and scene using predicted contacts. For human-to-humanoid real-to-sim transfer, an in-model G1 head and FAB-Retarget, a motion-level retargeter that reads dense points or the fitted mesh, map motion to a Unitree G1. FAB-Retarget produces references that follow the person more closely than specialized retargeters including SOMA, GMR, UMR, and OmniRetarget, and substantially more closely than Fast SAM 3D Body retargeted with our sequence IK. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[LoopedWAM: Looped World Action Models for Elastic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25595)**  `arXiv:2611.25595`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Anh Dao, Xinxin Liu, Hang Yu, Jeffrey A Chan Santiago, Yuzhang Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.
  </details>

- **[Mesh-Free Keypoint Learning for Category-Level 6D Object Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25513)**  `arXiv:2611.25513`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Junghoon Seo, Hakjin Lee, Jaehoon Sim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Category-level 6D pose estimation relates partial RGB-D observations of unseen instances to a canonical frame shared across a category. Object-aware reconstruction helps adaptive keypoints retain the geometry of the visible surface. Common reconstruction targets select observed points by their distance to a registered instance surface. We present MORSEPose, which derives this support directly from standard pose and metric-size annotations. We map observed 3D points to normalized object coordinates and retain those inside a tolerancedilated pose-and-size envelope. This construction is consistent across camera frames and metric scales. When the annotated extent bounds the object surface, the envelope conservatively contains the support of a conventional surface-distance selector. We integrate this target with multi-level RGB-D features, adaptive keypoints, and learnable category context. Experiments on REAL275 and HouseCat6D show that the envelope closely matches surface-based supervision at both point and task levels, remains stable under boundary perturbations and viewpoint changes, and supports competitive category-level pose accuracy across benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Phantom Success, Silent Failure: Auditing the Success Checkers of Simulated Manipulation Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25179)**  `arXiv:2611.25179`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zirui Song, Jiadong Zhang, Zheng Lu, Xiaosong Ma, Xiaojun Chang, Xiuying Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulated manipulation benchmarks measure policy performance using hand-written success checkers that label each episode as a success or failure. Passing an expert demonstration shows that a checker accepts one way of completing the task. Learned policies, however, may stop after partial progress or complete the task in a different way. A checker may then accept an incomplete execution (phantom success) or reject an execution that satisfies the instruction (silent failure). In this paper, we introduce SimAudit, an LLM-based pipeline that audits success checkers against task instructions. SimAudit analyzes the instruction and checker code to identify scenarios where the two may disagree, recreates those scenarios in simulation, and runs the original checker. Multi-view renderings are then used to verify whether the observed behavior satisfies the instruction. Across RoboTwin 2.0, RoboCasa365, and RoboDojo, 183 simulated test cases produced the predicted checker verdict. Visual verification and deduplication yielded 61 distinct defects across 49 tasks, including 40 phantom successes and 21 silent failures. Most defects in RoboCasa365 accepted incomplete executions, most in RoboDojo rejected valid executions, and both error types were common in RoboTwin 2.0. These findings suggest that validating success checkers requires testing both incomplete executions and valid alternatives to expert demonstrations, and motivate more rigorous evaluation protocols for future benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ReactWorld: Structuring Interaction Responses for Embodied Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06120)**  `arXiv:2611.06120`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Hanwei Liu, Huijia Zhang, Hanzhang Wang, Guangyu Liu, Hanbei Zhan, Linjie Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied video world models predict scene changes caused by robot actions, yet generated object responses may remain inconsistent with task intent and contact dynamics. Critical responses in embodied scenes often concentrate in local regions and specific time intervals. Inspired by chain-of-thought reasoning, we introduce ReactWorld, an embodied video generation model guided by structured interaction anticipation. In the generation backbone's feature space, an interaction response field predictor anticipates how the interaction will unfold, explicitly describing participant roles, future motion response intensity, and local motion changes. A response feedback adapter forms increments to object features from the difference between the object branch's outputs with predicted and zeroed local dynamics inputs. It also converts predicted displacements into positional biases for temporal attention. These two mechanisms guide object feature updates and information retrieval across frames, respectively. Through interaction anticipation and generative feedback, ReactWorld achieves an average score of 77.51 on RBench, outperforming PhysisForcing by 3.29 points with improvements in all nine categories. On EZS-Bench, it achieves a Domain score of 86.98 and an average score of 81.87.
  </details>

- **[Curvature-aware 3D length estimation of greenhouse cucumbers using RGB-D imaging and cubic spline arc-length integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22439)**  `arXiv:2606.22439`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Manveen Kaur, Rajmeet Singh, Saeed Mozaffri, Majid Ahmadi, Shahpour Alirezaee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Commercial greenhouse cucumber production is graded by fruit length, which drives harvest scheduling, labour allocation, and logistics. Manual measurement with thread or caliper is accurate but infeasible at commercial scale. This paper presents CucumberVision, a non-contact length estimation framework using an Intel RealSense D435 RGB-D camera. A YOLO26n instance segmentation model locates cucumbers, and SAM (ViT-B backbone) refines each detection to a pixel-precise mask. Five methods are evaluated under matched conditions: (M1) a dominant-axis skeleton scan-line baseline; (M2) PCA on the bounding-box depth point cloud; (M3) SAM mask with medial-axis skeletonisation; (M4) a hybrid keypoint-guided approach using a YOLO26-pose model predicting five anatomical landmarks (KP0--KP4) with piecewise 3D arc-length; and (M5) a novel medial arc spline method fitting a cubic spline through the 3D medial axis of the SAM mask and computing arc length by trapezoidal integration -- the first such application to elongated vegetable measurement. All methods share five-frame burst depth averaging, colour-stream intrinsic alignment, and adaptive method selection with cascading fallbacks ensuring 100% coverage. A benchmark of 48 captures across seven cucumbers in three size categories (small ~8 cm, medium ~13 cm, large ~25 cm) with thread-based ground truth establishes a significant accuracy hierarchy: M1 (MAPE 9.68%) > M2 (5.31%) > M4 (5.51%) > M3 (5.82%) > M5 (4.13%). M5 significantly outperforms all competitors at Bonferroni-corrected alpha=0.0125. A secondary contribution is identifying a 12--18% length underestimation caused by using depth-stream rather than colour-stream intrinsics afterthis http URL(this http URL) -- an under-reported error source. The complete system is released open source and runs in real time on a single consumer-grade GPU.
  </details>

- **[Backbone Matters: Benchmarking Encoder-Decoder Networks for Semantic Segmentation of Driving Scenes on BDD100K](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26191)**  `arXiv:2611.26191`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ehsan Rassekh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic segmentation gives an autonomous vehicle a dense, pixel-level description of its surroundings and is a core part of scene understanding. Most segmentation networks consist of an encoder (the backbone) and a decoder, yet the backbone is often chosen without a systematic comparison. This work studies this choice on the BDD100K driving dataset. Four widely used decoders (U-Net, FPN, LinkNet and PSPNet) are combined with 13 backbones from six families (ResNet, VGG, DenseNet, Inception, MobileNet and EfficientNet), and all 52 models are trained with one common protocol. The best model, an FPN decoder with an EfficientNet-B4 backbone, reaches 62.6% mean intersection over union (mIoU), 89.8% accuracy and a loss of 0.092. Only two of the 52 models, both FPN with EfficientNet, exceed the 56.9% mIoU reported with the BDD100K benchmark. The effect of the backbone depends strongly on the decoder: the same EfficientNet-B4 gives 42.6% mIoU with PSPNet but 62.6% with FPN, and the gap between the best and the worst backbone ranges from 12.0 points (U-Net) to 42.5 points (FPN). The results and the scope of this study are discussed.
  </details>

- **[AdaptReg: Skip-level FiLM Adapter for Task-Specific Adaptation of a Frozen Registration Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25761)**  `arXiv:2611.25761`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shuyue Shi, Rong Yuan, Lin Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Longitudinal PSMA-PET/CT registration is a key step in assessing treatment response in prostate cancer, and it requires more than generic alignment accuracy: the deformation must preserve lesion volume, so that measured changes in metabolic tumor volume (MTV) and total lesion glycolysis (TLG) reflect treatment response rather than registration artifacts. We propose AdaptReg, which adapts a frozen registration foundation model to such task-specific requirements through a lightweight trainable adapter branch with identity-preserving initialization. Keeping uniGradICON fully frozen, we apply FiLM-style per-voxel affine modulation ($\gamma$, $\beta$) to its decoder skip connections; the modulation is generated from anatomical semantic features of a frozen TotalSegmentator encoder and supervised by a lesion volume-preservation loss. On the official Codabench validation set (20 pairs), AdaptReg achieves DSC 0.7585, HD95 6.33 mm, MTV/TLG errors of 1.68%/2.11%, and 0.0022% non-diffeomorphic voxels, improving on the uniGradICON + instance optimization baseline by $+0.05$ DSC and reducing the MTV/TLG errors by 70.9%/67.7%. On the hidden official test set (160 pairs), AdaptReg achieves CT DSC 0.816, PET DSC 0.598, and MTV/TLG errors of 4.02%/3.46%, ranking fourth among 16 teams in the final PSMAReg evaluation. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[What One Sample Cannot See: Why Fewer Denoising Steps Can Score Higher on Physics Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25632)**  `arXiv:2611.25632`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yutao Li, Chen Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a storm can head north or south, a forecast down the middle may score better under squared error than either plausible path. The same paradox appears when video generators are tested as world models: fewer denoising steps can raise physics scores even as videos blur or stop moving early. Why? Physics-IQ compares each generated video with one recording, although a scene can have multiple valid futures. An exact decomposition shows its scores can reward reduced variation among generated futures, even when their distribution becomes less accurate. We introduce Kernel Physics-IQ to remove this incentive in expectation, needing only two independent samples per condition. Two-step sampling lowers Kernel Physics-IQ in all five image-to-video models. For CogVideoX, the official score rises by 0.055 while ours falls by 0.131. Changing the sampling schedule reveals further differences official scores miss. In controlled physical systems with known outcome probabilities, our metric favors the true distribution over collapsed predictions. In short, a world model should be judged not only by how closely one video matches what happened, but by how faithfully its predictions represent what could have happened.
  </details>

- **[FlowMap-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Few-Step Flow-Map Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37851)**  `arXiv:2609.37851`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiqi Li, Cheng Wan, Bo Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step flow-map generators, such as MeanFlow and consistency models, sample in a few long-range jumps. On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on states drawn from its own sampler, but existing methods compare teacher and student through the local stochastic transitions of a multi-step sampler, which a flow-map student never deploys. We present FlowMap-OPD, the first systematic study of OPD for flow-map students. It separates state collection from supervision: the student collects states with its native few-step sampler, while the teacher supervises the flow map, velocities induced from it, or the instantaneous velocity linked to the map by a separately weighted consistency penalty. We find that instantaneous-velocity supervision with a small consistency weight, our chosen form, transfers the teacher's gains best, whereas supervising the deployed map, although the natural choice, transfers them worst when teacher and student start from different pretrained models. This gives a counter-intuitive rule: \emph{sample with the flow map, learn the velocity}. As a further benefit, this form applies to any teacher that defines a velocity, including multi-step ones, and to students initialized from multi-step models. On FLUX and SD3.5, a single few-step student consolidates three specialist teachers within 300 updates, matches or exceeds each on its own benchmark (e.g., OCR 0.883 versus 0.850 on FLUX), and outperforms both multi-reward reinforcement learning and LoRA merging.
  </details>

- **[Visual Jev: Accurate and Efficient Decisions from Shared Visual Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25845)**  `arXiv:2609.25845`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Guanxu Yu, Yuhang Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Applications increasingly ask multiple closed-choice questions about the same image, requiring accurate probabilities and efficient inference. We present Visual Jev, a visual decision model combining LM-head candidate readout, answer-supervised post-training, and independent question branches over a shared visual prefix. On held-out test images from four benchmarks, task SFT raises the 4B model's macro accuracy from 83.4% to 88.5% and error-discrimination AUROC from 0.834 to 0.890. Its macro accuracy exceeds the hosted Jev and OpenAI Decisions APIs by 2.77 and 5.06 percentage points, respectively, with the lowest NLL. We further show that better accuracy and calibration do not ensure reliable decisions. Fixed confidence thresholds exceed target error rates, and simply reordering options changes 17% of the model's accept-or-review decisions on SNLI-VE. At 32 questions per image, shared-prefix inference reduces amortized time per question from 20.5 to 5.6 ms, a 3.7x speedup over batched full sequences on one RTX 5090. Engine-level prefix caching reduces KV memory to 24.3% of that used by our custom shared-prefix path.
  </details>

- **[What Does a Temporal Benchmark Score Measure? Decomposing Channel Use in Video VLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12304)**  `arXiv:2607.12304`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Farrukh Rahman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A score on a temporal video question answering benchmark is meant to measure that a model has temporal understanding, but it conflates two questions. (1) is the question even temporal, does it need several frames and their order? And (2) when it does, does the model recover the order from the pixels, or read it off the positional encoding (RoPE)? Most of a temporal score answers neither, since a single frame and answer priors often carry it, and the field's validity checks, frame-shuffle sensitivity and multi-frame gain, speak only to (1). For (2) we score a reversal conflict, frames reversed while RoPE stays forward, two ways: label-free, as the accuracy lost against the forward labels, and against reversed labels wherever labels transform deterministically under time reversal. This separates a model that follows the reversed content from one merely disrupted. The two readings dissociate two similarly scoring models at both model scales. Qwen3-VL answers the reversed event it actually sees, reading the visual sequence. Molmo2 correctly reports a backwards video when positions agree, and flipping only the RoPE indices flips its prediction to the forward events, i.e. it reads positions. We call these regimes visual-sequence-dominant and position-dominant. Controls show reversed content is processable whenever the channels agree, and that both families share a bias toward describing events as running forward (arrow-of-time prior) that, being similar in strength, cannot by itself account for the dissociation. The distinction matters because the two channels fail on opposite inputs: two models with the same score are not interchangeable.
  </details>

- **[BADAS-3.0: Real-Time Driving Perception and Planning from One Camera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26294)**  `arXiv:2611.26294`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Roni Goldshmidt, Hamish Scott, Lorenzo Niccolini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We ask how far a driving model can get with one camera and real-time latency. BADAS-3.0 is a 1B-parameter video encoder with four small heads. From two seconds of one forward camera, it predicts the current speed, the metric path just driven, a collision-risk score and six candidate futures over 6.4 seconds, in under 50 ms. Given the car's current speed, BADAS-3.0 has 44% lower minADE$_6$ over five trajectory benchmarks than the 34B Alpamayo 2 Super, which reads six cameras and runs over 100x slower. The gain comes from camera rigs that neither model was trained on. On the rig they share, the two are on par. Existing benchmarks are mostly nominal driving, so we add two open-loop safety benchmarks, one of real dashcam collisions and near-misses and one of Euro NCAP scenarios. On both, BADAS-3.0 has a higher pass rate than every published planner we tested.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Think with Images Faithfully via Adaptive Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26199)**  `arXiv:2611.26199`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yu Zhao, Jiaxin Zhang, Rohit Saxena, Fazl Barez, Pasquale Minervini, Yiwen Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thinking with images (TwI) empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to overcome perceptual bottlenecks by actively cropping and zooming into fine-grained visual details, promising both higher accuracy and interpretable, region-grounded reasoning. However, our analysis shows that the improved accuracy of TwI models does not guarantee faithful tool use; they frequently invoke tools without actually relying on the zoomed region, undermining the reliability of tool-assisted reasoning. We trace this unfaithful behaviour to the training data: when questions are answerable without zooming, the model can answer correctly while ignoring the returned evidence, and this problem is exacerbated during training as the model becomes better at answering without tools. To keep zooming useful throughout training, we adapt the training data to the model's evolving capability and train on questions where correct zoomed evidence can still help the model answer correctly. This selection is supported by a data synthesis pipeline that localises small objects and constructs verifiable questions about their fine details, and complemented by an adaptiveness reward that favours tool-free answers when zooming is unnecessary. The experimental results show that our method substantially improves reasoning faithfulness and achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual search benchmarks (+11.6% on V*, +7.9% on HR-Bench 4K, and +9.4% on HR-Bench 8K). On multimodal hallucination benchmarks, our model also outperforms the compared TwI baselines. These results suggest that improving TwI faithfulness does not sacrifice accuracy and that both can be learnt from the right training data without directly supervising the reasoning process.
  </details>

- **[What Does Anti-Hallucination Training Buy? Saying Less, Cheap Pruning, and the Rest](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25814)**  `arXiv:2611.25814`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Lingkai Bu, Qian Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference training is the standard recipe for reducing object hallucination in vision-language models, and each new method reports a lower CHAIR score. We ask what that reduction consists of. Every trained system is compared with its base model's caption truncated to the same object recall, which removes the part of the gain that comes from saying less; the remainder is compared with what a deliberately weak, frozen, eleven-feature post-hoc probe fitted on 500 annotated images can reproduce. All criteria were specified in the analysis code and frozen before the outputs they judge existed. For eight public methods on LLaVA-1.5-7B, the probe reproduces at least two thirds of what training removes beyond saying less for 7 of 8 methods on COCO, 8 of 8 on AMBER and 6 of 8 on Object HalBench, with RLAIF-V the consistent exception; a later public method released for both LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen2.5-VL (SENTINEL) meets the same bar on both. On our own DPO checkpoints the accounting holds on Qwen2.5-VL but fails on InternVL2.5 on COCO (2 of 4 training designs). At matched recall, the base model plus the probe beats truncation for 8 of 9 LLaVA-1.5 systems on COCO and 9 of 9 on AMBER, on the two newer backbones, and when realized as actual sentence deletion. The same measure prices training signals: DPO on the model's own samples labelled by the probe or by an object-level CLIP score recovers 0.70 and 0.87 of the beyond-brevity gain of ground-truth-labelled DPO on Qwen2.5-VL (the probe 0.73 on InternVL2.5), both beat length-matched random labels, and CLIP labels buy more than probe labels on COCO. Human judgements show that the sentences the probe deletes are wrong more often than those truncation would delete; a human check of the CHAIR evaluator's precision falls below our pre-set bar, which we report as a limitation.
  </details>

- **[SEAF: Selective Semantic Enrichment and Visual Evidence Fusion for Multimodal Sarcasm Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25672)**  `arXiv:2611.25672`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Faxian Wan, Xiaocui Yang, Yifan Cao, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal sarcasm detection requires textual, visual, and contextual evidence, particularly when multiple images provide complementary cues. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate auxiliary information from a post's text and images, but their outputs may mix useful background knowledge with redundant visual descriptions. We propose SEAF, a dual-branch framework for selective semantic enrichment and visual evidence fusion. SEAF assigns generated fields to distinct roles: entity background enriches semantic input, while image descriptions supply alignment statistics. The semantic branch combines the original post text, background from a frozen MLLM, and optical character recognition (OCR) text. The aligned visual branch combines global and local visual features with statistics of cross-modal and inter-image similarity. Text-Anchored Smoothed Aggregation (TASA) weights images by their similarity to the original post text while preserving contributions from images with low literal similarity. Summing the branch classification scores integrates contextual semantics with visual evidence. Experiments on the multi-image MMSD3.0 benchmark show that SEAF outperforms all compared methods.
  </details>

- **[GroundingPI: A Grounding Foundation Model towards Physical Intelligence with Visual Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601)**  `arXiv:2609.39601`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Boyu Chen, Jiaqi Liang, Xini Ding, Yue Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise grounding matters. It specifies which object is the target and where that object is, even in clutter and for tiny objects, and it has to be fast enough for closed-loop control. Yet vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models (WAMs) take perception from general-purpose vision-language and video-generation backbones, which still fail in these settings. We introduce GroundingPI, a 4B grounding foundation model that generates points and boxes as quantized coordinates in a shared vocabulary. Training combines multimodal and spatial pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning with GRPO, using supervision from public datasets and dedicated data engines. Against 44 baselines across 34 grounding benchmarks spanning 11 perceptual capabilities, GroundingPI establishes a new state of the art, averaging 73.68%, above the larger GPT-6 Astra (71.54%). As a downstream visual backbone, GroundingPI improves performance on robotic manipulation and autonomous driving. Across RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboCasa-GR1, it outperforms all evaluated backbones in all four out-of-distribution settings, with relative gains of up to 24.8% over the strongest baseline. On RoboCasa-GR1, GroundingPI trained with 50% of the demonstrations outperforms those baselines trained with 75%. Its mean open-loop L2 error on nuScenes is 0.296 m. We systematically analyze GroundingPI's pretraining in scale and data composition. Grounding quality and downstream manipulation improve with pretraining scale, with generalization tracking perceptual gains most closely. Analyzing the data recipe across these 11 perceptual capabilities shows dense grounding's substantial benefits for both, and OCR's potential as a catalyst for perceptual learning. These results support grounding as a perceptual foundation, and dedicated perceptual pretraining as a promising direction for foundation models of physical intelligence.
  </details>

- **[GroundAnything: Reconciling Parallel Decoding with Precise Visual Grounding at Flash Speed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39600)**  `arXiv:2609.39600`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Bowen Ping, Xini Ding, Zetian Song, Junbo Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) grounding models serialize spatial predictions, introducing sequential latency and imposing a causal order on output tokens. We view grounding as visual evidence extraction: objects, locations, and spatial relations are jointly constrained by the image and query, yet their dependencies do not imply an intrinsic left-to-right generation order. This distinction makes bidirectional diffusion a natural fit, allowing spatial hypotheses to emerge in parallel and be jointly refined through iterative denoising. We introduce GroundAnything, a 4B-parameter grounding foundation model that reconciles fast parallel decoding with precise localization through blockwise denoising. Training combines grounding pretraining from public datasets and dedicated data engines, direct AR-to-diffusion conversion with joint AR and diffusion objectives, supervised fine-tuning, and GRPO-based reinforcement post-training. Across 30 grounding benchmarks, our autoregressive variant, GroundAnything-VLM, establishes a new overall state of the art among similarly sized models at 72.42%, remaining competitive with GPT-6 Astra (71.35%). With entropy-guided decoding, GroundAnything also surpasses the prior state of the art at this scale, averaging 61.75% versus 53.32% for the fast MTP-based LocateAnything model.
