We further explore decoding strategies, showing that an optional self-speculative mode achieves a 4.51$\times$ speedup over the AR counterpart with a 0.74 percentage-point drop in COCO F1mIoU. Infrastructure experiments show that progressive inference optimizations translate parallel decoding into practical speedups. These gains support efficient visual grounding in latency-sensitive real-world systems.
  </details>

- **[Paint-Anything: Unified Any-Color Control for Image Generation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20816)**  `arXiv:2609.20816`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ji Xie, Dewei Zhou, Xinyu Huang, Zhennan Chen, Xun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Professional design requires any-color control: the ability to specify an object's target color with any 24-bit hex value for image generation and editing. Prior work has explored color generation, editing, and colorization, but often relies on dedicated color representations or specialized inference procedures. Advances in large language models offer a simpler starting point: even compact models can associate hex values with color semantics. We present Paint-Anything, which learns a shared hex-prompt interface for generation and editing through object-level color supervision. We develop a data pipeline that constructs Paint-500K from real images through object grounding, perceptual color labeling, and editing-pair synthesis. Since shadows make real-image labels only approximate colors, we complement this supervision with pure-color anchors whose pixels exactly match their paired hex values. These anchors are used only at high-noise timesteps, leaving low-noise training to natural images. We further introduce Any Color Benchmark (ACBench), comprising ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit, to measure object-level hex color fidelity across both tasks. On FLUX.2-4B, Paint-Anything improves ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit scores by 85.3% and 28.3%, respectively, relative to the base model, with ablations supporting the training recipe. It also achieves the highest average CompColor score among the compared methods.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Visual Fidelity: Benchmarking Super-Resolution Models for Large-Scale Remote Sensing Imagery via Downstream Task Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00310)**  `arXiv:2605.00310`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhili Li, Kangyang Chai, Zhihao Wang, Xiaowei Jia, Yanhua Li, Gengchen Mai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Super-resolution (SR) techniques have made major advances in reconstructing high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs. The increased resolution provides visual enhancement and utility for monitoring tasks. In particular, SR has been increasingly developed for satellite-based Earth observation, with applications in urban planning, agriculture, ecology, and disaster response. However, existing SR studies and benchmarks typically use fidelity metrics such as PSNR or SSIM, whereas the true utility of super-resolved images lies in supporting downstream tasks such as land cover classification, biomass estimation, and change detection. To bridge this gap, we introduce GeoSR-Bench, a downstream task-integrated SR benchmark dataset to evaluate SR models beyond fidelity metrics. GeoSR-Bench comprises spatially co-located, temporally aligned, and quality-controlled image pairs from about 36,000 locations across diverse land covers, spanning resolutions from 500m to 0.6m. To the best of our knowledge, GeoSR-Bench is the first SR benchmark that directly connects improved image resolution from SR models with downstream Earth monitoring tasks, including land cover segmentation, infrastructure mapping, and biophysical variable estimation. Using GeoSR-Bench, we benchmark GAN, transformer, neural operator, and diffusion-based SR models on perceptual quality and downstream task performance. We conduct experiments with 270 settings, covering 2 cross-platform SR tasks, 9 SR models, 3 downstream task models, and 5 downstream tasks for each SR task. The results show that improvements in traditional SR metrics often do not correlate with gains in task performance, and the correlations can be negative, indicating that these metrics provide limited guidance for selecting superior models for downstream tasks. This reveals the need to integrate downstream tasks into SR model development and evaluation.
  </details>

- **[λSplit: Self-Supervised Content-Aware Spectral Unmixing for Fluorescence Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23647)**  `arXiv:2603.23647`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Federico Carrara, Talley Lambert, Mehdi Seifi, Florian Jug_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In fluorescence microscopy, spectral unmixing aims to recover individual fluorophore concentrations from spectral images that capture mixed fluorophore emissions. Since classical methods operate pixel-wise and rely on least-squares fitting, their performance degrades with increasingly overlapping emission spectra and higher levels of noise, suggesting that a data-driven approach that can learn and utilize a structural prior might lead to improved results. Learning-based approaches for spectral imaging do exist, but they are either not optimized for microscopy data or are developed for very specific cases that are not applicable to fluorescence microscopy settings. To address this, we propose {\lambda}Split, a physics-informed deep generative model that learns a conditional distribution over concentration maps using a hierarchical Variational Autoencoder. A fully differentiable Spectral Mixer enforces consistency with the image formation process, while the learned structural priors enable state-of-the-art unmixing and implicit noise removal. We demonstrate {\lambda}Split on 3 real-world datasets that we synthetically cast into a total of 66 challenging spectral unmixing benchmarks. We compare our results against a total of 10 baseline methods, including classical methods and a range of learning-based methods. Our results consistently show competitive performance and improved robustness in high noise regimes, when spectra overlap considerably, or when the spectral dimensionality is lowered, making {\lambda}Split a new state-of-the-art for spectral unmixing of fluorescent microscopy data. Importantly, {\lambda}Split is compatible with spectral data produced by standard confocal microscopes, enabling immediate adoption without specialized hardware modifications.
  </details>

- **[When and How Much to Imagine: Adaptive Test-Time Scaling with World Models for Visual Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08236)**  `arXiv:2602.08236`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shoubin Yu, Yue Zhang, Zun Wang, Jaehong Yoon, Huaxiu Yao, Mingyu Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid progress in MLLMs, visual spatial reasoning remains unreliable when correct answers depend on how a scene would appear under unseen or alternative viewpoints. Recent work addresses this by augmenting reasoning with world models for visual imagination, but questions such as when imagination is actually necessary, how much of it is beneficial, and when it becomes harmful, remain poorly understood. In practice, indiscriminate imagination can increase computation and even degrade performance by introducing misleading evidence. In this work, we present an in-depth analysis of test-time visual imagination as a controllable resource for spatial reasoning. We first study when static visual evidence is sufficient, when imagination improves reasoning, and how excessive or unnecessary imagination affects accuracy and efficiency. To support this analysis, we then introduce AVIC, an adaptive test-time framework with world models that explicitly reasons about the sufficiency of current visual evidence before selectively invoking and scaling visual imagination. Finally, to further learn this gating and planning behavior without any annotation of when and how much to imagine, we introduce AVIC-R, which trains the policy via GRPO from QA-correctness rewards and penalties by imagination cost. Across spatial reasoning benchmarks (SAT, MMSI) and an embodied navigation benchmark (R2R), our results reveal clear scenarios where imagination is critical, marginal, or detrimental, and show that selective control can match or outperform fixed imagination strategies with substantially fewer world-model calls and language tokens. Our AVIC-R surpasses strong proprietary baselines including GPT-4o and GPT-4.1 while invoking the world model less often. Overall, our findings highlight the importance of analyzing and controlling test-time imagination for efficient and reliable spatial reasoning.
  </details>

- **[AURA: AUdio-dRiven streaming Avatar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26540)**  `arXiv:2611.26540`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Nathaniel Cohen, Nicolas Dufour, Amélie Royer, Alasdair Newson, Patrick Pérez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current audio-driven video diffusion models can synthesize realistic talking-head videos but generally require dozens of diffusion denoising steps over multi-second video chunks, making them unsuitable for streaming applications such as voice assistants and virtual avatars, where low-latency and online causal generation are required properties. AURA generates video chunk by chunk with a rolling KV cache, requiring only the audio of the current chunk to start generating, rather than a multi-second audio look-ahead like bidirectional models. It comes in two sizes: AURA 1.3B streams 576p video in real time on a single GPU (20.7 fps for a 16 Hz playback rate), making it suited to interactive avatar applications, and AURA 5B streams 720p video at 9.7 fps. Our two-stage distillation pipeline first aligns the causal student with the teacher, by training it to match the teacher's trajectories with fewer diffusion steps and a chunk-wise sliding window causal attention mask, then applies Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) with backward simulation to close the remaining quality gap. On standard talking-head benchmarks, AURA 1.3B runs 23x faster than its own bidirectional teacher and reaches a quality comparable to TalkVerse at a lower resolution, and AURA 5B matches its TalkVerse teacher at 720p while running 17x faster. To support reproducibility and further research on streaming avatar generation, we release the weights of both models along with a dataset of 30K synthetic videos and their precomputed teacher trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Asuka-FM: A System-Architectural Co-Design Framework for Efficient Text-to-Image Pretraining on Consumer Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26437)**  `arXiv:2611.26437`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Víctor Rodrigo González, Pablo M. Olmos, Ignacio Peis, Alejandro Lancho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier text-to-image (T2I) foundation models predominantly rely on multi-billion parameter Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) pretrained across massive compute clusters, creating an accessibility barrier for academic laboratories. When training DiTs from scratch on single consumer GPUs (e.g., an NVIDIA RTX 4090 with 24\,GB VRAM), practitioners encounter three primary bottlenecks: quadratic attention sequence costs, Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation graph breaks induced by variable aspect-ratio bucketing, and an autoencoder throughput wall where frozen Variational Autoencoder (VAE) operations dominate step latency. In this work, we introduce \textbf{Asuka-FM}, an end-to-end system and architectural framework demonstrating that competitive dual-stream DiTs ($\sim$206M parameters) can be pretrained from scratch on a single consumer GPU in $\sim$160 GPU hours for under \$55. Asuka-FM integrates four core designs: (i) an AdaLN-free dual-stream DiT backbone reducing parameter count; (ii) structured sequence sparsification via Sparse-Dense Residual Fusion (SPRINT) to cut attention FLOPs by over 50\%; (iii) continuous aspect-normalized Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) coupled with shifted square crop training, guaranteeing static tensor shapes while unlocking zero-shot virtual camera control; and (iv) a pixel adaptation phase utilizing a lightweight (10M parameter) Patch Detailer Head operating under velocity regression ($v$-prediction). Evaluated on open-domain benchmarks without benchmark-specific fine-tuning, Asuka-FM achieves GenEval 0.588 / DPG 78.96 scores at 256px and GenEval 0.609 / DPG 78.51 scores at 512px, setting a new Pareto-optimal efficiency standard for democratic foundation modeling.
  </details>

- **[Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26334)**  `arXiv:2611.26334`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ryan Lucas, Petros Prastakos, Rahul Mazumder_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video diffusion transformers reuse key--value (KV) caches across denoising steps, which quickly becomes a bottleneck for long video generation. We find that quantizing temporally distant cache vectors leads to a large range and high quantization error, but that nearby video tokens in the KV-cache share a persistent pattern per channel. These patterns also vary across videos, motivating rotations that are adapted to each video's unique cache. We propose Temporal Rotation Quantization (TRQ), a method that rotates frames in the cache that are adjacent in time before quantization. For each block of temporally adjacent cache vectors, TRQ fits an orthogonal transform that maps the block's per-channel means to a vector that is close to a constant across channels. To keep the quantization format lightweight, we parameterize this rotation as a tree of $2 \times 2$ planar rotations, each stored with only two bits, followed by a fixed Hadamard transform shared across all blocks. The rotations are fit without gradients or changes to model weights, and we prove that a simple greedy fit is globally optimal for our 'mean-flattening' objective. The transforms are fit online for each new video, and we release fused Triton kernels to make this process efficient at inference. At 2-bit precision and matched compression, TRQ improves VBench subject consistency over the state-of-the-art method QVG from 90.38 to 92.02 on LongCat-Video-13B and from 86.33 to 87.61 on HY-WorldPlay-8B, as well as improving other metrics.
  </details>

- **[Multi-View Transformer with Geometry-Guided Multi-Modal ControlNet for 3D Virtual Try-On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25979)**  `arXiv:2611.25979`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sarthak Batra, Armin Mustafa, Adrian Hilton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Editing a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) capture of a person so that they wear a new garment requires every rendered view to agree on the same garment details. Existing 3D virtual try-on (VTON) pipelines, however, edit training views largely independently and fuse them with hand-designed heuristics such as manually chosen reference views and fixed attention-blending weights. The resulting inconsistencies are baked into the 3D representation and surface as texture drift, blurred prints, and implausible geometry in novel views. We present GS-VTON-MVT, a three-stage framework that combines implicit and explicit 3D reasoning for consistent 3DGS try-on. (i) A multi-view inpainting transformer jointly edits a set of training views with full cross-view self-attention, so that cross-view correspondence is learned rather than imposed. (ii) A geometry-guided multi-modal ControlNet conditions the diffusion editor on the garment image together with depth and surface normals rendered from the current 3DGS state, supplying explicit structure where visual evidence is weakest. (iii) A pose-adaptive persona-aware attention replaces the fixed blending weight between current-view and reference-view features with weights derived from camera-pose proximity. On the 3D-VTONBench benchmark, GS-VTON-MVT improves FID, KID, LPIPS-based cross-view consistency, and user preference over general-purpose 3D editors and prior 3D try-on methods.
  </details>

- **[Harnessing Medical Visual-Language Models with Agentic Evidence Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25840)**  `arXiv:2611.25840`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiming Huang, Hanze Dong, Jipeng Zhang, Mike Zheng Shou, Xinxing Xu, Furu Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.
  </details>

- **[SAM 3 Sees More Than It Detects: Promptable Concept Maps Beyond Categories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25747)**  `arXiv:2611.25747`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yu-Huan Wu, Yun Liu, Liangli Zhen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Promptable segmenters answer what a thing is. Dense prediction tasks also ask class-agnostic questions, such as what stands out or hides, how light is blocked, transmitted or reflected, and where structure lies. Robots, surveillance systems and image editors build on their answers. We study promptable concept maps (PCM), which extend SAM 3's promptable concept segmentation from noun instances to maps of concepts named by short noun phrases such as `"transparent object", with "none" as an answer. PCM-Bench groups the standard test sets of seven such tasks by the question they ask. On it, we find that SAM 3 sees more than it detects. Its semantic path responds to many of these concepts, yet a low image-level presence score can block every detection of its instance path. We present UNVEIL, which trains a copy of SAM 3's semantic path for these maps, with LoRA on copies of its last blocks. A separate presence decoder learns from verified negatives whether a concept occurs. With one text phrase per query, UNVEIL maps region concepts such as salient and camouflaged objects, shadows, transparent objects and mirrors. The same weights map thin structures such as edges and skeletons as well as object categories. Run next to SAM 3, it leaves SAM 3's own outputs unchanged. On each of the seven tasks, UNVEIL reaches the highest score among the compared promptable models, including SAM 3's official fine-tuning and pipelines built on multimodal LLMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Entity Recognition: Vision-Centric Knowledge Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25680)**  `arXiv:2611.25680`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiatong Ma, Yuchen Liu, Longteng Guo, Yichen Yuan, Yuze Wu, Dongze Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose VC-VQA, a large-scale benchmark for vision-centric knowledge reasoning, aiming to systematically evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition and complex multi-stage reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily treat visual information as entity recognition and rely on text-based external knowledge, VC-VQA explicitly models the role of visual information by considering both its source (internal vs. external images) and stage of involvement (pre-retrieval vs. post-retrieval). Specifically, VC-VQA requires models to retrieve external visual evidence, identify visual clues before retrieval, and localize, verify, or match retrieved knowledge against the internal image. We further construct a controlled multimodal knowledge base with traceable textual and visual evidence for each sample. We evaluate 21 representative MLLMs under four settings: no external knowledge, with provided evidence, knowledge base retrieval, and open-web search. Experimental results lead to three key findings: current models still struggle with vision-centric knowledge reasoning; fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition remains the primary bottleneck; and agentic retrieval highlights the value of multi-step planning and cross-modal knowledge integration. Overall, VC-VQA provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating vision-centric knowledge reasoning in MLLMs. Our code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Wolf in Sheep's Pixels: Visual Camouflage Attacks via HDR Gain Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25469)**  `arXiv:2611.25469`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Qi Shen, Yujia Gong, Yue Zhao, Tong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An image can pass automated safety checks while showing harmful content to its human viewer. We introduce DRJanus, a visual camouflage attack that exploits the gap between standard dynamic range (SDR) processing and high dynamic range (HDR) display. Automated systems often read only an image's SDR base, whereas HDR viewers reconstruct its appearance using an auxiliary gain map. DRJanus inverts this reconstruction to embed two independently chosen color images in a single JPEG, without querying or optimizing against the target model. It preserves the chosen SDR base exactly and reconstructs HDR targets at an average PSNR of 43.03 dB across 400 images. On a benchmark of 100 sexual-content images, DRJanus evades detection by all six tested commercial moderation services and nine vision-language models. Across five agents, each evaluated on 120 task cases per threat setting, attack success ranges from 31.67% to 100% for moderation evasion and from 95% to 100% for task manipulation. We also explore cross-view consistency checks as a mitigation. These findings expose a gap in visual trust: securing what a machine reads does not secure what a user sees. A demo is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Raptor: Rejection-aware Adaptive Tree based on Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25436)**  `arXiv:2611.25436`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Qishu Hu, Haozhao Wang, Zicong Hong, Yufeng Zhan, Yuanqing Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tree-structured speculative decoding accelerates vision-language models (VLMs) by verifying multiple candidate continuations in parallel. However, long visual contexts increase the cost of speculative computation, making candidate coverage and redundant expansion important considerations beyond tree topology adaptation. We propose Raptor, a training-free framework that uses rejection feedback to jointly regulate candidate coverage and drafting effort. Our key insight is that acceptance length alone cannot distinguish insufficient candidate coverage from excessive deep expansion, and these conditions require different allocation decisions. Raptor therefore extracts complementary rejection-aware signals to characterize speculative progress, frontier coverage, and redundant expansion. These signals jointly guide bounded, momentum-smoothed updates of tree depth, branching width, and drafting budget, together with execution control that limits unnecessary drafting and progressively restores expansion when appropriate. Extensive experiments with two VLMs across seven multimodal benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of Raptor, achieving up to 2.39x average speedup over autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning for Mammography Vision--Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25418)**  `arXiv:2611.25418`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengbo Zhou, Dooman Arefan, Shandong Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision--language models (VLMs) often need to identify small, subtle lesions within high-resolution images containing extensive normal anatomy and redundant visual information. Visual-token compression improves efficiency, but preserving salient regions alone can discard the context needed for diagnostic interpretation. This context can extend across images: in bilateral mammography, the opposite breast provides a patient-specific anatomical reference. Yet retaining such evidence does not ensure that the model uses it effectively. We propose Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning (BESR), which connects evidence preservation with comparative assessment through a bilateral-guided token selector and a bilateral reward. Under a shared token budget, the selector jointly prioritizes lesion appearance and its local and bilateral context, while the reward encourages assessments that benefit from the preserved comparison. Experiments on a bilateral dataset constructed from CBIS-DDSM and on VinDr-Mammo demonstrate improved evidence retention and diagnostic performance under constrained visual-token budgets. Inference benchmarking further shows reduced prefill latency.
  </details>

- **[Keel: A Frontier Model Teaches a Vision-Language Model by Rewriting Its Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25257)**  `arXiv:2611.25257`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Bangji Yang, Qinglong Zheng, Ziwen Wang, Chaoran Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a frontier model repair a vision-language model (VLM) by rewriting its weights? Small reasoning VLMs such as Qwen3.5 need such a repair: in thinking mode they fall into loops on many image questions, repeating themselves until the token limit, spending the whole budget and losing the answer. The usual remedies act on the decoding, leaving the looping model as it was, or must be told what to change. Yet the cause is written in the weights. Keel, the first agentic weight-editing method for VLMs on image tasks, finds and removes it there. Told only the symptom, an editor, a frontier multimodal LLM acting as an agent, locates the cause, edits the weights and tests them, with a failure census and instruments showing it the failing images and where the VLM attends; among several editors, the harness keeps only edits that clearly reduce the failure without lowering accuracy on questions no editor saw. The repair needs no training, keeps the model's size and per-token cost, and applies to already-edited weights. The editors expose where the loops live: from the original weights, independent editors on both models trace them to the attention outputs of the middle layers, and the smaller VLM stops consulting the image inside a loop. On Qwen3.5-0.8B and Qwen3.5-2B, across six image benchmarks from visual mathematics to GUI grounding, the edit removes most loops on every benchmark and shortens answers, and the edited models are significantly more accurate than the thinking models on average; the larger one gains most on GUI grounding, a task absent from the practice questions. Because the repair lives in the weights, it combines with budget forcing where held-out questions favor it: on the best available weights, Keel has the best average accuracy on both models, significantly above every training-free baseline we compare on the larger one.
  </details>

- **[VisAlloc: Allocating Visual Supervision for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25069)**  `arXiv:2611.25069`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Xu, Tian Chen, Yuhe Wu, Yanbo Zhao, Junchi Liao, Hongyu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers a promising approach to improving visual reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, not all samples benefit equally from privileged visual guidance, and uniform token supervision can dilute visually grounded signals. We propose VisAlloc, a dual-level framework combining sample-level visual filtering with token-level counterfactual reweighting. A fixed paired-teacher filter retains samples answered correctly from an evidence-centered positive crop but incorrectly from a spatially displaced negative crop. At the token level, both EMA teachers score the same student-generated response, and their absolute log-probability differences measure view sensitivity. Additional supervision targets tokens admitted by sensitivity or teacher-distribution disagreement, with shared frozen-prior, template-bigram, and student--teacher divergence gates. Inverse-frequency redistribution preserves the total additional weight within each micro-batch, while the positive teacher remains the sole distillation target. Inference requires only the full-image student. Across eight benchmarks, our 4B and 9B models achieve macro-averages of 81.44 and 84.92, outperforming Vision-OPD reproductions by 0.97 and 0.52 points, respectively. Code on GitHub:this https URL
  </details>

- **[2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12805)**  `arXiv:2611.12805`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave, Akanksha Narula, Marius Sebastian Shokoui, Alina Hafner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $\Phi$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.
  </details>

- **[How to "train" a surgeon? Benchmarking generalist agents in surgical scene understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11437)**  `arXiv:2611.11437`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gengyuan Zhang, Xiao Han, Shijie Zhou, Abdullah Koukash, Jingpei Wu, Jindong Gu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. SurgAgentGym evaluates whether generalist agents can improve surgical understanding at inference time through tool-mediated evidence use, without additional surgical training. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. This opens a path toward future studies of agentic surgical understanding.
  </details>

- **[AgroGround: What Grounding Fine-Tuning Changes in Agricultural Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04425)**  `arXiv:2610.04425`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Abdulla Alshehhi, Zongyan Han, Rao Muhammad Anwer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale agricultural grounding data can now be generated automatically, but how training on such data affects a vision-language model's localization, recognition, and refusal capabilities remains unclear. To study these effects, we introduce AgroGround, containing 794,850 automatically grounded agricultural instructions for training and a separate benchmark of 1,478 human-verified images for evaluation. Among these images, 500 are independently re-annotated, yielding an inter-annotator instance F1 of 0.789. We use controlled comparisons to isolate the effects of different training choices on localization, recognition, their joint correctness, and refusal. Grounding-only fine-tuning reduces four-way recognition from 51.7% to 29.2%, with predictions showing a strong candidate-position bias. However, changing only the instruction format raises recognition to 72.7% while maintaining similar grounding performance. Training with healthy-image negatives achieves 94.1% refusal on healthy images, but only 28.6% refusal of incorrect target names on positive images, showing that these two refusal behaviors do not necessarily improve together. Reinforcement learning reveals a further trade-off, improving localization by 0.014 F1 over a matched supervised control while reducing recognition and refusal. Across model families and held-out training sources, localization transfers more reliably than recognition and target-specific refusal. External benchmarks further show that the gains depend on the domain and task. Together, these findings show that grounding fine-tuning changes the balance between localization, recognition, and refusal rather than improving all three uniformly.
  </details>

- **[Towards Embodied Air-Ground Cooperative Object Search: Benchmark, Dataset and Agentic Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08402)**  `arXiv:2609.08402`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Boao Yu, Zimo Chen, Junreng Rao, Yue Hu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Yong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Air-Ground Object Search (AGOS) in urban environments is a challenging embodied task, which requires an Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) and an Unmanned Ground Vehicle (UGV) to jointly search for and verify a specified target vehicle from multi-view visual references. To study this underexplored problem, we introduce AGOS-Bench, the first dedicated benchmark for evaluating whether general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can integrate aerial discoveries and ground-level verification through UAV-UGV cooperation. We further provide AGOS-Dataset as the companion resource of exemplary trajectories constructed by an automatic pipeline. It consists of 7.7k episodes for searching objects of diverse categories and attributes, spanning three difficulty levels. To address the AGOS task, we propose AGOS-Agent, a training-free and tool-augmented approach. The agentic method relieves VLMs from complex and dynamic coordination via a deliberate search-handoff-verify cooperation protocol, only demanding VLMs for scene understanding and decision-making. Extensive experiments on nine VLMs show that AGOS-Agent improves overall success rate for eight of the nine evaluated backbones while reducing decision steps for all nine. On the hard split, the SR and SPL of Gemini-3.6-Flash increase from 8.6% to 55.7% and from 7.6% to 44.0%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Detecting AI-Generated Videos with Spiking Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05895)**  `arXiv:2605.05895`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Minsuk Jang, Yujin Yang, Hee-Seon Kim, Minseok Son, Younghun Kim, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern AI-generated videos are photorealistic at the single-frame level, leaving inter-frame dynamics as the main remaining axis for detection. Existing detectors typically handle this temporal evidence in three ways: feeding the full frame sequence to a generic temporal backbone, reducing one dominant temporal cue to fixed video-level descriptors, or comparing temporal features to real-video statistics through a detection metric. These strategies degrade sharply under cross-generator evaluation, where artifact type and timescale vary across generators. On caption-paired benchmark, GenVidBench, we identify two signatures that prior detectors do not jointly exploit: AI-generated videos exhibit smoother frame-to-frame temporal residuals at the pixel level, and more compact trajectories in the semantic feature space, indicating a temporal smoothness gap at both levels. We further observe that, when raw video is fed into a Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs), fake clips elicit firing predominantly at object and motion boundaries, unlike real clips, suggesting that the SNN responds to temporal artifacts localized at edges. These cues are sparse, asynchronous, and concentrated at moments of change, which makes SNNs a natural choice for this task: their event-driven, sparsely-activated dynamics align with the structure of the residual signal in a way that dense ANN backbones do not. Building on this observation, we propose MAST, a detector that processes multi-channel temporal residuals with a spike-driven temporal branch alongside a frozen semantic encoder for cross-generator generalization. On the GenVideo benchmark, MAST achieves 93.14\% mean accuracy across 10 unseen generators under strict cross-generator evaluation, matching or surpassing the strongest ANN-based detectors and demonstrating the practical applicability of SNNs to AI-generated video detection.
  </details>

- **[VLRS-Bench: A Vision-Language Reasoning Benchmark for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07045)**  `arXiv:2602.07045`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiming Luo, Hebaixu Wang, Haonan Guo, Jing Zhang, Bo Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have enabled complex reasoning. However, existing remote sensing (RS) benchmarks remain heavily biased toward perception tasks, such as object recognition and scene classification. This limitation hinders the development of MLLMs for cognitively demanding RS applications. To address this, we propose a Vision Language ReaSoning Benchmark (VLRS-Bench), which is the first benchmark exclusively dedicated to complex RS reasoning. Structured across the three core dimensions of Cognition, Decision, and Prediction, VLRS-Bench comprises 2,000 question-answer pairs with an average question length of 129.87 words, spanning 14 tasks and up to eight temporal phases. VLRS-Bench is constructed via a specialized pipeline that integrates RS-specific priors and expert knowledge to ensure geospatial realism and reasoning complexity. Experimental results reveal significant bottlenecks in existing state-of-the-art MLLMs, providing critical insights for advancing multimodal reasoning within the remote sensing community.
  </details>

- **[Risk-Controlled Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26438)**  `arXiv:2611.26438`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zixuan Liu, Hashem Haghbayan, Zizhan Zheng, Siavash Khajavi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented vision-language-action (VLA) models enable efficient test-time adaptation by leveraging expert demonstrations as additional context without updating model parameters. However, even when retrieved demonstrations are relevant to the current task, the VLA may fail to effectively utilize the retrieved information, ultimately leading to task failure. To address this challenge, we propose R2-VLA, a risk-controlled retrieval-augmented VLA framework that enables reliable test-time adaptation by satisfying a user-specified task success rate requirement with minimal expert assistance. Specifically, R2-VLA learns an uncertainty predictor that estimates the uncertainty of the VLA's predicted actions given the retrieved demonstrations, guiding when to act autonomously or request expert assistance. We further introduce a task-level risk-control procedure that calibrates the uncertainty threshold to satisfy a user-specified task-failure risk requirement. Moreover, R2-VLA continually incorporates expert corrections into its retrieval memory to improve future adaptation while preserving the prescribed task-level risk guarantee. Experiments across three VLA backbones and four LIBERO benchmark suites demonstrate that R2-VLA improves average task success by 9.2--11.2 percentage points, achieving the desired target with fewer expert requests. These results highlight the effectiveness of connecting the usefulness of retrieved demonstrations with task-level risk control to enable successful robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Training Vision Models for LLM-Driven Weight-Space Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26408)**  `arXiv:2611.26408`  `cs.CV`  
  _Timo Ropinski, Sebastian Kranz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  It has been demonstrated that, in certain settings, large language models can perform weight-space programming to directly modify the weights of neural networks. However, it is unknown how dedicated training of these networks can contribute to the successes of weight-space programming. We present a benchmark of LLM-driven weight-space programming on vision models, in which five LLMs edit convolutional networks and vision transformers on relabeling and error-correction tasks on Waterbirds, CIFAR-100, PACS and Entity-30. We report more than 2,600 such edit processes, whereby each edit is scored on held-out images for success, side effects and accuracy loss. We compare standard training with label smoothing, gradient-based editable training, and our Edit-Ready Training (ERT), a novel method that rehearses the direct, closed-form weight edits LLMs perform. We show that preparing networks for editing raises the success of LLM edits substantially where standard networks are hard to edit. ERT further achieves the highest mean score for GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 across all evaluated baselines. We will release our LLM testing framework alongside all tasks, editing trajectories and scores. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ViDecide: Teaching Video Diffusion Models to Reason and Plan Step by Step](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26389)**  `arXiv:2611.26389`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaiwen Zhang, Yiming Gao, Zeqi Xiao, Xintao Wang, Pengfei Wan, Kun Gai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current leading video generation models can synthesize highly realistic videos, while still frequently violating physical and logical constraints in multi-step reasoning and planning, even after training with large-scale reasoning data or existing diffusion RL methods. We attribute these failures to the conventional video training paradigm, which treats a solution as a fixed-length clip rather than a chain of decisions. We present ViDecide, a decision-centric framework that trains video diffusion models to predict state transitions causally and decide when to stop, with a decision-focused training schedule. We further propose Corrective Fine-tuning (CFT), which corrects failed decisions by mapping their noise to the model's own successful continuations, achieving stable improvement without policy-gradient optimization. Across abstract games, planning tasks, and a new benchmark of multi-step robot planning, ViDecide achieves an average strict success rate of 83.8%, far above 23.3% for standard fine-tuning and 67.8% for the strongest prior method, and generalizes to larger puzzles and unseen layouts. Our framework therefore unlocks greater potential for video generation models as reliable reasoners and planners in the visual world. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SEEC: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Self-Supervised External-Eye Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26386)**  `arXiv:2611.26386`  `cs.CV`  
  _George R. Nahass, Michelle Fleishaker, Sanjay Ganesh, Sasha Hubschman, R. V. Paul Chan, Chad A. Purnell, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External-eye photography captures clinically relevant periocular anatomy at low acquisition cost, yet no large-scale standardized resource exists for evaluating self-supervised external-eye representations. We introduce a 4.35-million-image Standardized External-Eye Corpus (SEEC) derived from fourteen heterogeneous public face datasets, together with a benchmark for external-eye representation learning. For evaluation, we provide held-out evaluation distributions, landmark-annotated anatomical probe sets, and clinical disease-classification experiments spanning ptosis, thyroid eye disease, and craniofacial syndromes. We evaluate representations at two complementary levels: (a) the representation level, by assessing embedding geometry across multiple data distributions, and (b) the functional level, by assessing anatomical recovery, out-of-distribution anatomical transfer, and disease classification. Our models include ResNet-101 and ViT-B encoders pretrained using InfoNCE, VICReg, and LeJEPA objectives and compared with DINOv2 and MAE. Our results indicate that representation-level and functional-level evaluations are dissociable: broader embedding-space utilization does not consistently correspond to anatomical transfer or disease-classification performance, and no single encoder is universally optimal. Domain-specific external-eye encoders outperform DINOv2 and MAE on cross-dataset anatomical transfer despite being pretrained at substantially smaller scale, while external-eye-pretrained ViT models match or outperform these general-purpose baselines on frozen disease-classification probes. We release benchmark code, evaluation scripts, and preprocessing pipelines to reproduce SEEC atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[EgoCaptioner: Towards Comprehensive and Fine-Grained Egocentric Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26380)**  `arXiv:2611.26380`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqi Tang, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Tengfei Liu, Rundong Wang, Zongyang Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Egocentric video captioning requires fine-grained detail and accurate timing, yet current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often miss brief actions, confuse their order, or misinterpret the wearer's motion. Existing caption formats also offer limited support for combining detailed narration with precise timing. We present EgoCaptioner, which pairs a video-level overview of the setting, participants, and the wearer's overall activity with timed steps whose narration adapts to varying perceptual demands and marks key moments at specific timestamps. We develop a coarse-to-fine annotation pipeline that establishes event structure before expanding step descriptions, yielding 50,000 training examples from eight egocentric video sources. Complementary rewards assess factual accuracy and coverage, measure step-level temporal alignment, and penalize fragmented or redundant narration, guiding caption refinement through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). To improve the reliability of factual supervision, we supplement caption references with pose-derived motion facts when sensor data are available. Across five egocentric benchmarks, EgoCaptioner achieves leading performance among evaluated open-source models in caption quality, caption-conditioned question answering, and temporal alignment. It also surpasses Gemini 3.1 Pro on PhysCapBench and all evaluated proprietary baselines on Minerva-Ego under the caption-to-QA protocol. Ablations support the complementary roles of factual and temporal rewards and show that coarse-to-fine annotation provides more effective supervision than single-pass annotation.
  </details>

- **[ResVision: Spectrum-Matched Resizing and Context-Preserving Inspection for Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26318)**  `arXiv:2611.26318`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guojian Zhan, Jiahao Xu, Yahui Liu, Yao Hu, Chuan Mu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic visual reasoning improves vision-language models by selectively enlarging evidence without adding new scene content, but the benefit depends on whether fine structure remains accessible and whether local observations stay grounded in the full scene. We identify three recurring bottlenecks: high-frequency detail can be attenuated during visual-token formation; spatially distributed evidence can remain encoded yet difficult for the language model to retrieve; and isolated crops can weaken whole-detail correspondence through scale and positional mismatch. ResVision addresses these limitations with two complementary components. Spectrum-Matched Resizing (SMR) estimates image complexity and selects an adaptive global sampling scale, avoiding unnecessary enlargement on simple inputs while allocating more visual tokens to detail-rich images. Context-Preserving Inspection (CPI) returns each inspected crop together with the complete working view, preserving local detail and global context across tool turns. We evaluate Direct and Agentic variants on four frontier VLMs and 29 benchmarks spanning six visual-reasoning families. Complete ResVision (Agentic Base plus SMR plus CPI) is best for every model, improving by 3.36 to 4.94 points over Direct Base and 1.87 to 2.94 points over Agentic Base. These results show that adaptive visual sampling and context-preserving inspection address distinct, complementary constraints in multimodal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Tracking Objects Through Assembly and Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26269)**  `arXiv:2611.26269`  `cs.CV`  
  _Cheryl Lam, Yihong Sun, Bharath Hariharan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking objects through transformations is essential for understanding real-world dynamics. Recent work has extended traditional object tracking to detect state changes. However, existing methods require an initial object prompt and detect only its decompositions, missing assembly events, which begin with multiple objects rather than one. We therefore introduce a prompt-free formulation of this task, where the goal is to recover all object tracks and detect both decomposition and assembly events given only a video. Our key insight is that assembly is decomposition in reverse. Building on this insight, we present Atlas, a zero-shot system that constructs and jointly reasons over forward and reverse spatiotemporal partitions to recover object tracks and their transformations. We further introduce OmniST, a benchmark with dense annotations of objects and both transformation types. On OmniST, Atlas recovers more than twice as many state changes as prior methods while remaining competitive on existing benchmarks that only evaluate decomposition.
  </details>

- **[Prism: Hierarchical Text Detection with Minimalist Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26254)**  `arXiv:2611.26254`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Su, Xuesheng Wang, Zhilong Ji, Jinfeng Bai, Shijian Lu, Zhineng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical text detection aims to localize words, text lines, and paragraphs while parsing their structural relationships. It provides essential structural cues for visual text understanding. To maximize accuracy, existing methods typically rely on complex multi-scale feature fusion necks and heavy-weight heads, resulting in bloated architectures and low inference efficiency. In this paper, we argue that a sufficiently pre-trained backbone can effectively replace these sophisticated external modules, while simple geometric inclusion suffices for hierarchical text structural parsing. Thus, we propose Prism, a minimalist framework for hierarchical text detection. Prism performs multi-granularity detection by attaching parallel lightweight heads to a single-scale backbone feature map, covering words, text lines, and paragraphs, and reconstructs the structural hierarchy via an efficient geometric inclusion algorithm. In addition, we systematically compare representative lightweight head archetypes and show that pixel-level regression offers the best accuracy-efficiency trade-off in this setting. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks show that our significantly simplified architecture achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance with remarkable efficiency. Specifically, it runs $1.7\times$ faster than LRANet on CTW1500 while achieving higher accuracy, and surpasses the current SOTA method, OmniParser V2, by over 6 PQ points on the hierarchical HierText dataset. Code will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[Attention Drift Regularization with Adaptive Initialization for Domain-Robust Visual Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26234)**  `arXiv:2611.26234`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shivangi Rai, Vinod K Kurmi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a pretrained source model to unlabeled target domains without source data access, yet two critical failure modes remain unaddressed. First, attention drift: source-learned attention maps shift toward spurious or background features under distribution shift, destabilizing feature representations. Second, domain-gap-agnostic initialization: applying a single fixed source model uniformly across all target domains ignores domain gap severity, causing premature overfitting to noisy pseudo-labels under large shifts. We propose DGA-CAFC to address both failures. The Counterfactual Attention-based Feature Consistency (CAFC) module enforces feature stability by minimizing the discrepancy between representations computed under learned attention and those under counterfactual-inspired normalized perturbations, thereby promoting domain-robust features independent of spurious attention shifts. The Domain-Gap Aware Source Model Selection (DGASS) module estimates domain gap severity via target prediction entropy and selects the source model variant whose confidence level best matches the estimated gap, reducing negative transfer and stabilizing pseudo-label quality. Crucially, CAFC transfers beyond SFDA: applied as a lightweight regularizer to standard domain generalization objectives without target data. Extensive experiments across SFDA and DG benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently improves over recent baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of the proposed framework. Project page is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[SpecWM: Turning Verification into Refinement for Streaming World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26211)**  `arXiv:2611.26211`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaming Li, Lingzhen Li, Xiaze Yang, Chenyu Zhu, Kaiqi Liu, Xiaotong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, video world models have advanced interactive generation, with large models offering higher quality and smaller models enabling faster inference. Existing speculative methods combine their strengths through fast draft generation and selective target regeneration guided by rewards on decoded frames. However, verification covers only the external scorer's evaluation dimensions and adds decoding and scoring costs. Moreover, these scores provide no correction, requiring costly target regeneration for rejected drafts. To address these limitations, we propose Speculative World Model (SpecWM), a unified, training-free inference framework for streaming world models. It verifies draft chunks using the target's latent velocity field. Verification also yields a reusable correction; two thresholds select Accept, Refine, or Reject. Specifically, one-step refinement reuses it to improve perceptual quality, while three-step refinement strengthens camera following and trajectory consistency. These findings motivate transition-aware refinement. On WBench Navigation, SpecWM improves visual quality at 1.63$\times$ speedup over the target and raises NavScore from the draft's 75.99 to 85.63, achieving the best quality-speed-control trade-off. Additionally, our LongSpecBench shows sustained quality and control over long, multi-turn rollouts, while experiments on Hy-Nomas validate SpecWM across backbones and denoising budgets.
  </details>

- **[SpatialScout-V2: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore Real-World 3D Scenes and Localize Objects?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26177)**  `arXiv:2611.26177`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoxi Zhang, Hao Liang, Linxuan Wu, Yiming Xu, Yifu Yuan, Kun Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence requires agents to acquire visual evidence and integrate partial observations into coherent three-dimensional representations. We introduce SpatialScout-V2, which extends SpatialScout's active mapping task from simulation to real residential scenes through three advances: real visual observations, preservation of original scene content, and interactive exploration of recorded views. Built from 100 homes in Realsee3D, the benchmark covers 3,171 target objects across 29 categories. Given a fixed target inventory, agents autonomously select recorded viewpoints and viewing directions, integrate calibrated RGB observations, and iteratively estimate and revise object centers within a fixed interaction budget. We evaluate the localization accuracy and completeness of object-center maps using mean IoU, IoU-threshold hit rates, and target coverage. Evaluating seven frontier vision-language models (VLMs), we find that six achieve an all-target mean IoU below 20\%. Even the best-performing model, Claude Opus 5.5, reaches only 22.52\%, with just 18.36\% of targets exceeding an IoU of 0.5. Further analysis shows that broad submission coverage can coexist with limited localization accuracy, while target omissions further reduce overall scores. These results highlight substantial challenges in constructing accurate and complete object-center maps through active exploration of real scenes.
  </details>

- **[MultiBinder: Preventing Multi-Subject Identity Leakage in Personalized Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26164)**  `arXiv:2611.26164`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wei Li, Yiheng Zhang, Zhaofan Qiu, Ting Yao, Xiaoyan Sun, Tao Mei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized video generation faces a significant scalability challenge: as the number of custom subjects increases, existing methods suffer from severe identity leakage and semantic entanglement, resulting in videos with blended attributes and incoherent narratives. We identify that this stems from a lack of explicit binding between textual entity phrases and their corresponding visual patterns prior to fusion in diffusion models. To address this, we propose MultiBinder, a novel framework built on a ``bind-then-narrate'' paradigm. Our approach introduces a two-stage conditioning process before video diffusion. First, Cross-modal Entity Binding devises an entity-aware Q-Former to extract identity-centric features from each subject's reference image and tightly align them with its unique entity phrase, yielding disentangled ``identity tokens.'' Second, a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) acts as a ``narrative director'' in the Narrative-Aware Subject Arrangement stage, capturing the action of each subject from the text prompt and synthesizes contextualized subject features that encapsulate ``who is doing what'' and preserve role-specific distinctions. These features are then injected into a video diffusion model via dedicated conditioning pathways, ensuring precise attribute allocation. Extensive experiments on both general and newly curated multi-subject benchmarks show that MultiBinder significantly outperforms existing methods in multi-subject identity fidelity.
  </details>

- **[CryoGaussian: Heterogeneous Cryo-EM Reconstruction Made Explicit with Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26128)**  `arXiv:2611.26128`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziwen Wang, Jiajun Fan, Chaoran Cheng, Ruihan Guo, Yanru Qu, Juexiao Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-particle cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) determines macromolecular structures from noisy particle images. However, rare, transient compositional states and subtle, continuous conformational variation are difficult to resolve. We present CryoGaussian, a Gaussian splatting method for heterogeneous cryo-EM reconstruction that fits an explicit Gaussian density representation directly to the particle images through a differentiable rasterizer rendering orthographic line integrals modulated by the contrast transfer function. The Gaussians are initialized randomly, with their number derived from the estimated spatial support of the molecular density and their minimum size bounded by the Nyquist frequency of the particle images. To capture the compositional and conformational variation across particle images, we learn particle-image and per-Gaussian latents that jointly modulate the base position, amplitude, and scale of each Gaussian. Each structural state is then reconstructed by fitting the Gaussians conditioned on its inferred latent. On CryoBench, CryoGaussian achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction accuracy for both compositional and conformational heterogeneity. On experimental data, it resolves a rare compositional state of the 50S ribosomal subunit (class C4, 1.3% of particles) with rRNA helix 68 docked in the absence of the central protuberance, and continuous conformational motion of the SF3b and helicase subcomplexes of the pre-catalytic spliceosome. We further optimize the rasterizer for additive cryo-EM projection, reducing training time by 12.8-28.0$\times$.
  </details>

- **[One-to-Many: Diverse and Bidirectional Image-to-Image Translation via Rectified Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26058)**  `arXiv:2611.26058`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sho Shimotsumagari, Seiichi Uchida, Shumpei Takezaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many paired image-to-image translation tasks admit multiple valid target appearances, while training provides only one target per source. We propose One-to-Many Flow (O2MFlow), a latent-conditioned Rectified Flow (RF) model for one-to-many generation and backward translation (target$\rightarrow$source) within a single transport framework. Conditioning the RF dynamics on an appearance latent turns transport into a family of latent-indexed bijections: a fixed latent gives a mapping that is inverted by time-reversed integration, and sampled latents produce diverse outputs for the same input. We formalize reusable appearance variations as Shared Latent Transport and show that Kullback--Leibler (KL) regularization directly controls the source--latent distribution gap between training and generation. Across three benchmarks, O2MFlow offers dataset-dependent forward diversity--fidelity trade-offs and the lowest backward LPIPS-Dist among evaluated methods, with the lowest backward FID on two benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Less Than Meets the Eye: Action-Set Identifiability and Principled Abstention for 3D Physical Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26056)**  `arXiv:2611.26056`  `cs.CV`  
  _Reshad Ul Karim, Abrar Samin, Sammam Mahdi, Syeda Maliha Tabassum, Md Rashad Al Hasan Rony_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A corridor, its mirror reflection, and its image on a display can look identical, yet their physical surfaces lie at different depths. We test whether bounded camera motions distinguish these explanations. Intervene3D takes an image and an initial geometry estimate, predicts the view under each hypothesis and candidate motion, and selects a motion by maximin separability. It reports the mechanism and first physical surface only if the available motions separate the hypotheses still in contention. Otherwise it abstains. An adequacy test also rejects observations that no hypothesis explains. On a matched synthetic benchmark with exact landmarks, disabling abstention gives confident answers on 76.4% of unresolvable scenes. With abstention, none receives a confident answer under exact or noisy landmarks. At 100% accuracy on answered scenes, maximin selection answers 61.0% of scenes and summed selection 43.1%. The adequacy test lowers wrong answers on strongly curved mirror scenes from 56.8% to 12.4%. Real display experiments compare two hypotheses using one frozen depth-network estimate. The second image supplies photometric evidence and is never passed through the depth network. One executed motion reduces the pooled median surface error of twelve networks by 31.5%. These networks have larger error inside the annotated panel than outside on 33.4% of illusion-benchmark images. They place the first physical surface behind the glass on 36.9% of Mirror3D frames.
  </details>

- **[MorphTwin: Enriching Deformable Material Responses from Videos for Morphable and Interactive Physical Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25963)**  `arXiv:2611.25963`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoqin Hong, Ding Fan, Fubin Dou, Tianyu Huang, Yuzhuo Chen, Zhemeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing interactive physical twins from video requires accurately capturing how objects deform while maintaining efficient simulation. We observe that reproducing motion in a video does not guarantee correct responses to new forces when the physical representation restricts how an object can deform. In spring-based twins, coupling between volume and shape responses creates such a limitation. We present MorphTwin, which enriches local deformation modes to capture and independently control these responses while retaining standard spring forces and explicit simulation. To support interpretable material editing and transfer, we use offline calibration to map shared relative response changes to stiffness updates on each reconstructed graph. Under identical inverse settings on synthetic scenes, MorphTwin reduces the mean absolute error of Poisson's ratio by 75.8% and the mean relative error of Young's modulus by 22.1% compared to PhysTwin. We introduce an egocentric stereo and RGB-D benchmark covering interactions with objects of varying stiffness. Real-world experiments demonstrate improved reconstruction accuracy and up to 14.4% lower future tracking error than PhysTwin at interactive speeds.
  </details>

- **[TwinD: Generalizing Deepfake Detection from Blending to Entire Face Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25935)**  `arXiv:2611.25935`  `cs.CV`  
  _Andrii Yermakov, Jan Cech, Mario Fritz, Jiri Matas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Facial deepfake detectors are usually trained on blending fakes, where a generated face is pasted back into a real frame. Recent generators synthesize the entire face and leave no traces of blending. On a benchmark of 19 datasets released from 2019 to 2026, the best prior facial deepfake detector reaches a mean AUROC of 96.2% on ten blending datasets, but none exceeds 78.1% on five datasets of entire face synthesis (EFS). AI-generated content (AIGC) detectors do not close this gap. We propose TwinD, a detector trained on FaceForensics++ fakes and two kinds of twins: fakes made from a real frame. A reconstruction twin is a real frame reconstructed by an autoencoder, with the real pixels put back in a random region. It targets the neural traces of a decoder. A blob twin blends an altered copy of a real frame with the frame itself, targeting blending artifacts. It needs no generator and no face-shaped mask. Together, these twins provide two complementary forensic cues that generalize to unseen manipulations. TwinDreaches a mean AUROC of 96.3%, 8.9 points above the prior state-of-the-art, with 98.1% on the blending datasets and 95.6% on the EFS datasets. Trained on twins alone, without a deepfake dataset, single models still reach 94.3% on average over three seeds. For reproducibility, the code, trained model, and training data are at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SpatialMem: Building Quantitative Spatial Reasoning Agents with Hierarchical Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25925)**  `arXiv:2611.25925`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zinan Zheng, Nuo Chen, Jia Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision Language Models (VLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in qualitative spatial reasoning, but remain limited in quantitative spatial reasoning. Current work leverages the VLM agent to invoke external vision tools to perform computation. However, these methods usually bind each functional requirement to a single predefined vision specialist, implicitly resulting in fixed and rigid tool pipelines. In practice, addressing diverse spatial scenarios and tasks demands a broader diversity in pipeline orchestration and vision specialist selection to achieve robust spatial reasoning. However, such diversity leads to a combinatorial execution space, making it challenging for the agent to orchestrate the optimal pipelines and specialists for different scenarios.In this work, we propose SpatialMem, a memory framework that enables a VLM agent to accumulate tool-use experience and leverage that memory to orchestrate vision specialists to solve the spatial reasoning problem. SpatialMem builds a two-level memory structure, with task memory capturing tool orchestration and scene memory capturing tool selection. It further introduces a dual-state trajectory optimization process to preserve successful tool experiences and refine suboptimal ones. Moreover, existing spatial reasoning benchmarks suffer from quality issues and limited coverage of scene types and tasks. To address these gap, we propose MetricBench, a benchmark covering both indoor and outdoor scenes and three quantitative spatial reasoning tasks. Our method achieves state-of-the-art results on MetricBench, Q-Spatial, and SpatilRGPT-Bench, outperforming best VLM baselines by 32% on MetricBench. Additionally, the memory design yields a 9.1% absolute gain over a memory-free agent on MetricBench.
  </details>

- **[EvoMem: Self-Evolving Skills for Time-Aware Long Video Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25889)**  `arXiv:2611.25889`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yutong Guan, Meixing Shi, Haoran Sun, Bing Sun, Yuxiang Cai, Yankai Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although memory-augmented architectures have expanded the ability of large vision-language models (LVLMs) to process long videos, existing retrieval processes still largely rely on global semantic matching and lack state-driven temporal control, often returning fragmented or redundant evidence and failing to maintain a coherent evidence chain. To address this challenge, we propose EvoMem, a time-aware long-video memory agent guided by self-evolving retrieval skills. EvoMem constructs a hierarchical time-indexed memory, where different types of evidence are aligned onto a unified video timeline; during retrieval, temporal cues from acquired evidence are further converted into explicit signals that constrain subsequent memory access. To address recurring procedural errors in long-video question answering, such as unclear retrieval targets, improper memory access paths, or premature stopping, EvoMem introduces retrieval skills to provide process-level guidance, enabling the agent to dynamically adjust retrieval content, memory operations, and target temporal ranges according to the current evidence state, while deciding when to stop. Furthermore, inspired by reinforcement learning, we formulate skill evolution as a reward-guided policy improvement process. Each candidate skill is treated as an external policy patch, and rewards are assigned according to answer-correctness state transitions on the validation set. In this way, effective skills are accepted and low-quality skills are rejected without updating model parameters. Experiments on four long-video question answering benchmarks show that EvoMem significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average accuracy of 60.1% and an average improvement of 8.6 percentage points over the previous best methods, demonstrating its effectiveness for long-video reasoning.
  </details>

- **[WHU-Road3D: A dataset and benchmark towards fine-grained road surface mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25873)**  `arXiv:2611.25873`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoxin Mi, Zhen Cao, Chong Liu, Yuhao Li, Zhen Dong, Bisheng Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately extracting and mapping road surface elements is crucial for applications such as high-definition maps, road asset management, and intelligent transportation systems. This study first provides a comprehensive review of the benchmark datasets for extracting and mapping road elements, encompassing overhead-to-perspective data, large-to-local scales, and coarse-to-fine details. Subsequently, we thoroughly review the relevant approaches for extracting road surface elements. However, we argue that existing datasets still require enhancements in semantic richness, data modality diversity, and scenario complexity. To bridge these gaps, this paper presents WHU-Road3D, a large-scale multimodal dataset for fine-grained road surface mapping to address these shortcomings. WHU-Road3D provides approximately 100 km of diverse Chinese highways and urban streets. It features a rich combination of MLS point clouds, associated panoramic images, vehicle trajectories, detailed vectorized annotations, and pointwise instance annotations for road surface elements. The dataset carefully annotates 15 distinct categories of road elements, including common markings like road boundaries, zebra crossings, lane dividers, stop lines, as well as 10 types of direction symbols. To our knowledge, WHU-Road3D stands as the first multimodal dataset for mapping such a comprehensive range of road surface elements, designed to facilitate cutting-edge research in instance segmentation, full-element extraction and mapping on road surfaces. Beyond these immediate applications, WHU-Road3D also provides a valuable source for hierarchical road topology reasoning, map updating, and street scene generation. The dataset, along with the code for our benchmark methods, will be made publicly available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Thinking in Streams: Proactive Spatial Reasoning with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25865)**  `arXiv:2611.25865`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyoung Hong, Minsu Kim, Jaehyun Kim, Jeonghwan Cho, Jiwoo Lee, Seon Joo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing when a requested spatial condition holds in streaming video can require maintaining and updating spatial knowledge across changing viewpoints. We study this capability through proactive spatial reasoning, where a model receives a query in advance and determines whether and when to respond as the stream unfolds. We introduce ProSpace-Bench, comprising nine tasks across object, camera, and scene reasoning, and ProSpace-Train for supervised training. To support spatial decisions, we propose ProSpace-VLM, which injects explicit geometric encodings estimated causally from incoming frames into intermediate VLM layers, using the existing KV cache as spatial memory and spatial token selection for efficient streaming. Experiments reveal a substantial gap between VLMs and humans, while ProSpace-VLM improves timely responses and correct abstention over fine-tuned baselines. Analyses examine how ProSpace-VLM represents previously observed spatial information across changing viewpoints.
  </details>

- **[EgoWitness: Benchmarking Instance-Level Spatial Memory and Reasoning in Egocentric Video Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25849)**  `arXiv:2611.25849`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengru Fang, Zehao Liu, Mingyu Tsoi, Yaoyu Zhu, Junlong Li, Fengmao Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recalling where and when an object was last seen and who saw it requires recognizing it across days and people. Existing long-video benchmarks focus on events, object categories, or relative locations, leaving instance-level spatial memory largely untested in multimodal models. To address this gap, we present EgoWitness, a benchmark for instance-level spatial memory and reasoning in egocentric video. Its 8,453 human-verified questions span approximately 324 hours of week-long and short-horizon recordings and test seven abilities, from recognizing an object or room to recalling who saw it and reasoning about room visits. Over one million reviewed masks of 34 registered landmarks and 21 movable-object labels are linked through room timelines. Among twelve direct vision-language models (VLMs), open-weight models reach at most 46.97% accuracy, and none exceeds 35% on identifying who saw an object last. We therefore propose EgoClue, a spatial-memory agent that retrieves candidate intervals, verifies instance identity, and computes spatial and temporal relations with five tools. Two-stage training with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) raises its 4B controller from 23.64% to 72.65% accuracy on 1,320 held-out questions. Evidence-trace synthesis matches the best direct VLM at 74.85% on week-long questions and achieves 80.56% in environments excluded from training.
  </details>

- **[Suppressing the Shortcut Hidden Behind the Gains of VideoLLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25776)**  `arXiv:2611.25776`  `cs.CV`  
  _Youngwoo Shin, Jongsuk Kim, Junmo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the stage in which Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) advance their capability to reason over video. However, the VideoLLM also learns to answer from textual regularities of its training data, a shortcut that bypasses the video.
