The regularities behind the shortcut are hard to define at the data level, and the shortcut forms regardless of training epoch, data size, or backbone. The shortcut is hidden behind a rise in accuracy within the training distribution, yet it is not required for the rise and even costs SFT the gain the video offers under distribution shift. We therefore propose Video-Evidence Training via Orthogonal projection (VETO), which intervenes on the fine-tuning update where the shortcut is written rather than on the data. VETO calibrates the subspace that the text alone occupies in the activations of the base model and projects the update onto its orthogonal complement. The VideoLLM is thus left to ground its answer in the full input, with the shortcut suppressed as it forms and never defined. Across seven VideoLLMs, VETO preserves the gain of SFT on the training distribution and suppresses the shortcut. On six benchmarks under shift it gains more than SFT and answers from the full input rather than the shortcut. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DACA: Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent for Zero-Shot 3D Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25722)**  `arXiv:2611.25722`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaming Liu, Linghe Kong, Guixu Zhang, Huaxi Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D visual grounding (3D-VG) is a task that bridges the natural language and 3D space, serving as a foundational capability for embodied intelligence and human-computer interaction. Although recent 3D-VG agents overcome the reliance on pre-processed 3D point clouds, they essentially employ a unidirectional, pipeline-style tool-calling architecture. This architecture separates semantic understanding from geometric reconstruction, leading to error accumulation and an inability to autonomously recover from failures. To address this flaw, this paper proposes the Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent (DACA), which reinterprets 3D-VG as a semantic-geometric dual-anchor collaborative reasoning paradigm. DACA comprises two complementary specialized tools: the semantic anchor tool holds the visual semantic features of the target and achieves efficient target tracking via optical flow and VLM local verification; the geometric anchor tool holds the 3D spatial geometric features of the target and performs global spatial reasoning via voxelization and multi-keypoint projection. The two components exchange information bidirectionally via a standardized tool call protocol, autonomously deciding on the next perception-action step to form a closed-loop optimization. Experiments on the ScanRefer and Nr3D benchmarks demonstrate that zero-shot DACA achieves an overall Acc@0.25 of 73.6\% on ScanRefer and 70.4\% overall accuracy on Nr3D, far surpassing the latest zero-shot 3D-VG methods. The code and models are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Slide2PPT: Native PowerPoint Reconstruction with Localization-Guided Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25708)**  `arXiv:2611.25708`  `cs.CV`  
  _Baoliang Chen, Xinlong Bu, Lingyu Zhu, Xiangjie Sui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-to-slide reconstruction must recover both visual appearance and native object structure for subsequent editing. Yet visually faithful charts and tables may lack editable series and cells, and direct code generation couples content prediction to rendering implementation. We present Slide2PPT, a model-agnostic, training-free framework that equips both open- and closed-source vision-language models with native PowerPoint reconstruction capabilities. Through a unified typed interface, the model predicts object records while a fixed renderer materializes their native properties, enabling integration with different backbones without task-specific training or architectural changes. Localization-guided refinement then connects visual discrepancies to object records for correction. Regional updates preserve unselected records, while whole-slide updates address cross-region relationships. Record refinement and targeted object editing support structural and attribute-level changes, with rendered feedback governing iterative acceptance. To evaluate this framework across diverse reconstruction demands, we introduce Slide2PPT-Bench, covering controlled elements, real slides, and scientific figures. Evaluation separately measures file production, rendered agreement, and native-object availability. Experiments across open- and closed-source backbones demonstrate substantially more reliable PPTX generation than direct code generation, improved layout and text recovery on scientific figures, and native chart and table interfaces beyond geometric primitives.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Attribute Execution for Sequential Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25648)**  `arXiv:2611.25648`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiyang Zhang, Yuanhe Tian, Hongzhi Wang, Yan Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given an image and successive user instructions, sequential image editing should return an updated image that satisfies each new request and preserves earlier requirements that remain active. This lets users develop a result without repeatedly restating or repairing their decisions. The difficulty is that a retrieved image can contain a valid color on an obsolete surface, while the current image may lack the newly requested material. Neither reference selection nor a precise object mask alone specifies how to reconcile attributes occupying the same region. We present TRACE, which separates the continuing attribute from its visual carrier and prioritizes local appearance operations under complete-request checks. Active-goal binding identifies what should persist, carrier selection supplies suitable current or newly generated pixels, and tonal calibration reduces tinting near highlights and shadows. Experiments across frozen editors, independent visual readers, new source images, and complete sequential benchmarks support joint color-material recovery and show how local execution preserves the surrounding scene.
  </details>

- **[LG-FiLM: Local-Global Mixture-of-Experts Modulation for RAW-to-RGB Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25639)**  `arXiv:2611.25639`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fei Li, Xilei Cao, Shichang Zhai, Kun Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An image signal processor does more than reconstruct an image. It also has to decide how strongly to denoise, how to balance color, and which tone curve maps the scene onto the display, and these settings depend on the scene itself, the curve that suits a backlit sky is not the one that suits an indoor portrait. Most neural ISPs learn a single fixed transformation and apply it to every scene. We instead make the adaptation an explicit part of the network. Our model, LG FiLM, keeps one shared encoder decoder and places a small mixture of affine experts at its bottleneck; the experts scales, shifts, and mixing weights are predicted from local neighborhood evidence together with a global scene descriptor. Reconstruction stays in a single shared pathway and only the processing mode is switched. We evaluate LG FiLM on three datasets, Fujifilm UltraISP, Zurich RAW to RGB, and a COCO derived benchmark built with an inverse ISP. The model reaches the best reconstruction fidelity of the compared entries on the primary benchmark and transfers to the other two.
  </details>

- **[CaT-Movie: Balancing Global Planning and Local Rendering for Long Multi-Shot Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25620)**  `arXiv:2611.25620`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinpeng Lu, Haoyuan Shi, Yuanshen Guan, Jiyuan Xia, Bin Wu, Yinda Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current multi-shot video generators render only limited durations per pass, requiring long movies to be stitched from local segments across which narrative progression and visual identity drift. To synthesize long-horizon movies with Coherence across Time, we present CaT-Movie, a multi-shot generation system consisting of a global story planner and a local window renderer. The planner expands user requests via a large language model to preserve, infer, and complete story details, while deterministic code maintains the resulting storyboard across shots to compile self-contained shot prompts and index reference frames from generated history. To execute this guidance, the renderer applies reference-routed sparse self-attention and shot-wise cross-attention to route identities from the indexed frames while isolating per-shot textual conditions. Given the scarcity of high-quality open-source multi-shot data, we construct a dataset of over 350K film shots annotated with semantic state transitions, entity grounding, and multi-granularity requests. We further build CaT-MBench, a benchmark on held-out films with the same annotations. It gives every baseline its native format and counts cross-shot consistency only when a cut opens a new camera setup, preventing repeated framing from passing as consistency. On CaT-MBench, CaT-Movie attains the highest average score and the most consistent characters among all methods. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Anchored Inertial Proposals with Objective Verification for Diffusion-Prior Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25551)**  `arXiv:2611.25551`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minwoo Kim, Seunghyeok Shin, Dabin Kim, Hongki Lim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free diffusion priors are powerful for inverse problems, but measurement guidance during reverse sampling is local: aggressive updates can improve immediate data fit while disrupting later denoising. We introduce an anchored inertial correction rule that separates proposal generation from acceptance. At each corrected step, the denoiser prediction anchors a frozen clean-space objective combining measurement fit with a noise-level-dependent proximal term. Heavy-ball dynamics with step size and momentum computed from the measured curvature generate a clean-state candidate; a separate verifier evaluates it with the same objective, with the denoiser prediction retained as fallback; both stages are derived from one objective for the whole trajectory, considered one step at a time. The accepted clean state is re-noised to continue sampling. The rule applies to pixel and latent diffusion with differentiable measurement operators, requires no retraining, and adds no denoiser calls inside the correction loop. We analyze how objective verification yields local nonincrease and displacement control. Across natural-image benchmarks, the method achieves competitive reconstruction quality. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Query-Aware vs. Query-Agnostic Training-Free Video Token Pruning in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25537)**  `arXiv:2611.25537`  `cs.CV`  
  _Divya Kothandaraman, Timo Kumkel, Jaclyn Pytlarz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video Large Language Models process large numbers of visual tokens, making token pruning important for efficient inference. Training-free pruning methods differ in whether and how they use the user query, and the value of query conditioning remains unclear. We present a controlled study of training-free, pre-LLM video token selection. Using a shared Qwen-3-VL backbone, token budgets, and decoding settings, we compare 21 pruning principles spanning query-agnostic, shallow query-conditioned, structured query-conditioned, and holistic strategies. To instantiate the last two categories, we introduce Structured Query Pruning, which decomposes the question into semantic anchors, and a Pareto variant, which combines anchor relevance with query-agnostic visual diversity. Across Video-MME, MVBench, and LongVideoBench, relevance computed from the raw question embedding is unreliable (8--16 points below the best methods), whereas anchors derived from query decomposition avoid this failure. However, query access is not inherently advantageous: strong query-agnostic coverage methods remain competitive or superior in the full-field comparison. Relative to a redundancy-only baseline, structured selection helps most on evidence-specific and long-context tasks at tight budgets; on broad tasks, the holistic selector improves over either relevance or redundancy alone. Further, pruning reduces cost and memory. These results support a practical design rule: preserve visual coverage by default, and add structured relevance when the task and token budget demand selective evidence retrieval.
  </details>

- **[Support-Guided Triadic Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Dialogue with LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25535)**  `arXiv:2611.25535`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiheng Li, Wenhao Wang, Xingyi Li, Zhilin Tang, Zichang Tan, Zhen Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multimodal multi-turn dialogue, dialogue history plays a dual role: reliable responses provide useful context for subsequent turns, whereas hallucinated responses can propagate errors across turns. Existing training-free methods globally adjust the effect of dialogue history on generation, making it difficult to suppress misleading information without weakening useful context. To address this trade-off, we introduce Support-Guided Triadic Decoding (SGTD), a training-free framework that selectively controls the influence of unreliable history and fuses complementary predictions during decoding. Specifically, Historical Answer Support Estimation measures the visual support of historical responses against type-matched alternatives and identifies low-support content that may mislead later generation. Support-Targeted Attention Intervention then weakens the influence of this content while preserving the surrounding dialogue, producing a support-refined prediction. Triadic Decoding combines this prediction with the full-history prediction and answer-free visual evidence, while the Support-Adaptive Plausibility Constraint filters implausible tokens using visual agreement and historical support. Experiments on MMHalSnowball and VisDiaHalBench across representative LVLMs show that SGTD consistently achieves the highest accuracy under hallucinated histories while preserving the benefits of factual context.
  </details>

- **[Contrast Where It Matters: Visual Evidence-Guided Decoding for Mitigating Hallucinations in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25524)**  `arXiv:2611.25524`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiheng Li, Wenhao Wang, Xingyi Li, Mingda Jia, Zichang Tan, Yinhang Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite strong multimodal capabilities, large vision-language models (LVLMs) often hallucinate when linguistic priors outweigh visual evidence or when predictions are grounded in irrelevant or misleading image regions. Training-free contrastive decoding mitigates hallucinations by contrasting the original prediction with an auxiliary one that exposes linguistic priors. Yet existing methods focus on altering the auxiliary input or computation and do not ensure that either branch uses query-relevant visual evidence during decoding. We propose Visual Evidence-Guided Decoding (VEGD), a training-free framework that identifies query-relevant visual evidence and guides its use throughout contrastive decoding. VEGD locates this evidence by aggregating localization heads with strong visual responses and coherent spatial structure, then selectively weakens it in the auxiliary image. During generation, VEGD directs visual-carrier heads in both branches toward the same evidence location, strengthening access to intact evidence in the main branch while anchoring the auxiliary branch to its weakened counterpart. An adaptive distribution fusion strategy then combines or contrasts their predictions according to distribution divergence. Experiments across diverse LVLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that VEGD consistently reduces hallucinations.
  </details>

- **[Carry the Fine Print: Budget-Aware Visual Token Compression for Document OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25506)**  `arXiv:2611.25506`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yixuan Huang, ShengFan Zhang, Jiawei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token compression often treats salience or similarity as a proxy for transcription importance. Complete document OCR breaks this assumption: small symbols and repeated entries can be indispensable to the transcript despite being visually inconspicuous. We present Select and Compensate, a two-stage framework built on a simple idea: choosing where to retain a token need not determine all the evidence it carries. Selection combines adaptive encoding resolution with global importance and local contrast to choose which tokens to retain as anchors. Compensation enriches these anchors with nearby discarded content and its relative position, without adding tokens. The OCR backbone remains frozen, and native coordinates and structural tokens are preserved. We evaluate complete page transcription across five backbones on OmniDocBench and document reconstruction on MPDocBench. With half the original visual tokens, our input-adapted configuration reaches 95.42 OmniDocBench Overall on Ovis versus 96.09 for the uncompressed model, and paired evaluations show gains over simply reducing the input resolution on both benchmarks. With the original input grid, our method stays within 0.17 points of the uncompressed model at 75% retention and exceeds a learned global selector by 4.70 points at 25%. On a fixed 200-page Ovis workload at the same 50% visual-token budget, only 3 of its outputs hit the length limit, versus 30-34 for the evaluated RTPrune, DivPrune, and HiPrune adaptations, and it finishes the batch 35-40% sooner. Code will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[SegAnyFruit: Learning Generalizable and Edge Deployable Fruit Instance Segmentation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25475)**  `arXiv:2611.25475`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yanan Wang, Yuzheng wang, Yibin Ying, Zhenghao Fei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying fruit instance segmentation in real agricultural settings requires generalizing across unseen species, orchards, and imaging conditions under strict onboard compute and annotation budgets. We introduce SegAnyFruit (SAF), a data construction and progressive training framework that distills foundation-model priors into lightweight, prompt-free segmenters. SAF combines generic mask pre-training, broad mid-training on automatically annotated web imagery from our curated WebFruits dataset, and human-verified agricultural refinement post-training on PaperFruits, together spanning 733K images and 9.66M whole-fruit masks across 165 fruit types. Across seven benchmarks, zero-shot SAF-RF-DETR-XL reaches 69.06 average mask AP50:95 (+4.76 over SAM 3) while remaining deployable on edge devices. SAF initialization also boosts few-shot to full-shot adaptation across five agricultural domains while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Finally, we systematically analyze training stages, data scale, model capacity, and generative augmentation, and validate our pipeline's transferability to underwater, cell, and human segmentation. We will release our datasets, code, and SAF models.
  </details>

- **[RemoteVLPG: How to Obtain a Specific VLM for Language-Guided Path Generation on Remote Sensing Images?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25458)**  `arXiv:2611.25458`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junzhe Dang, Mengmeng Zhang, Yuxiang Zhang, Wei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how to obtain a task-specific Vision-Language Model (VLM) for language-guided path generation between start and end points on remote sensing images. We explore this question from three aspects. First, we implement three widely used architectural approaches and compare their performance. Second, we design a scalable method for constructing pre-training data and build an effective pre-training dataset with a degree of diversity. Methods trained on this dataset achieve promising zero-shot performance. Third, we construct three benchmarks that support stratified evaluation and design three metrics for path generation. We investigate this problem through extensive experiments. The results show that the zero-shot performance of methods trained on the constructed pre-training dataset can exceed that of some advanced commercial VLMs. Code and Dataset can be available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[VisualTalk-Bench: Evaluating Question-Driven Avatar-Led Video Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25444)**  `arXiv:2611.25444`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xin Zhang, Tujie Xu, Zhiyi Chen, Xiaodong Zhang, Sicheng Yang, Minglei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in video generation and avatar synthesis enable new ways to answer user questions through visual explanations. This task requires a system to formulate a correct answer, translate relevant information into visual material, and deliver the explanation through an avatar's speech and actions. Existing benchmarks rarely assess whether avatars point to the visual content being discussed. We introduce VisualTalk-Bench for question-driven avatar-led video explanations in finance and Chinese and English education. It includes 800 curated reference videos and 100 independent evaluation requests, assessing Content Factuality, Speech-Visual Consistency, Visual Quality, Motion Smoothness, and Avatar-Content Pointing. Yet end-to-end video generators often struggle to align gestures with specific visual targets. To explore this challenge, we develop Avatar-O Agent, a modular reference implementation with explicit interaction planning. Experiments across six agents show that factual precision and visual quality do not guarantee accurate pointing. A 26-participant study comparing four agents finds that Avatar-O Agent receives the highest average ratings for visual guidance, perceived pointing accuracy, and explanation effectiveness. We will release the data and code.
  </details>

- **[Fold the Loop: Decoding-Time KV Memory for Looped Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25369)**  `arXiv:2611.25369`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kun Zhou, Wenmian Yang, Zhensheng Wang, Yanzeng Li, Weijia Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen visual reasoning by repeatedly applying weight-tied modules. However, their decoding memory grows with recurrent depth and limits the image size and batch size that fit in a fixed memory budget. Each recurrent step produces its own key-value (KV) states, so the cache holds several copies of the same image. We find that, after an exact prefill, earlier steps still need access to visual information but can reuse the final visual KV within the same module. We propose FoldCache, which compresses visual KV along the recurrent axis while preserving the full prefill computation. FoldCache partitions recurrent steps within each module into groups and retains the final visual KV of each group for its members to share during decoding. Sharing stays within module boundaries, while question and answer KV remain separate for each step. The grouping granularity controls the degree of compression, and folding can be combined with token eviction and quantization to further reduce memory use. On LoopVL, FoldCache approximately halves prefix KV memory while matching full-cache accuracy on five open-ended benchmarks. These memory savings support batch sizes up to 1.7 times those of a full cache under the same GPU memory budget, and up to 2.6 times with the more aggressive FoldCache-Lite.
  </details>

- **[ScaleDiag: Diagnosing the Limits of Test-Time Scaling in GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25297)**  `arXiv:2611.25297`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chen Liu, Zhen Fang, Xin Yu, Dadong Wang, Ling Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding maps a screenshot and a natural language instruction to the coordinates of the intended interface element. Existing approaches improve grounding through visual scaling, instruction scaling, and sampling scaling, yet it remains unclear which grounding bottlenecks these strategies address and where their gains saturate. To address this gap, we introduce ScaleDiag, a controlled diagnostic framework that isolates the effects of visual input, instruction information, and prediction sampling on GUI grounding by independently varying target scale and surrounding context, target-specific information and auxiliary descriptions, and candidate coverage and selection, respectively. We evaluate ScaleDiag across five benchmarks and up to eight vision-language models and find that each scaling strategy operates within a much narrower regime than commonly assumed. (1) Visual scaling is governed by target occupancy rather than a fixed crop ratio, with full-screen errors typically rescued once the target occupies only 0.32% of the cropped view, while further zooming eventually becomes harmful. (2) Instruction scaling is driven by information that identifies the target rather than description richness itself, as even ground-truth-derived attributes such as color, size, and shape are often neutral or harmful, while appearance and spatial descriptions account for over 90% of the achievable gain. (3) Repeated sampling exposes a different limitation, as correct coordinates are frequently generated but remain difficult to select, leaving 5.1--11.0 points of oracle headroom while geometric consensus provides almost no improvement. Together, these results show that test-time scaling is constrained by distinct operating regimes rather than benefiting monotonically from more visual focus, richer instructions, or additional samples. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CalibCache: Cache-to-Full Feature Dynamics Calibration for Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25296)**  `arXiv:2611.25296`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingyu Liang, Dingkun Xu, Jingwei Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images, but iterative sampling remains computationally expensive. Feature caching reduces the number of full network evaluations, yet its approximation error can become more severe under short sampling schedules, where fewer full-compute steps and a coarser sampling grid can make history-based extrapolation less reliable. In this paper, we propose CalibCache, a lightweight offline calibration method that corrects systematic discrepancies between cached and full-compute feature dynamics. Specifically, CalibCache calibrates feature acceleration, defined as the second-order finite difference across sampling steps. Using only a single pair of full-compute and naive-cache trajectories at each setting, CalibCache fits scalar acceleration maps offline by centered least squares. These maps are then applied during sampling without additional full-network evaluations or online optimization. Across FLUX.1-dev and Qwen-Image on DrawBench and DiT-XL/2 on ImageNet-256, CalibCache consistently improves fidelity or generation quality over TaylorSeer under 20-step sampling at cache intervals $N=3,4,5$. At $N=5$, it reduces FLUX.1-dev LPIPS from $0.563$ to $0.429$ while achieving a $2.86\times$ denoising speedup over full-compute sampling, and lowers DiT-XL/2 FID from $16.39$ to $10.37$ relative to TaylorSeer.
  </details>

- **[Branch Caching After Step Distillation in Four- and Eight-Step Image Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25248)**  `arXiv:2611.25248`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingyu Liang, Dingkun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a distilled image generator save time by reusing features between its few remaining denoising steps? We study a fixed branch cache that replaces selected attention and MLP evaluations while preserving the student's noise schedule. One full trajectory and one raw-cache trajectory from the same initial noise supply a scalar correction for each branch type and reuse position. At $1024^2$, one internal reuse reduces four-step Qwen-Image-Lightning denoising time from 1.279 to 0.977 seconds. Three reuses yield $1.51\times$ speedup on eight-step Lightning and $1.53\times$ on eight-step Hyper-FLUX. We evaluate image quality on all 200 DrawBench prompts. With the reuse positions held fixed, correction improves mean same-seed image fidelity over raw reuse by 0.66 and 0.87 dB on the two Lightning students. On Hyper-FLUX the PSNR difference is 0.01 dB, while LPIPS improves from 0.1625 to 0.1546. These results measure the effect of branch caching and correction while retaining each student's original sampling grid.
  </details>

- **[Recurrent Vision Language Models Solve the Shell Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25210)**  `arXiv:2611.25210`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dingqiang Ye, Jiacong Xu, Wen Zhang, Yiqun Mei, Vishal M. Patel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tracking a hidden object through a video is effortless for humans, yet it remains a bottleneck for vision language models (VLMs). In the shell game, where a ball is hidden under one of several identical cups that are then swapped, VLMs that answer directly stay near chance, thinking models fail on long games, and fine-tuning learns only the lengths it has seen. We argue that the limit is serial computation: each swap must be composed with the last result, and a VLM of fixed depth has only a fixed number of state transitions for it. To address this, we propose RecurrentVLM, which makes every attention layer of a pretrained VLM recur over time by computing the keys and values of finished frame groups from the layer's own output. With a single answer token, RecurrentVLM reaches 100% on VET-Bench and 99.6% on MET-Bench, and 99% on 50-swap games after training on at most 30 swaps. We also observe several interesting phenomena: length generalization emerges suddenly at an aha moment; without recurrence, each swap costs an attention layer; the ball's state forms in the first four attention layers; and recurrence keeps the latest state at the cost of details of earlier moments. These results suggest that recurrent VLMs are a potential solution for tracking the states of unobserved objects in video. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[MV-JEPA: Post-Training Video Encoders for Cross-View Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25172)**  `arXiv:2611.25172`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yangchen Zhao, Ali Payani, Caiwen Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  JEPA-based video encoders learn strong representations for video and image understanding through prediction in latent space. However, their standard pretraining predicts features within individual images or video clips, without explicitly exploiting paired camera views of the same object or moment. Consequently, the learned representations may not reliably support matching across different camera viewpoints. To solve this problem, we introduce multi-view predictive post-training to strengthen cross-view instance and temporal correspondence while retaining independent single-view inference. A shared encoder processes masked context views independently, and a training-time predictor jointly predicts latent tokens for masked context regions and a fully held-out view. We alternate cross-view prediction with single-view masked prediction on general image and video data to help retain the encoder's pretrained capabilities. After only 9,000 post-training updates on a single GPU, MV-JEPA improves cross-view instance retrieval and coarse cross-camera moment retrieval on various datasets, with further gains in zero-shot action-phase retrieval and frozen-feature classification on AE2. Frozen-probe evaluations further indicate that post-training largely preserves motion recognition, image recognition, and dense semantic segmentation performance on the evaluated benchmarks. Together, these results demonstrate that multi-view predictive post-training can strengthen instance and coarse temporal correspondence in JEPA video encoders without modifying the encoder architecture or single-view inference interface.
  </details>

- **[What a Calibrated Probability Hides: Belief-Level Post-Training for Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25168)**  `arXiv:2611.25168`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinye Li, Zirui Song, Huaxing Liu, Xiang Wang, Shuai Li, Zixiang Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A calibrated decision model can predict how often each answer is chosen. The same prediction can reflect disagreement among people who are confident in their choices or uncertainty among people who share a belief. Disagreement calls for identifying whose perspective applies, while uncertainty calls for gathering more evidence. Standard post-training scores a single prediction against one observed choice at a time, learning the pooled distribution without distinguishing these two sources of uncertainty. We introduce Belief-Grouped Policy Optimization (BGPO). The policy proposes a list of beliefs, estimates their prevalence, and predicts a choice distribution for each. During training, BGPO uses Bayes' rule to estimate how likely each belief was to generate an observed choice. These estimates weight the choice's contribution to each belief's log-score reward. To discover rare beliefs, the policy also proposes lists after seeing a few observed choices and retains them only if they predict held-out choices well. At inference, the policy uses only the question and combines the belief-specific distributions by prevalence to produce a calibrated aggregate prediction. On benchmarks covering opinions, conversation safety, emotions elicited by artwork, and photo aesthetics, BGPO achieves the lowest conditional, worst-group, and overall Jensen-Shannon divergence across model initializations. It improves conditional divergence on both text and image tasks. On a synthetic image task with known beliefs, BGPO recovers the underlying groups more accurately than baselines trained with belief labels.
  </details>

- **[TALD: Transferability-Aware Logit Disentanglement for Cross-Modal Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25126)**  `arXiv:2611.25126`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hui Li, Pengfei Yang, Xinyi Feng, Junhong Liu, Tianyang Zheng, Hui Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-modal knowledge distillation can incur negative transfer when source-modality supervision conflicts with the target modality's discriminative characteristics. We propose TALD, a two-stage framework for Transferability-Aware Logit Disentanglement in class-aligned logit space. Guided by task-dependent self-benefiting and cross-modal exclusivity criteria, a Residual Disentangler decomposes logits from a teacher and an independently trained target-modality reference student into modality-general and modality-specific components. A Sample-Adaptive Modulator regulates decomposition constraints and sample-specific fusion weights. The learned modality-general components are then adaptively fused to supervise the final student. Across five benchmarks spanning vision, audio, and text, TALD achieves the largest mean accuracy gain and highest positive-transfer frequency among the compared methods. Across ten transfer directions with five runs each, TALD improves over training without distillation in 84% of evaluations, compared with at most 70% for the baselines, while offering a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. We plan to release the code.
  </details>

- **[EgoPass-Bench: Identifying Objects That May Affect the Wearer's Passage in Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24995)**  `arXiv:2611.24995`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongyu Wang, Chuhan Li, Congjin Xu, Xuandong Zhao, Hua Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding an egocentric environment requires reasoning about how surrounding entities affect the observer's actions. Existing evaluations of multimodal foundation models (MFMs) examine observer localization, motion trajectories, and action feasibility, but leave the discovery and interpretation of evolving observer-entity interactions insufficiently assessed. We introduce EgoPass-Bench, a benchmark for observer-relative interaction understanding in real-world egocentric video. EgoPass-Bench comprises 284 manually reviewed evaluation clips recorded using Ray-Ban Meta (Gen 2) smart glasses and 2,201 distinct questions across five tasks assessing motion perception, interaction judgments about specified entities, and relevant-entity discovery without target cues. Our evaluation of eight frame-based and six native-video configurations reveals a pronounced discrepancy between motion perception and interaction understanding. The strongest evaluated model, GPT-6 Astra, correctly recognizes object motion in 91.37% of 394 comparisons involving the same targets, but correctly identifies changes in passage constraints in only 66.50%. Across all 574 entity-discovery questions, it trails the two-person human baseline by $23.65 \pm 7.45$ percentage points (paired 95% CI), while exceeding the always-empty baseline (36.76%) by at least 18.9 percentage points. Further analysis reveals frequent inclusion of entities that neither pose a plausible physical conflict nor constrain passage, showing that accurate motion recognition does not ensure reliable relevant-entity discovery.
  </details>

- **[Look Closer, Look Back, or Wait for Agentic Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24267)**  `arXiv:2611.24267`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Li, Zhitao Gao, Lingling Zhang, Wenxuan Huang, YiCheng Xiao, Chen Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video models must answer questions about a live video under strict memory and latency limits. Feeding only the latest few frames keeps present perception sharp, whereas adding past frames dilutes attention on the current scene and triggers hallucinations. Recent cascade methods retrieve past frames when uncertain, but they still fail when current frames are blurry or when decisive future clues have not appeared yet. We introduce ActiveForager, a streaming video agent that maintains a clean short working window and gathers extra evidence only on demand. For each query, the agent either answers directly, zooms into high-resolution recent frames, recalls past events from memory, or waits for upcoming frames. When recalling history, a shared-weight sub-agent verifies candidate clips in isolation and returns a compact evidence summary. A stream-decoupled attention mask then keeps historical evidence from interfering with present visual tokens. We train this policy using counterfactual information gain, which rewards a tool call only when the gathered evidence makes the true answer more likely and prevents tool collapse across all actions. Across Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B on OVO-Bench, ActiveForager improves real-time perception, historical recall, and proactive response at once, lifting overall accuracy on Qwen3-VL-8B from 47.6% to 65.9%. It also doubles zero-shot accuracy on the multi-hop Video-Holmes benchmark while adding minimal visual tokens per query.
  </details>

- **[Do Thinking VLMs Need to Keep Looking at Long Videos? Write It Down, Then Look Away](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24259)**  `arXiv:2611.24259`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Li, Zhitao Gao, Lingling Zhang, Heng Wang, Chengqian Ma, WenHao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) reason about long videos over hundreds to thousands of tokens. Every decoding step re-reads the key-value (KV) cache of the entire video. Must the model keep looking while it reasons? In LookAway, the model first writes timestamped notes on what the question needs and then puts the video away. Once the notes close, the inference engine hides the video's KV cache for the rest of the response. Across both hybrid and pure-attention VLMs, dependence on the video is concentrated early in the response. On Qwen3.6-27B, writing notes preserves accuracy better than free reasoning at the same withdrawal step. Replacing them with another video's notes harms accuracy, whereas keeping the video visible alongside the model's own notes adds no significant benefit. Without additional training, LookAway nearly matches full-video thinking accuracy at 0.12-0.13 times its modeled decode cost. To shorten this initial look, we fine-tune the model on its own correct LookAway responses. Reinforcement learning then rewards correct answers while penalizing KV reads. The trained model puts the video away after a median of 66-68 tokens. Across three held-out benchmarks, it writes more tokens than an identically trained full-video model yet reads only 27-35% as many KV positions at matched accuracy.
  </details>

- **[PCRG: Training-Free Pruning and Caching of Reference Tokens for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22717)**  `arXiv:2611.22717`  `cs.CV`  
  _Siyuan Wang, Haoran Li, Yi Yue, Tak L. Fung, Quan Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-to-video diffusion transformers pack the variational autoencoder (VAE) latents of all references and the generated tokens into one full-attention sequence, in which reference tokens make up about half. Because attention cost grows quadratically with sequence length, these tokens take up an even larger share of the attention computation, yet most of them are static, smooth or irrelevant to the output. To accelerate reference-conditioned generation, we propose pruning and caching of reference tokens for video generation (PCRG), a training-free, plug-and-play method that uses two signals the model already computes to assign each reference token to one of three tiers, namely pruned, cached or kept. The first is a low-level redundancy r from VAE latents, since a token whose latent barely changes over time or across its neighbors adds little new content. The second is a high-level importance g from the vision-language-model (VLM) conditioner, where the attention that the prompt and any first or last frames pay to a token tells whether the output needs to read it. Tokens that are not redundant are kept, and redundant tokens are cached if important and pruned otherwise. Pruned tokens leave the sequence before the first step with all rotary position embedding positions unchanged. Cached tokens have their keys and values computed once and reused, and kept tokens are processed as usual. On MiniMax-H3, already distilled to 8 timesteps and quantized to int8, PCRG is 1.57x faster end to end on our V2V-Bench and 1.49x faster on the public VACE-Benchmark, while the quality of VBench drops by only 0.24% and 0.17%, respectively. PCRG shortens the sequence rather than changing how attention is computed or which steps are run, so it also stacks on step caching and sparse attention. On V2V-Bench it reaches 1.86x with TeaCache, 1.82x with Spark at nearly unchanged fidelity and 2.15x with Veda.
  </details>

- **[RICapBench: A Fine-Grained Visual Understanding Benchmark Dataset for Vision-Language Models in Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16413)**  `arXiv:2611.16413`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xue Wang, Yubin Xu, Kun Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in vision-language models (VLMs) have improved remote sensing image analysis and supported geospatial decision-making. However, fine-grained understanding of remote sensing imagery remains challenging for VLMs. Large variations in object scale, complex geospatial relations among instances, and limited visual detail hinder precise instance localization, visible attribute identification, and spatial relation understanding, potentially leading to descriptions that lack visual grounding. Existing datasets and benchmarks insufficiently assess end-to-end instance localization and captioning without target-specific textual cues. To support this evaluation, we introduce RICapBench, a dataset and benchmark that unifies instance localization and fine-grained captioning in remote sensing. RICapBench contains 245,466 instance-level annotations and 498,005 spatial relation tuples spanning general remote sensing, UAV remote sensing, and dense small-object scenarios. Each annotation pairs an object's oriented bounding box with a natural language description of its visible attributes and spatial relations to surrounding instances and environmental elements. To evaluate the accuracy and completeness of spatial relation captioning, we propose a remote sensing spatial relation parser and the RelCap-F1 metric. We combine RelCap-F1 with instance localization and attribute captioning metrics into a composite metric, RICScore. Our evaluation of 15 advanced open-source and closed-source VLMs identifies instance localization as a major bottleneck for these models. LocateAnything achieves the highest RICScore of 31.68, but scores lower on spatial relation captioning than other task-fine-tuned open-source VLMs. Code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WorldDraft: If Code Builds the World, Can Video Models Just Render It?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16217)**  `arXiv:2611.16217`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiyu Hou, Wen-Jie Shu, Harold Haodong Chen, Weilin Ruan, Chenfei Liao, Liuzhou Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal coding agents decouple 4D world construction from visual generation, repurposing video models from world generators toward generative renderers. This division of labor raises a fundamental question: if code builds the world, can video models just render it? A reliable generative renderer hinges on two complementary criteria: (i) world preservation, retaining prescribed 4D kinematics and camera paths; and (ii) visual realization, persistently binding text-specified target attributes over time. To evaluate this capability, we introduce WorldDraft, a benchmark of $432$ strictly paired test cases across $48$ executable 4D world families spanning six dynamic challenge categories. Under a $3 \times 3$ factorial design, WorldDraft orthogonally crosses draft granularity (abstract proxy to previs) and appearance alignment (neutral to conflicting) under identical dynamics. Extensive evaluations across $12$ representative models reveal that: (I) world preservation and visual realization remain in stark operational tension; (II) richer draft scaffolding anchors structure but paradoxically impairs appearance fidelity; and (III) conflicting source cues trigger severe source leakage (up to $64\%$), exposing fragile cross-modal disentanglement. To mitigate these bottlenecks, we propose WorldDraft-Primer, a plug-and-play visual prompting strategy that consistently improves both objectives across frozen video renderers. Together, WorldDraft and WorldDraft-Primer lay a foundation for bridging programmatic 4D simulation with video generation, advancing physically grounded generative rendering.
  </details>

- **[DailyWildfireCAN: A Dataset and Benchmark for Next-Day Wildfire Spread Prediction in Canada](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14207)**  `arXiv:2611.14207`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mahacine Ettahri, Hager Radi Abdelwahed, Toluwaleke Olutayo, Daniel Aloise, Gaétan Marceau Caron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wildfires represent a critical ecological and socio-economic challenge in Canada, where vast boreal forests and a warming climate have amplified the frequency and severity of extreme fire seasons. Predicting next-day wildfire spread can support fire management, but requires capturing complex interactions among weather, fuels, and terrain. Deep learning has emerged as a promising approach to learning these relationships from heterogeneous environmental data, yet its development and evaluation depend on representative datasets. Existing deep learning research on wildfire spread relies predominantly on US-focused datasets, leaving Canadian fire environments underrepresented. To address this gap, we present DailyWildfireCAN, a multi-modal, multi-temporal dataset and benchmark for predicting next-day wildfire spread across Canada. The dataset tracks continuous fire progression over multi-day events at a high spatial and temporal resolution of 90-m and 24-hour, respectively. This resolution preserves fine-scale information relevant to fire-edge geometry, local fuel variations, and terrain effects, supporting research toward improved daily wildfire spread forecasting. DailyWildfireCAN covers 2,440 wildfire events across eight fire seasons 2017-2024 and integrates 33 features across diverse modalities, including topography, meteorology, land cover, vegetation and forest structure, human influence indices, and satellite imagery. We establish a comprehensive benchmark comparing mono-temporal and multi-temporal prediction models, accompanied by a multi-dimensional evaluation framework assessing robustness across wildfire characteristics, geographic regions, and temporal conditions, spatial prediction quality, and sensitivity to reference label variations.
  </details>

- **[From Game Generation to Game Playing: Fully Automated Ability Training for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11136)**  `arXiv:2611.11136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Changran Liu, Qi Wu, Wenchao Ma, Sharon X. Huang, Haomiao Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training vision-language-action (VLA) models to follow gameplay instructions across diverse games is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. We introduce a framework that automatically generates game environments and expert demonstrations. An LLM-powered agent uses game-building tools to construct executable environments and tasks targeting specified skills. Programmatic experts then use privileged simulator state to generate aligned instructions, rendered observations, and keyboard and mouse actions. We train our game policy, Script2Play, by post-training a 4B vision-language model with behavior cloning on 150.3 hours of synthetic gameplay in Minecraft and Roblox, without human gameplay demonstrations or reinforcement learning. Evaluation on multiple public game benchmarks demonstrates that Script2Play follows gameplay instructions effectively and generalizes across game engines, highlighting the effectiveness of targeted synthetic supervision.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Brain MRI Inpainting with 2.5D Unconditional Flow Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08983)**  `arXiv:2610.08983`  `cs.CV`  
  _Arnela Hadzic, Franz Thaler, Simon Johannes Joham, Martin Urschler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative inpainting of brain MRI volumes is essential for synthesizing healthy tissue in pathological regions, improving the accuracy and reliability of automated downstream brain analysis applications such as image registration, brain extraction, and segmentation. However, standard 3D approaches are computationally prohibitive, while efficient 2D slice-wise methods suffer from severe inter-slice discontinuities. Furthermore, traditional models rely on conditional training, requiring task-specific learning of masked inputs. We propose a zero-shot brain MRI inpainting framework utilizing 2.5D unconditional flow priors to capture spatial context along the superior-inferior axis without the overhead of full 3D convolutions. During training, our flow matching model learns the joint distribution of adjacent axial slice triplets, modeling the manifold of healthy brain anatomy while explicitly excluding pathological regions from the loss function. At inference, the model processes the input triplets autoregressively along the depth axis. We employ the Restora-Flow solver to constrain the unconditional prior using the input mask, achieving accurate zero-shot inpainting. Evaluations show our 2.5D strategy resolves the structural discontinuities of 2D baselines, synthesizing plausible healthy tissue while maintaining volumetric consistency across the axial, sagittal, and coronal planes. As a final step, we generate and average an ensemble of multiple stochastic reconstructions to form the final prediction. Quantitative results benchmarked on the official BraTS 2026 Inpainting Challenge validation set demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed approach, yielding an SSIM of 0.816 $\pm$ 0.112, MSE of 0.007 $\pm$ 0.005, and PSNR of 22.923 $\pm$ 4.343. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RevisitQA: Short Videos Are Not Easy under Dense Revisits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30981)**  `arXiv:2609.30981`  `cs.CV`  
  _Siru Zhong, Shenghan Tan, Rihong Yan, Xiaohui Lv, Yuzheng Zhuang, Shuai Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video question answering benchmarks have increasingly focused on longer recordings, yet short videos can also be challenging when scenes are revisited frequently. As a camera repeatedly leaves and returns to the same workspace, objects may change out of view, making earlier observations outdated or misleading. Understanding such videos requires models to track evolving states across repeated visits rather than simply retrieve past observations. To study this challenge, we introduce RevisitQA, a benchmark containing 2,577 questions across 622 episodes with a median duration of 44.8 seconds, drawn from five real-world egocentric domains and two simulated environments. RevisitQA covers six question types and evaluates models at specific query times, requiring them to answer from the observed history or abstain when it is insufficient. Across 15 offline and seven streaming video LLMs, the median model correctly answers only 55.5% of answerable questions and abstains on just 30.3% of unanswerable ones. Removing earlier frames further reveals that historical observations help answer questions but can also make models less likely to abstain when answers remain unresolved. These findings highlight dense episodic revisits as an important and unresolved challenge for video question answering. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DF26: We Cannot Tell Fake From Real Anymore](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07369)**  `arXiv:2609.07369`  `cs.CV`  
  _Severyn Shykula, Andrii Yermakov, Ivan Samarskyi, Dmytro Mishkin, Jan Cech, Anastasiia Mishchuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce DF26, a novel benchmark for detecting AI-generated videos containing fully synthetic clips produced by recent text-to-video and image-to-video models. The videos capture single-person public-speaking scenarios, spanning direct-to-camera recordings, official statements, and studio interviews - 271 real and 2,420 synthetic videos generated by seven modern video models. The study on DF26 shows that human accuracy in detecting AI-generated videos is 52.6% while recognizing 76.0% of real ones, and that state-of-the-art deepfake detectors, using the current evaluation setup, perform close to random chance. Our results highlight the limitations of current evaluation protocols and motivate the need for benchmarks that explicitly measure robustness to modern generative model distribution shifts. DF26 can be accessed atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[FuncRoom-Agent: Sequential Feed-Forward 3D Functional Indoor Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29519)**  `arXiv:2608.29519`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Feng, Zhi Zuo, MingJian Liang, Jingyu Hu, Xiaowei Hu, Liupengfei Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Function-Room Generation, a new indoor 3D scene generation setting that creates rooms supporting explicit functional goals rather than merely visually plausible layouts. Existing agentic and executable methods improve controllability, but often depend on costly test-time generate--evaluate--revise loops, making functional room generation slow and computationally expensive. We address this challenge with three technical contributions. First, we design a recursive domain-specific language to effectively organize the hierarchical object compositions required by functional rooms, from room structure and major furniture to dense support-surface and nested small objects. It represents rooms as staged executable programs with explicit geometric and functional relations. Second, we propose a sequential feed-forward scene construction framework that distills recursive construction traces into a scene construction expert. At inference time, the expert writes executable DSL code stage by stage, and a deterministic executor directly instantiates each stage without teacher agents, online critics, or iterative repair. Third, we introduce ScenePRM, an execution-grounded process reward framework that improves the expert through reinforcement learning with functional, geometric, relational, and future-constructability feedback. We further establish a function-oriented benchmark and show state-of-the-art performance on both general indoor scene generation and function-room generation, achieving stronger functional completeness, relation correctness, geometric executability, and generation efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Reason in the Words You Speak: Idiolectal Paraphrasing Off-Policy Traces for Reasoning Distillation in VideoLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26684)**  `arXiv:2608.26684`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ji Soo Lee, Jinyoung Park, Seohyun Lee, Jongha Kim, Joonmyung Choi, Jinsung Yoon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent large language models achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, where reinforcement learning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has emerged as a leading paradigm for optimizing models on self-generated trajectories. However, the on-policy nature of GRPO bounds the model to the reasoning skills it can already produce, restricting to learn more advanced capabilities. Prior works inject privileged reasoning traces from a stronger teacher policy to guide training, yet these traces are inherently out of distribution with respect to the student policy. We observe that this mismatch between on-policy and off-policy causes gradient clipping on semantically critical reasoning tokens, ultimately rewarding correct answers while leaving the reasoning that justifies them unlearned. Hence, we propose \textbf{Echo-GRPO}, a framework that lets the model reason in the words it speaks. Rather than imitating low-probability privileged traces from the teacher model, Echo-GRPO rewrites them into the student policy's own \textit{idiolect}, that is, its own characteristic vocabulary and expression patterns, while preserving their semantics via Dual-Reference Decoding. We instantiate this framework as \textbf{VideoEcho-R1} for video reasoning distillation, achieving consistent improvements across three multimodal LLM backbones and five benchmarks. Finally, we show that our idiolectal paraphrasing is a plug-in module that consistently improves both RL and supervised fine-tuning frameworks for reasoning distillation, demonstrating that policy-aligned supervision extends beyond GRPO.
  </details>

- **[Depth-Dominant Skeleton Detection for Natural Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16367)**  `arXiv:2608.16367`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chengkun Rao, Xinrong Hu, Min Li, Yangjun Ou, Ziwei Luo, Ye Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing natural-scene skeleton detectors follow an RGB-representation-based feature encoding paradigm: they locate skeletons within the RGB appearance space, in which the skeleton's shape and topology can only be inferred implicitly from entangled color and texture cues. Despite notable progress, this encoding paradigm degrades markedly on complex scenes such as cluttered or occluded ones, where appearance distractors overwhelm the weak structural signal. We observe that depth images are inherently insensitive to color and texture and provide clear regional contours and inter-region spatial relationships, which naturally eases the difficulty of skeleton detection in complex scenes. Motivated by this, we propose for the first time a novel skeleton detection paradigm built on depth-representation-dominant feature encoding, in which depth dominates and RGB serves as the auxiliary, and present a model, DDSkel, under this paradigm. DDSkel adopts an asymmetric encoder design that fuses the feature representation of RGB images into that of depth images, with the RGB branch containing only 23% of the parameters of the depth branch. DDSkel has a simple structure without intricate designs. Nevertheless, with only about 19% of the trainable parameters of the largest state-of-the-art methods, DDSkel attains the highest F-measure scores on four mainstream benchmarks, namely SK506, SK-LARGE, WH-SYMMAX, and SymPASCAL. Extensive experiments suggest that for natural-image skeleton detection, the depth-dominant paradigm is a more suitable choice than the RGB-based paradigm, achieving better performance with substantially fewer parameters.
  </details>

- **[Distilling Physical Priors into Streaming World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07981)**  `arXiv:2608.07981`  `cs.CV`  
  _Liangliang Zhao, Junying Wang, Danni Yang, Yifan Chang, Bin Fu, Yu Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming world models predict future visual states online while maintaining physically coherent dynamics over long horizons. However, their rollouts often violate basic physical constraints. A common approach distills pretrained bidirectional DiTs into few-step causal generators. However, this paradigm suffers from two fundamental limitations: generic bidirectional teachers acquire limited physical priors from visually oriented pretraining, and the limited priors suffer further loss during bidirectional-to-causal distillation. We present PhyS, a three-stage framework for distilling physical priors into streaming world models. To acquire physical priors from real-world interactions, we construct PhyS-120K, a dataset of 120K real-world physical-interaction videos spanning rigid-body dynamics, soft-body deformation, fluid phenomena, and phase transitions. Each video is annotated with structured descriptions of object properties and causal state transitions. Physics-aware supervised fine-tuning injects the physical priors into a bidirectional 14B DiT teacher, which we then distill into a lightweight 1.3B causal DiT for few-step autoregressive streaming generation. Finally, we use online reinforcement learning to incentivize the distilled model to generate physically plausible rollouts and further propose Temporal Credit Routing (TCR) to address temporal credit assignment. TCR evaluates physical consistency over overlapping temporal windows and routes the resulting group-relative advantages to temporally aligned denoising actions. On PhysicsIQ, PhyS improves the Wan2.1-14B teacher by 18.2\% and the Self Forcing, Rolling Forcing, and Causal Forcing by 23.7\%, 14.8\%, and 31.4\%, respectively. Results also improve the physics-aware video benchmarks VideoPhy, VideoPhy2, and PhyGenBench. The dataset, code, and more sample videos are available on our Project Page.
  </details>

- **[GeoMetric: Injecting Metric Geographic Structure into Worldwide Image Geo-Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08918)**  `arXiv:2606.08918`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junchao Cui, Wenqi Shi, Xiangyang Luo, Biru Zhu, Xuanzi Ma, Nan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Worldwide image geo-localization estimates where a photograph was taken on Earth. Existing methods infer location from visual appearance alone, an unreliable cue for geography: near-identical scenes occur on different continents. We present GeoMetric, a three-phase retrieval framework that encodes geographic distance directly in the representation. Phase I trains image, text, and location encoders into a unit hypersphere. The location encoder combines ScaleFusion and GeoAnchorAttention. ScaleFusion projects a coordinate onto an equal-area chart and encodes it with three Fourier bands, fusing them by attention into one multi-scale embedding. GeoAnchorAttention adds a global prior from fixed spherical anchors without mixing samples, so each coordinate embedding is batch-independent. A trimodal contrastive objective aligns images, reverse-geocoded texts, and coordinates, masking semantic and geographic false negatives. Phase II retrieves candidates, each carrying a GPS coordinate and granularity-aware geographic text. Phase III lets a frozen open-source 8B-parameter large multimodal model (LMM) choose the coordinate from those candidates. On four benchmarks, GeoMetric is best in 15 of 20 threshold cells, and ablations show that the components act at different scales: the text encoder carries coarse geographic context, while the location encoder resolves fine-grained distinctions.
  </details>

- **[EVIDENT: Routing MLLM Adaptation through Entity-Grounded Visual Evidence for Cross-Domain Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26104)**  `arXiv:2605.26104`  `cs.CV`  
  _Geo Ahn, Jiwook Han, Youngrae Kim, Joonseok Lee, Jinwoo Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning MLLMs for Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) often improves in-domain performance but degrades sharply under domain shift. In this work, we find that this failure is primarily driven not just by unseen query concepts, but by visual domain shift, which prevents the model from coupling its learned temporal localization knowledge with its inherent entity-attention capability. To address this, we introduce EVIDENT, a parameter-efficient adaptation framework that anchors temporal grounding in the inherent entity-attention of pre-trained MLLMs by routing VTG adaptation through explicit visual entity evidence. EVIDENT consists of three components: (i) an Entity Bottleneck Adapter that transforms dense visual tokens into compact entity-level slots, (ii) an Entity-Binding Distillation loss that instills objectness priors into the semantically unstructured MLLM visual space, guiding each slot to bind to a coherent entity, and (iii) an Entity-to-eVidence gating mechanism that leverages the captured entities as evidence, steering the model to localize moments containing query-relevant entities. Together, these components enable VTG fine-tuning to rely on entity-grounded evidence rather than brittle dataset shortcuts. Experiments on cross-domain VTG benchmarks show that EVIDENT consistently improves out-of-domain robustness while preserving competitive in-domain performance with modest parameter overhead. These results suggest that entity-level grounding is an effective inductive bias for generalizable temporal localization.
  </details>

- **[Eyes on VLM: Benchmarking Gaze Following and Social Gaze Prediction in Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19859)**  `arXiv:2605.19859`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hengfei Wang, Anshul Gupta, Pierre Vuillecard, Jean-Marc Odobez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have rapidly evolved into general-purpose multimodal reasoners with strong zero-shot generalization. In this context, VLMs could greatly benefit the analysis of human gaze and attention, a central task in human behavior understanding that requires reasoning about the physical scene as well as the activity, interactions, and social context. However, the extent to which VLMs can reliably understand human gaze and related attentional behaviors remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present EyeVLM, a systematic evaluation framework for gaze understanding in VLMs across two complementary dimensions: tasks and models. To assess gaze understanding capabilities, we focus on two core tasks. The first, gaze following, i.e., predicting the 2D location where a person is looking, has a geometric and visual processing focus, requiring a precise understanding of the human face, attention direction, 3D scene structure, and spatial grounding of attended targets. The second, social gaze prediction, requires social and relational reasoning over multi-person interactions (e.g., mutual gaze and shared attention), and may benefit more from the LLM semantic reasoning capabilities within VLMs. Regarding models, EyeVLM evaluates these tasks in two ways: a zero-shot setting with a diverse set of state-of-the-art open- and closed-source VLMs, exploring different prompting strategies; and a fine-tuning approach based on task-specific QA pairs, studying the impact of model scale and data scale. As benchmarks, we rely on existing gaze understanding datasets and perform a systematic comparison with state-of-the-art purely visual models. Overall, our results show that current VLMs lack precise gaze understanding capabilities. While standard training helps reduce the gap with visual models, significant improvements are still needed. Code available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[UniCustom: Unified Visual Conditioning for Multi-Reference Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12088)**  `arXiv:2605.12088`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiyan Xu, Qiulin Wang, Wenjie Wang, Yunyao Mao, Xintao Wang, Pengfei Wan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-reference image generation aims to synthesize images from textual instructions while faithfully preserving subject identities from multiple reference images. Existing VLM-enhanced diffusion models commonly rely on decoupled visual conditioning: semantic ViT features are processed by the VLM for instruction understanding, whereas appearance-rich VAE features are injected later into the diffusion backbone. Despite its intuitive design, this separation makes it difficult for the model to associate each semantically grounded subject with visual details from the correct reference image. As a result, the model may recognize which subject is being referred to, but fail to preserve its identity and fine-grained appearance, leading to attribute leakage and cross-reference confusion in complex multi-reference settings. To address this issue, we propose UniCustom, a unified visual conditioning framework that fuses ViT and VAE features before VLM encoding. This early fusion exposes the VLM to both semantic cues and appearance-rich details, enabling its hidden states to jointly encode the referred subject and corresponding visual appearance with only a lightweight linear fusion layer. To learn such unified representations, we adopt a two-stage training strategy: reconstruction-oriented pretraining that preserves reference-specific appearance details in the fused hidden states, followed by supervised finetuning on single- and multi-reference generation tasks. We further introduce a slot-wise binding regularization that encourages each image slot to preserve low-level details of its corresponding reference, thereby reducing cross-reference entanglement. Experiments on two multi-reference generation benchmarks demonstrate that UniCustom consistently improves subject consistency, instruction following, and compositional fidelity over strong baselines.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Time and Space: Decoupled Spatio-Temporal Alignment for Video Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08014)**  `arXiv:2604.08014`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuezhen Tu, Jingyu Wu, Fangyu Kang, Qingpeng Nong, Kaijin Zhang, Chaoyue Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatio-Temporal Video Grounding requires jointly localizing target objects across both temporal and spatial dimensions based on natural language queries, posing fundamental challenges for existing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). We identify two core challenges: \textit{entangled spatio-temporal alignment}, arising from coupling two heterogeneous sub-tasks within the same autoregressive output space, and \textit{dual-domain visual token redundancy}, where target objects exhibit simultaneous temporal and spatial sparsity, rendering the overwhelming majority of visual tokens irrelevant to the grounding query. To address these, we propose \textbf{Bridge-STG}, an end-to-end framework that decouples temporal and spatial localization while maintaining semantic coherence. While decoupling is the natural solution to this entanglement, it risks creating a semantic gap between the temporal MLLM and the spatial decoder. Bridge-STG resolves this through two pivotal designs: the \textbf{Spatio-Temporal Semantic Bridging (STSB)} mechanism with Explicit Temporal Alignment (ETA) distills the MLLM's temporal reasoning context into enriched bridging queries as a robust semantic interface; and the \textbf{Query-Guided Spatial Localization (QGSL)} module leverages these queries to drive a purpose-built spatial decoder with multi-layer interactive queries and positive/negative frame sampling, jointly eliminating dual-domain visual token redundancy. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Bridge-STG achieves state-of-the-art performance among MLLM-based methods. Bridge-STG improves average m\_vIoU from $26.4$ to $34.3$ on VidSTG and demonstrates strong cross-task transfer across various fine-grained video understanding tasks under a unified multi-task training regime.
  </details>

- **[How Well Do Segmentation Foundation Models Generalize Across Structural and Functional Medical Imaging?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07643)**  `arXiv:2602.07643`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yichi Zhang, Le Xue, Feiyang Xiao, Wenbo Zhang, Gang Feng, Chenguang Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Segmentation foundation models offer reusable tools for universal medical-image segmentation, but their development and evaluation have largely centered on structural CT and MRI imaging. Functional imaging such as PET provides complementary metabolic information yet remains less studied and validated. Moreover, existing evaluations often involve different datasets and tasks, making modality-associated variation difficult to distinguish from differences in segmentation targets and data distributions. To enable a more direct comparison, we introduce UMD, a whole-body multi-organ dataset comprising 490 PET/CT and 464 PET/MRI examinations with 13 annotated organs (approximately 675,000 2D slices and 12,000 3D organ annotations). UMD evaluates structural and functional images separately on corresponding organs within the same examination, reducing variation in patient anatomy and target selection. We benchmark the out-of-the-box segmentation capabilities of five representative 3D foundation models on UMD. Overall, most evaluated models show limited PET segmentation performance, and some configurations also yield near-zero overlap on CT or MRI under the evaluated workflows. These findings highlight the need to assess tool applicability across different imaging conditions and identify opportunities for cross-modality generalization. UMD provides a paired data resource and evaluation setting for this investigation.
  </details>

- **[Show, Don't Tell: Morphing Latent Reasoning into Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02227)**  `arXiv:2602.02227`  `cs.CV`  
  _Harold Haodong Chen, Xinxiang Yin, Wen-Jie Shu, Hongfei Zhang, Zixin Zhang, Chenfei Liao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) generation has achieved remarkable progress, yet existing methods often lack the ability to dynamically reason and refine during generation--a hallmark of human creativity. Current reasoning-augmented paradigms most rely on explicit thought processes, where intermediate reasoning is decoded into discrete text at fixed steps with frequent image decoding and re-encoding, leading to inefficiencies, information loss, and cognitive mismatches. To bridge this gap, we introduce LatentMorph, a novel framework that seamlessly integrates implicit latent reasoning into the T2I generation process. At its core, LatentMorph introduces four lightweight components: (i) a condenser for summarizing intermediate generation states into compact visual memory, (ii) a translator for converting latent thoughts into actionable guidance, (iii) a shaper for dynamically steering next image token predictions, and (iv) an RL-trained invoker for adaptively determining when to invoke reasoning. By performing reasoning entirely in continuous latent spaces, LatentMorph avoids the bottlenecks of explicit reasoning and enables more adaptive self-refinement. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LatentMorph (I) enhances the base model Janus-Pro by $16\%$ on GenEval and $25\%$ on T2I-CompBench; (II) outperforms explicit paradigms (e.g., TwiG) by $15\%$ and $11\%$ on abstract reasoning tasks like WISE and IPV-Txt, (III) while reducing inference time by $44\%$ and token consumption by $51\%$; and (IV) exhibits $71\%$ cognitive alignment with human intuition on reasoning invocation.
  </details>

- **[StyleQoRA: Quality-Aware Low-Rank Adaptation for Few-Shot Multi-Style Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11236)**  `arXiv:2511.11236`  `cs.CV`  
  _Cong Cao, Huanjing Yue, Yujie Xu, Xiaodong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, image editing has garnered growing attention. However, general image editing models often fail to produce satisfactory results when confronted with new styles. The challenge lies in how to effectively fine-tune general image editing models to new styles using only a limited amount of paired data and a minimum number of parameters. To address this issue, this paper proposes a novel few-shot multi-style editing framework. For this task, we construct a benchmark dataset that encompasses five distinct styles. Correspondingly, we propose Quality-Aware Low-Rank Adaptation for few-shot multi-style editing (StyleQoRA). Our StyleQoRA can automatically determine the optimal rank for each layer through a novel approach that estimates the importance score of each single-rank component using an image quality metric. To balance specialization and knowledge sharing, we design a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LoRA with hybrid routing in our StyleQoRA, consisting of style-specific routing to prevent cross-style confusion and style-shared routing to capture common transformation patterns. Additionally, we explore the optimal location to insert LoRA within the Diffusion in Transformer (DiT) model and integrate adversarial learning and flow matching to guide the diffusion training process. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed method outperforms existing state-of-the-art approaches with significantly fewer LoRA parameters. Our code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Understanding MoE Decisions Beyond Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26075)**  `arXiv:2611.26075`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhen Xu, Zitao Zhang, Kevin Zhao, Xuefeng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding a language model's decision requires knowing how its internal computations affect its preference between answers. Gradient-based methods estimate this influence by predicting the effect of changing those computations. In mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, replacing one expert can change which experts later layers select. Those switches can alter the answer scores in ways that derivatives do not capture. We introduce a predictor that uses the model's own expert outputs and selection rules to estimate these extra effects, then adds them to a second-order prediction. Across questions from three benchmarks, our method reduces the error in predicting answer-score changes by 60\% on Qwen3.6 and 47\% on Gemma4, relative to a multistep baseline using gradients and second derivatives.
  </details>

- **[ICARE: cross-domain text classification with incremental class-aware representation and distillation learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24959)**  `arXiv:2611.24959`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Son Duong, Truong X. Tran, Hai-Anh Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rise of powerful, fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) necessitates robust natural language inference (NLI) systems.
