These NLI systems ideally train across various tasks, encompassing diverse concepts like sentiment analysis, news classification, and coreference resolution. However, this continual, multi-tasking approach can lead to catastrophic forgetting, where previously learned tasks are lost, especially in domains lacking specific labels. Rehearsing past examples with labels can further exacerbate this issue by causing overfitting, hindering the system's ability to classify new categories. To tackle these challenges, this research proposes ICARE, a novel framework with two key components: an instance selection module and a knowledge distillation method. The former is based on representation learning, which meticulously selects training examples to present to the NLI system. The latter, knowledge distillation with feature and logit-level transfer, facilitates knowledge transfer between tasks. This leverages pruning techniques and optimized objective functions to transfer both essential feature-level and decision-making (logit-level) knowledge, mitigating catastrophic forgetting while adapting to new patterns. Extensive evaluations on benchmark text classification datasets have demonstrated ICARE's effectiveness. The framework achieves a significant improvement in target accuracy and reduction in the ``forgetting metric''. These results solidify ICARE's ability to preserve and leverage learned knowledge across diverse tasks, ultimately enhancing the adaptability and performance of NLI systems. The source code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Are We Grading Properly? Understanding Failure Modes in Medical Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16023)**  `arXiv:2609.16023`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Prithvi Dixit, Pedram Hosseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical evaluation is shifting from static option-based questioning to realistic clinical scenarios with open-ended output modes. Grading these at scale naively, however, is expensive, and rubric-based evaluation has become the dominant scalable alternative. We ask what happens when the rubrics themselves are not airtight, and whether such flaws can be detected and corrected. We apply RIFT, a global rubric failure taxonomy, to three clinical benchmarks (HealthBench Professional, LiveMedBench, and MentalHealthBench), and find failure modes are meaningful: on HealthBench Professional an LLM judge flags 29.6% of criteria as non-atomic and 65.4% as misaligned/rigid, although these rates vary severalfold across five detection judges. Then, we show that these flaws are meaningful and not simply cosmetic. As an example, rewriting bundled criteria of the form "at least one of / all of the following" as equally weighted children and regrading identical responses shifts scores by up to 15.9 percentage points on affected conversations, with disjunctive bundles inflating scores and conjunctive bundles deflating them. Across six answering models and two common graders, these shifts leave the model ranking unchanged, and their net direction depends on how many bundled criteria are penalties. We also find that RIFT can under-detect bundling on clinical rubrics, by an amount that depends on the detection judge: on the same LiveMedBench criteria, judges flag 3.8-15.9% as non-atomic against an estimated 7-19% that are genuinely bundled.
  </details>

- **[MemFolder: Structure-Guided Memory Folding for Efficient Agent Memory Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26427)**  `arXiv:2611.26427`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Xuyang Liu, Chenwen Lu, Junxi Wang, Siyu Ren, Xinying Lin, Siteng Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term agents accumulate large external memory stores as interactions unfold, increasing the costs of storage, retrieval, and response generation. Efficiency is constrained by two coupled factors: a growing record count increases retrieval overhead, while longer retrieved contexts increase prefill computation and decoding-time attention costs. Existing methods prune or consolidate records, but pruning can discard useful evidence, whereas consolidation may produce fewer yet longer retrieval units. We introduce MemFolder, a structure-guided memory folding framework that sequentially reduces record count and context length. The framework comprises two core components that share memory groups, using cross-record structure to guide both evidence consolidation and compact representation. RecordFolder grounds entity mentions, progressively selects representative yet complementary anchors, and combines semantic and entity affinity with temporal chaining to consolidate compatible records into fewer retrieval units. ContextFolder encodes the original records within these groups into compact text and uses verification feedback to revise encodings when key facts cannot be recovered. Experiments on the three text-based memory benchmarks LoCoMo, PersonaMem, and LongMemEval and the two multimodal benchmarks M3-Bench-web and M3-Bench-robot demonstrate the strong performance of our methods. Notably, on Mem0, RecordFolder retains 99.0% of the original average performance across the three text-based benchmarks with 50% fewer records, exceeding the strongest baseline by 1.9 percentage points in performance retention. Combined with ContextFolder, existing record compression methods further shorten their output records while retaining most of their performance.
  </details>

- **[TETHER: Training-Free, Elastic, ToC-Anchored Hierarchy Extraction and Recovery in Design-Driven Long Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24960)**  `arXiv:2611.24960`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Zou, Yan Chen, Jialei Zeng, Xiaorui Zhou, Aixi Zhong, Qi Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Converting complex documents into structured knowledge constitutes the critical infrastructure urgently required for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in downstream research. However, blind to the Table of Contents (ToC) as a macro-structure, existing bottom-up paradigms rely on localized visual cues, inducing heuristic fragility in high-entropy layouts where visual prominence decouples from logical depth; meanwhile, direct LLM extraction lacks spatial grounding, triggering hallucinations in long contexts. To address this, we introduce TETHER, a training-free framework that elevates the ToC from discarded noise to a vital structural prior, enabling robust adaptation across design-driven long documents. TETHER operates via a two-stage neuro-symbolic mechanism: first, it constructs a document skeleton from the raw ToC using a Large Multimodal Model (LMM) empowered by spatio-semantic constraints; second, it employs an anchor-based alignment strategy to tether content blocks to this skeleton, effectively resolving boundary ambiguities. Evaluated across a multilingual benchmark of ESG reports, product manuals, and annual reports, TETHER delivers highly precise, state-of-the-art (SOTA) zero-shot hierarchy recovery, outperforming deep hierarchical recovery models, dedicated parsers, and general-purpose LMMs. Moreover, stress tests highlight TETHER's resilience to sparse omissions and textual noise, demonstrating that its precise semantic alignment is fundamentally anchored in topological and depth fidelity. Crucially, ablations confirm the framework's architectural irreducibility, while achieving 90% token reduction and drastic speedups over end-to-end baselines, establishing a compelling equilibrium between structural precision and deployment scalability.
  </details>

- **[From Geographic Memory to Spatial Generalization in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25703)**  `arXiv:2611.25703`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chen Chu, Zhaonan Wang, Cyrus Shahabi, Hengcai Zhang, Feng Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can associate places with geographic descriptions, but recovering an observed association does not establish an ability to generalize across geographic space. We distinguish geospatial memory, the recovery of location--description associations observed during training, from spatial generalization, the prediction of descriptions for locations in unobserved regions. We investigate whether continuous location representations improve this generalization and encourage geographic knowledge to become spatially organized within an LLM. We frame this organization as a latent spatial field: a learned mapping from locations to descriptions connected through spatial dependencies. Building on a continuous spatial encoder, we project location representations into the model's embedding space and supply them as spatial tokens. We evaluate the resulting models on two newly curated geo-text benchmarks for Los Angeles and London using two complementary assessments. Geospatial captioning tests both memory and spatial generalization by generating descriptions at observed locations and at locations beyond the spatial coverage of the training observations. A separate geospatial grounding model tests whether generated location--description pairs provide useful additional supervision for mapping text to geographic space. Across multiple LLM families, continuous location representations improve captioning at unobserved locations relative to textual coordinates, while their generated descriptions yield modest but consistent improvements in downstream grounding. Layer-wise probing and representation clustering provide additional evidence consistent with spatially organized geographic representations. Together, these findings show the value of evaluating spatial generalization separately from memory and of incorporating continuous spatial representations into language models.
  </details>

- **[STEPQuant: When and Where Errors Matter in Delta-Rule Recurrent State Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38169)**  `arXiv:2609.38169`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bingchen Yao, Haobo Xu, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear attention replaces growing KV caches with fixed-size recurrent states, yet these persistent states can become a substantial memory bottleneck under concurrent serving. Directly quantizing recurrent states to low precision often leads to severe accuracy degradation, as quantization errors propagate through successive state updates. We discover that the impact of these errors depends on two complementary dimensions: temporally, errors in long-lived memory can persist across many decoding steps; spatially, errors in different key rows affect model outputs differently, while state magnitudes vary substantially along both rows and columns. Motivated by these observations, we propose STEPQuant, a spatial-temporal post-training quantization framework for Delta-rule recurrent states. STEPQuant allocates precision according to error magnitude and memory lifetime, and jointly fits key-row and value-column scales based on state distributions and key-row impact on output error. Experiments on Qwen3.8-27B and Kimi-Linear-48B-A3B-Instruct across both long- and short-generation benchmarks show that STEPQuant closely matches FP32-state accuracy under a nominal 6-bit budget and outperforms uniform INT8 in its 4-bit configuration. Integrated into SGLang with optimized GPU kernels, 6-bit STEPQuant achieves over 5x recurrent-state compression and reduces total serving memory by up to 68.7%. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Supportive Token Revealing for Fast Diffusion Language Model Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04236)**  `arXiv:2606.04236`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Giries Abu Ayoub, Mario Barbara, Lluís Pastor-Pérez, Tanja Bien, Aneesh Barthakur, Alaa Maalouf, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete diffusion language models can generate text efficiently by updating multiple masked positions in parallel, but this parallelism introduces a quality-latency trade-off. Aggressive decoding may commit mutually dependent tokens too early, while conservative decoding requires many denoising steps. Existing methods address this tension by deciding which tokens are safe to reveal using confidence or dependency criteria. However, avoiding unsafe commits does not necessarily make the remaining masked sequence easy to decode, since uncertain tokens may depend on masked tokens, creating a bottleneck for denoising steps. We propose AXON, a training-free module that can be added on top of existing parallel decoding strategies for diffusion language models. Rather than replacing the base decoder, AXON monitors the remaining uncertain masked tokens and intervenes only when their current state suggests that additional context is needed. It then shifts the criterion from which tokens are safest to reveal to which confident reveals would best support later denoising. AXON selects anchors, confident masked tokens that uncertain positions attend to, using attention, uncertainty, and confidence signals. Experiments on reasoning and code-generation benchmarks across multiple diffusion language models show that AXON improves the quality-latency trade-off of existing parallel decoders, often reducing the number of function evaluations while maintaining or improving accuracy.
  </details>

- **[PhoneWorld: From Real-App Trajectories to Dynamic and Verifiable Environments for Phone-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29486)**  `arXiv:2605.29486`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Liu, Xin Lai, Junyi Li, Pengyuan Lyu, Chenxin Li, Yiduo Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real applications provide the training setting closest to phone-agent deployment, but are difficult to reset, scale safely, and verify programmatically. Static screenshots and interaction trajectories preserve realistic evidence but cannot generate new experience. We introduce PhoneWorld, a trace-grounded framework that converts such evidence into runnable, resettable, and verifiable Android environments. PhoneWorld induces a usage-weighted interaction skeleton from observed pages, transitions, and state-changing operations; translates it into a behavior-grounded app specification; realizes the specification through an autonomous build--inspect--repair loop; and synthesizes executable tasks with programmatic verifiers. The resulting suite spans 34 consumer-facing apps across 16 domains and supports an audited online benchmark, verified trajectory generation, and online RL through common reset and verification interfaces. Evaluations with diverse general and open-source GUI agents show that PhoneWorld supports reliable end-to-end online interaction and exposes capabilities complementary to AndroidWorld. Controlled SFT experiments further show that PhoneWorld trajectories complement AndroidWorld supervision, transfer across online and offline benchmarks, and become more effective as data volume and app coverage increase. Under a matched RL budget, combining PhoneWorld mock-app rollouts with real-app rollouts improves performance over real-app RL alone on both real-phone tasks and AndroidWorld. Together, these results demonstrate that trace-grounded executable abstraction can bridge realistic mobile behavior and scalable agent learning, turning limited real-app evidence into a growing supply of controllable and verifiable environments for training and evaluation.
  </details>

- **[One Framework to Diagnose Them All: A Stratified Benchmark for Bengali LLM Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26336)**  `arXiv:2611.26336`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Asif Shahriyar Sushmit, Shefayat E Shams Adib, Abdullah Al Jubaer Gem, Ahmed Shafin Ruhan, MD. Nazmuddoha Ansary, Biplab Kumar Sarkar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucination research in low-resource languages fragments easily: each newly named failure mode prompts its own dataset, spending scarce annotators on incomparable resources. We argue for consolidation and test it at community scale. Alikbachan is a Bengali hallucination-detection benchmark stratified along three crossed axes: a human-validated cultural-distance band, six task types, and a five-family, 32-mode hallucination taxonomy. It comprises a harvested 2,516-item test set (Phase 1) and a quota-constructed, gold-labeled 5,000-item set (Phase 2). The stratification is diagnostic. Four zero-shot LLM judges vary by over 30 macro-F1 points across strata, and the item-level predictions of 99 Phase-1 and 32 Phase-2 competition systems reproduce the profile: discourse-level omission defeats judges and most teams alike, whereas only a minority over-flag negative controls. The judges' verdict probabilities rank items well (ROC-AUC 0.74-0.95) but are overconfident (ECE 0.04-0.29). An open competition on Alikbachan drew 222 teams and 3,566 submissions, and a single-prompt 32B judge reaches the 57th percentile. Of the 25 teams in both phases, 24 regress on Phase 2 (mean $-17.6$ points) while every zero-shot judge improves, and Phase-1 rank barely predicts Phase-2 rank (Spearman $\rho=0.35$), so a Phase-1 leaderboard alone would have crowned a different winner.
  </details>

- **[MATE: Mobile Agent Trustworthy Execution for Safe and Efficient Mobile Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25526)**  `arXiv:2611.25526`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Minsuk Jang, Hee-Seon Kim, Hui-Cheol Yu, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile agents on personal smartphones can affect private data, payments, settings, and the foreground screen. We present MATE (Mobile Agent Trustworthy Execution), an execution-layer system that controls the boundary between agent action proposals and device effects without modifying the underlying model. MATE represents each UI primitive as a structured action record that combines the instruction, target app, screen context, primitive, and action fields. Runtime Guardrail (RG) maps this record to an allow, block, or user-approval decision; Predefined Task Routing (PTR) serves recurring requests through verified routes with post-execution screen checks; and a floating interface keeps execution in a compact overlay. On MobileSafetyBench, MATE raises high-risk blocking from 29.7% to 97.0% under GPT-4o. On AndroidWorld, it reduces LLM calls by up to 6x and raises recurring-use task success to 95.7%.
  </details>

- **[Evolve Where It Matters: Coverage-Guided Self-Evolution of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25429)**  `arXiv:2611.25429`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Boyin Tan, Jiadong Zhang, Xiaosong Ma, Chun Jason Xue, Xiaowei Huang, Youcheng Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills package procedural knowledge as natural-language instructions, and a skill helps only when its instructions are correct and properly followed by the agent. Self-evolving agents revise their own skills from the runs they make, but existing methods judge each run as a whole, by its outcome or by where it went wrong, and cannot tell whether a failure comes from a wrong instruction or from one the agent did not follow. Instruction-level coverage was introduced to test skills and labels each instruction of a run as follow, not-follow, or not covered, which separates these causes. We leverage it for skill self-evolution with SkillPlumber, a framework in which an agent evolves its own skills where coverage shows they fall short. A persistent root cause analysis agent turns each label into a revision question and checks each revision against the next run, while a maintainer distills investigations across tasks into a shared strategy library. Across three benchmarks and three executors, SkillPlumber solves 64.7% to 70.5% of the tasks, 25.6 to 30.4 percentage points higher than the initial skill and 8.7 to 14.0 percentage points higher than the strongest baseline, and removing coverage reduces recovered failures by 20.8% on SkillsBench with GPT-5.5.
  </details>

- **[Forget Freely, Grep Later: Lossless, Index-Free Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25123)**  `arXiv:2611.25123`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyan Yang, Taiwei Shi, Jieyu Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents often exceed their context windows, forcing them to discard information that may be needed later. Existing context management strategies rely on summarization, masking, or structured memory, requiring decisions about what to preserve or how to retrieve it before future needs are known. However, these methods can introduce lossy compression, potentially discarding details needed for future tasks. To address this limitation, we introduce GrepMem, a simple, lossless, and index-free memory mechanism built on two components: an append-only plain-text log of the agent's complete history and a regular-expression search tool for on-demand recall. Unlike structured memory systems, GrepMem requires no embeddings, retrieval index, memory extraction, or additional models. We train Qwen3.5-9B with reinforcement learning under five context management strategies and evaluate on multi-question research sessions, long-stream recall, and agent-trajectory question answering. GrepMem achieves 95-97% accuracy on ContextBench, versus at most 21% for lossy baselines, and outperforms SUPO's summarization by 8.8 percentage points on BCP-Multi. GrepMem scales effectively to increasingly long input streams while remaining on the accuracy-token-cost Pareto frontier across all five evaluation tasks. Our results show that a plain-text log and grep provide a simple but competitive approach to long-horizon agent memory, without requiring complex structured memory infrastructure.
  </details>

- **[No-Harm-Jury A Jury-Based Multi-Dimensional Harm Evaluation Framework for Medical AI Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24972)**  `arXiv:2611.24972`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Antonio Puertas Gallardo, Mario Ceresa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present No-Harm-Jury, a jury-based automated evaluation framework that scores AI-generated medical responses across seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions using a panel of five diverse language models as independent jurors. Responses generated by Ministral-14B on 3,000 questions drawn from PubMedQA, MedQA, and MedMCQA are scored by five jurors across dimensions of informational, social, psychological, autonomy, economic, privacy, and epistemic harm; the 151 empty responses (98 MedQA, 53 MedM- CQA) are excluded, leaving 2,849 valid samples. The median aggregation robustly suppresses individual juror biases while maintaining coverage through complementary specialisation. No existing system combines all five prop- erties of No-Harm-Jury: automated scalable evaluation, five diverse LLM jurors, robust me- dian aggregation (with a reliability-weighted extension), seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions, and application to medical QA benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[The ROI of Small Examples: Optimizing In-Context Learning Under Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24971)**  `arXiv:2611.24971`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Anxo Perez, Jorge Gabín, Javier Parapar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) improves large language model performance at inference time without updating model parameters. However, these gains are not free: in practical deployments, longer prompts directly increase inference cost and latency. Under a fixed token budget, prompt construction therefore becomes a resource-allocation problem. While prior work has often focused on how many examples to include in ICL, we study the complementary question of whether the available budget is better spent on a few long demonstrations or on a larger number of shorter ones. We study this aspect in question answering (QA), by organizing candidate demonstrations into length-based bins and constructing prompts from examples within the same length range under fixed budgets. Experiments on two QA benchmarks (\textit{Beat the AI} and \textit{Natural Questions}), using six instruction-tuned LLMs, show that shorter demonstrations are generally the most effective use of prompt space. This effect is especially strong on \textit{Beat the AI}, and remains visible on NQ, although gains are smaller and saturate earlier in the long-context setting. An additional natural language inference experiment on ANLI R3 finds that one of the two shorter bins performs best for five of six models, although longer demonstrations remain competitive. Overall, our results highlight demonstration granularity as an important factor in prompt design.
  </details>

- **[Global Importance Estimation for KV Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24969)**  `arXiv:2611.24969`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Seojin Kim, Noseong Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory consumption of the key-value (KV) cache remains a central bottleneck for long-context LLM inference. We introduce PRKV, a training-free KV eviction method that estimates multi-hop global token importance by applying Personalized PageRank over attention-induced transition graphs. This enables importance propagation beyond recency or instantaneous attention, without modifying the model. We benchmark PRKV on long-context understanding, sparse retrieval, and chain-of-thought reasoning tasks using multiple LLM families. Under moderate to aggressive compression ratios, including up to 90%, PRKV achieves competitive task performance against established eviction baselines while reducing peak memory usage with low end-to-end overhead. These results demonstrate that multi-hop global influence estimation yields a favorable accuracy--memory trade-off for scalable long-context inference.
  </details>

- **[Physics of Language Models: Part 4.2, Canon Layers at Scale where Synthetic Pretraining Resonates in Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24963)**  `arXiv:2611.24963`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyuan Allen-Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Do standard benchmarks see the architectural differences that matter? We study it with 64 from-scratch pretraining runs of Transformers and three linear-model families at 1.3-8B parameters on 1-2T Nemotron-CC tokens, at a cost of roughly 100k USD per 8B run.In academic-scale pretraining (1.3B parameters, 100B tokens), architectural differences can all but disappear when the models are carefully tuned. Instead, at 1-2T tokens they become measurable: Canon layers, which add local token mixing, consistently improve Transformers, saving about 30% of pretraining tokens for the same MMLU accuracy at 8B. We also introduce GLA5 and GDN2, which improve gated linear attention and Gated DeltaNet with low-rank output gates. Surprisingly, with Canon, the simplest linear model, gated linear attention, matches Gated DeltaNet and outperforms Mamba2 even at 8B/1T. Therefore, the extra machinery of Gated DeltaNet and Mamba2 may do little more than mix neighboring tokens.Even at 8B/1T, however, high scores on standard benchmarks can hide weaknesses in basic in-context skills. In particular, linear models that match or beat Transformers on these benchmarks cannot, in fact, reliably retrieve information from a short 500-token context. Moreover, no model, Transformer or linear, reliably beats random guessing on arguably the simplest two-hop question, stated in a few short sentences, even at this scale.These findings $\textbf{challenge traditional architecture research}$ based primarily on standard benchmark scores. In contrast, small synthetic playgrounds, which predicted the architectural differences above at <100M parameters, can reveal what these benchmarks miss. For practitioners, one lesson is to include in-context reasoning examples in pretraining data from an early stage, rather than reserve them for later mid-training, let alone post-training.
  </details>

- **[EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20846)**  `arXiv:2611.20846`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.
  </details>

- **[NL2AGBench: Benchmarking LLM Auto-Formalization for AlphaGeometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28481)**  `arXiv:2608.28481`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Samuel Xiao, Judy Song, Rory Hu, Ziliang Zong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in natural language understanding and mathematical reasoning. However, their ability to translate informal mathematical problems into formal representations remains underexplored. This limitation is particularly important for neuro-symbolic geometry systems such as AlphaGeometry, whose theorem-proving engine requires inputs in a specialized domain-specific language (DSL). Although AlphaGeometry achieves near-IMO gold-medalist performance, manually converting natural-language problems into its formal syntax remains a significant usability bottleneck. To address this challenge, we introduce the Natural Language to AlphaGeometry Benchmark (NL2AGBench), which evaluates LLMs in translating English geometry problems into AlphaGeometry-compatible formal representations. NL2AGBench uses execution-based verification within AlphaGeometry to assess translation quality rather than relying solely on textual similarity. We evaluate ten state-of-the-art open- and closed-source LLMs across multiple parameter scales and analyze executable translation accuracy, syntactic correctness, and error characteristics. Our experiments reveal a substantial performance gap between closed- and open-source models: leading closed-source models achieve executable translation rates above 80%, while even the largest open-source models struggle to consistently preserve geometric constraints and produce valid formalizations. We introduce an error taxonomy distinguishing syntax and logic errors and investigate mitigation strategies, including few-shot prompting, fine-tuning, and human-guided hinting, which yield measurable improvements across multiple model families.
  </details>

- **[MINTEval: Evaluating Memory under Multi-Target Interference in Long-Horizon Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18565)**  `arXiv:2605.18565`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hyunji Lee, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Joykirat Singh, Zaid Khan, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world agents operate over long and evolving horizons, where information is repeatedly updated and may interfere across memories, requiring accurate recall and aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of information. However, existing benchmarks focus on static, independent recall and fail to capture these dynamic interactions between evolving memories. In this paper, we study how current memory-augmented agents perform in realistic, interference-heavy, long-horizon settings across diverse domains and question types. We introduce MINTEval (Long-Horizon Memory under INTerference Evaluation), a benchmark featuring (1) long, highly interconnected contexts with frequently updated information that induces substantial interference, (2) diverse domains (state tracking, multi-turn dialogue, Wikipedia revisions, and GitHub commits), enabling evaluation of domain generalization, and (3) diverse question types that assess robustness to interference, including (i) single-target recall tasks requiring retrieval of a specific target from long contexts, and (ii) multi-target aggregation tasks requiring reasoning over multiple relevant pieces of information. Overall, MINTEval has 15.6k question-answering pairs over long-horizon contexts averaging 138.8k tokens and extending up to 1.8M tokens per instance. We evaluate 7 representative systems, including vanilla long-context LLMs, RAG, and memory-augmented agent frameworks. Across all systems, we observe consistently low performance (avg. 27.9% accuracy), especially on questions requiring aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence. Our analysis shows that performance is primarily limited by retrieval and memory construction. Furthermore, current memory systems struggle to recall and reason over earlier facts that are revised or interfered with by subsequent context, with accuracy degrading as the number of intervening updates increases.
  </details>

- **[dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26275)**  `arXiv:2611.26275`  `cs.CL`  
  _Handa Li, Pengxiang Li, Mingyu Cao, Xilu Wang, Li Shen, Da Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) support parallel generation, yet they often require many denoising steps because only a fraction of token predictions are sufficiently reliable to be committed at each step. Existing acceleration methods primarily optimize confidence learning or commitment policies. However, these approaches operate on the outputs of a single denoising forward pass, in which information from the prompt, previously committed tokens, and uncommitted positions may not yet be fully integrated. Consequently, some predictions remain under-refined when the commitment decision is made, and modifying the commitment policy alone cannot improve their underlying representations. We introduce dLoop, which restructures a pretrained diffusion language model into a Prelude-Loop-Coda architecture and recurrently applies a parameter-shared intermediate block to the same partially masked state. Rather than advancing the denoising process, each additional loop provides further latent computation, allowing information distributed across uncommitted positions to continue interacting and become more fully integrated before commitment. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval show that dLoop achieves the highest or tied-highest accuracy on every benchmark. With tokens per forward pass (TPF) adjusted for loop computation, it outperforms Fast-dLLM in both accuracy and TPF on all four benchmarks and dParallel on three, and is more accurate than d3LLM on all four, while d3LLM attains higher TPF; no compared method surpasses dLoop in both. Mechanistic analyses show that extra loops mainly help on slow, unstable trajectories and that updates at uncommitted positions drive changes in token decisions and later generation. Pre-commitment latent computation thus makes predictions more reliable, offering a route to DLM acceleration distinct from forcing confidence through the training loss.
  </details>

- **[Same Judgement, Different Scores: Numerical Scoring Topology in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26077)**  `arXiv:2611.26077`  `cs.CL`  
  _Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Jiwoo Kum, Minsoo Song, Chanjun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are increasingly used as automated evaluators to reduce the cost and time required for human annotation. However, the role of scoring-scheme design in the reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation remains underexplored. To address this problem, we introduce numerical scoring topology, which characterizes the structural design of a scoring scheme in terms of its scale width, granularity, offset, symmetry, and boundary placement. Building on this definition, we conduct the first systematic study of how numerical scoring topology influences zero-shot LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation across diverse benchmarks and judge models. Our results show that numerical scoring topology systematically affects judge scores, calibration, and robustness, particularly in judgment-intensive evaluations. Shifted and narrow ranges tend to induce some of the largest score deviations across many settings. We further find that stronger and larger judge models generally achieve higher discriminative validity and greater robustness to scoring-topology changes. These findings suggest that numerical scoring topology should be treated as an integral component of LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation and highlight the need for more principled and standardized scoring protocols.
  </details>

- **[Xing-Audio-NVVASR: Scaling and Refining Supervision for Joint Recognition of Speech and Non-Verbal Vocalizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26000)**  `arXiv:2611.26000`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pengyu Xu, Gang Liu, Genliang Zhao, Hongjie Chen, Jian Kang, Jie Lian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint recognition of speech and non-verbal vocalizations (NVVs) is hindered by scarce event-rich training data and inaccurate or missing event labels. We present Xing-Audio-NVVASR, an end-to-end recognizer trained through progressive data mining and iterative supervision refinement. Guided by unified acoustic criteria, two heterogeneous models cross-audit annotations, flagging disagreements for teacher reannotation. To uncover shared omissions that model agreement may conceal, complementary breath detectors screen the entire corpus for human review. Human-confirmed labels accumulate across rounds and are preserved during subsequent refinement. Over five rounds, we refine annotations for 170,000 utterances from public corpora and incorporate 630,000 mined utterances. The resulting corpus of approximately 800,000 utterances is used to train a single 1.7B-parameter model that transcribes speech with inline tags for six NVV classes. Across four human-annotated benchmarks, Xing-Audio-NVVASR ranks first among nine models in both composite score and six-class Macro-F1. It achieves a mean composite score of 64.46, surpassing the strongest comparison model, StepAudio 3 Chat Preview, by 6.03 points, and exceeds the annotation teacher's breathing F1 by 14.40 to 31.57 percentage points. We plan to release the model weights, inference code, four evaluation benchmarks, annotation guidelines, and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[HALTT: A HAte Lexicon Through Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25862)**  `arXiv:2611.25862`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dario Franzese, Farah Benamara, Véronique Moriceau, Viviana Patti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated hate speech detection degrades over time as everyday vocabulary is weaponized into covert slurs while retaining original benign meanings (polysemy retention). Existing lexical semantic change detection (LSCD) frameworks struggle with this phenomenon due to a lack of standardized, sense-delineated diachronic resources. We propose a multi-stage framework to construct and validate a diachronic lexicon of semantically shifted hate speech terms. Filtering > 20,000 candidates, our pipeline combines historical (1770-1964) and modern (2001-2024) corpus frequency grounding, masked definition-level scoring to mitigate LLM temporal bias, and a dual-persona reasoning prompt paradigm. We release HALTT, a grounded lexicon of 19 polysemous terms with literal and derogatory senses, analyze shift typologies, and establish evaluation protocols for downstream diachronic benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[TableRoute: Capability-Aware Routing for Scalable Table Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25853)**  `arXiv:2611.25853`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxiang Wang, Junhao Gan, Jianzhong Qi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Table question answering with large language models remains difficult to scale: tables range from compact to million-token sizes, while questions require precise grounding of columns, values, and computations. Existing methods often specialize in a fixed inference strategy, which is brittle across different table scales and model capabilities. Full-table reading is efficient for compact inputs but suffers from irrelevant context. Table reduction helps long inputs but depends on reliable evidence localization, while execution is required when tables exceed the model context window and remains sensitive to schema and value grounding errors. We propose TableRoute, a capability-aware routing framework with evidence-conditioned inference for scalable TableQA. TableRoute first generates validated question--table evidence and identifies the required operations, then routes each instance among these three strategies according to serialized table length and calibrated estimates of how reliably the base model handles these operations. The validated evidence supports the selected route by grounding columns, values, and conditions. Across WikiTQ, WTQ-L, BirdQA, and TableBench, TableRoute consistently improves accuracy through complementary routing and evidence conditioning across table scales, while reducing online token cost. Code will be made available upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Rubric Semantics or Numerical Priors? Auditing Scale-Direction Bias in LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25588)**  `arXiv:2611.25588`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chanwoo Kim, Doyun Kim, Minsoo Song, Sihyeon Lee, Chanjun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent benchmark evaluations increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) as automatic judges. This paradigm assumes LLMs evaluate based on the rubric's semantic meaning rather than its numeric scale. We question this assumption by introducing semantic faithfulness: the principle that semantically equivalent rubrics must yield consistent scores. To quantify this, we propose a scale-reversal probe that inverts score anchors without altering evaluation criteria. Furthermore, we introduce floor normalization to calibrate reversal disagreement against intrinsic model variability, enabling fair cross-model comparisons. Experiments across five meta-evaluation benchmarks with seven diverse LLM judges spanning open-weight and proprietary models reveal widespread violations of semantic faithfulness, showing that raw reversal agreement alone is misleading. Finally, we demonstrate that bidirectional anchor averaging reduces dependence on any single anchor direction without compromising alignment with human judgments. Our findings emphasize that trustworthy LLM judges must reliably follow rubric semantics, not just correlate with human ratings.
  </details>

- **[PILAPS: A Bilingual Benchmark for Evaluating Pragmatic Inference across Phenomena and Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25459)**  `arXiv:2611.25459`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiani Cao, Anna Korhonen, Chen Cecilia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work reports strong pragmatic performance by large language models (LLMs) in English, yet whether this performance generalises across languages and evaluation settings remains unclear. This question is increasingly important as LLMs are deployed across languages, cultures, and conversational contexts. In this paper, we first introduce PILAPS, a bilingual English/Mandarin Chinese benchmark covering eight pragmatic phenomena across purpose-written and dialogue-derived items, with both culturally adapted and independently sourced data. We then evaluate eight LLMs from four families across a range of model sizes and prompting strategies. Our results reveal substantial variation across models and pragmatic phenomena: open-source models continue to struggle particularly with humour, while cross-linguistic performance varies with model origin. Our error analysis shows that, compared with human participants, models are more prone to literal interpretations in conversational contexts. Theory-of-Mind prompting lowers the literal share of errors, whereas Chain-of-Thought prompting slightly raises it. Together, these findings demonstrate that strong pragmatic performance in one evaluation setting does not necessarily generalise across languages or conversational contexts.
  </details>

- **[PAIR-MED: Policy-reward Alternation for Iterative Refinement in Multilingual Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25446)**  `arXiv:2611.25446`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fan Gao, Xiaohang Xu, Lang Huang, Nan Lu, Irene Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models demonstrate strong medical question-answering capabilities, yet reasoning performance remains uneven across languages. Process reward models (PRMs) provide step-level feedback for improving reasoning, but a fixed PRM may become increasingly misaligned with the trajectory distribution of an evolving policy during reinforcement learning, thereby reducing the reliability of its supervision. We propose a policy--reward alternating training framework that jointly adapts a medical PRM and optimizes a multilingual reasoning policy. Following supervised initialization, the current policy generates multilingual trajectories to update the PRM, which then provides process-level rewards for the next policy update. Medical step supervision maintains the PRM's medical foundation, while process, outcome, and language-related rewards guide policy learning. We develop multilingual medical PRMs (PAIR-PRM) and reasoning models (PAIR-MED) at 8B and 9B scales across 12 languages. On the medical subsets of Global-MMLU and MMLU-ProX, our models improve average accuracy by 3.01--7.47% over their corresponding Qwen backbones. On CUREMED-BENCH, PAIR-MED-9B improves the joint language consistency and logical accuracy metric by 12.88% and 7.45% over baselines for in-domain and out-of-domain languages. Further analyses examine PRM adaptation, multilingual reasoning quality, and the contributions of individual training components.
  </details>

- **[RoCoKV: Accelerating LLM Inference Serving through Uncertainty and Redundancy Aware KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25433)**  `arXiv:2611.25433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Payman Behnam, Elaheh Sobhani, Saad Amin, Yahya Hassan, Daniyal Khan, Biswadeep Chakraborty, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KV cache compression is critical for large reasoning models, where most of the cost comes from long generated reasoning traces rather than the input prompt. These traces often contain redundant and low-utility tokens due to looping, self-verification, and corrections. However, such tokens may still receive high attention scores, making it difficult to remove them without harming the reasoning process. We propose RoCoKV, a KV cache compression method designed for large reasoning models. RoCoKV introduces two key components. First, uncertainty-guided adaptive pruning adjusts the compression strength based on the model's confidence, allocating more memory during uncertain phases and applying stronger pruning when the model is confident. Second, redundancy-aware token selection reduces the impact of tokens that are highly similar to recent reasoning content, encouraging the model to retain more informative and diverse tokens. Beyond the algorithm, we co-design RoCoKV with the serving stack: we develop fused three-phase CUDA decode kernels and integrate them into vLLM as a drop-in plugin that preserves its paged KV layout and CUDA graph capture, turning algorithmic savings into real end-to-end speedups. Extensive experiments across three reasoning benchmarks and two reasoning models demonstrate that RoCoKV consistently improves reasoning accuracy under constrained KV cache budgets, achieving higher accuracy compared to state-of-the-art KV cache compression methods. For DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B in vLLM, RoCoKV achieves a 1.27X speedup and 38% higher throughput on AIME25, the most challenging evaluated reasoning benchmark, and a 1.43X speedup and 45% higher throughput on the long synthetic benchmark, compared with Full-KV.
  </details>

- **[ChronoDossier: Long-Form Personas as Temporal Memory Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25407)**  `arXiv:2611.25407`  `cs.CL`  
  _Noah Sturgill, Jinho D. Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents rely on stored records to recover a person's state as facts change. Reliable answers depend on how facts are recorded, retrieved, and presented to the reader. We treat a long-form persona dossier as a memory representation of a known latent state, an invariant identity plus a timeline of facts, routines, and decision rules tied to life phases. We present ChronoDossier, a benchmark of 311 synthetic dossiers of 1k-10k words written by seven generators from dated timelines. Intrinsic metrics evaluate the records against their intended states; extrinsic metrics test recovery by five readers on a seeded 32-persona GPT-5.1 panel balanced across lengths (520 rated turns per condition). The linked states, records, evidence packets, and answers support tests of construction and evidence delivery. In a contextual-stress control, tag-scoped writer inputs remove detected out-of-scope anchor copying. Frozen questions authored on staged dossiers transfer less reliably to one-pass dossiers of the same states. In matched evidence controls, whole sections repair 29 of 32 GPT-5.1 and 25 of 30 Sonnet 4.6 failures labeled as packaging, compared with 2 of 8 and 1 of 8 answer-formation failures. Attribution uses separate key-based and key-free support measures, with model disagreements retained. Length remains confounded with persona and question composition; the observed between-bucket widening depends on excerpt construction. The benchmark supports offline evaluation against known facts. Memory acquisition from interaction and validation against real users remain outside its scope.
  </details>

- **[LipSpell: A Language-Independent Phonetic Scoring Framework for Spelling Correction Combining Distinctive-Feature Edit Distance, Keyboard Geometry, and Corpus Frequency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24285)**  `arXiv:2611.24285`  `cs.CL`  
  _Leonardo Araujo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic spelling correction must handle both keyboard typographical slips and cognitive or phonetic misspellings where writers approximate a word's pronunciation. Conventional spell checkers rely either on orthographic edit distance and keyboard proximity--which struggle when phonetic errors diverge by multiple characters--or on English-centric phonetic hashes such as Soundex and Metaphone. We present LipSpell, a unified, language-independent phonetic scoring engine paired with swappable language-specific G2P frontends, keyboard maps, and lexicons integrating: (1) a precomputed |P|x|P| phone-distance matrix from universal IPA distinctive features, (2) physical keyboard geometry, and (3) corpus unigram frequency priors, accelerated by a two-stage symmetric-delete and seven-class phonetic-code index. Across six English benchmarks (including 1,000 L2 errors from TOEFL-Spell), LipSpell outperforms Norvig and Hunspell on every dataset and beats Aspell on Norvig1 (78.52% vs. 75.56%), Holbrook (32.00% vs. 26.67%), and TOEFL-Spell (77.30% vs. 72.80%). Evaluated across six languages spanning five branches (Germanic, Romance, Slavic, Semitic, Turkic), three families (Indo-European, Afro-Asiatic, Turkic), and three scripts, LipSpell achieves strong fixed-weight transfer on Brazilian Portuguese (73.00% Top-1 on n=500 Wikipedia without NFD tolerance and 75.80% with NFD vs. 66.40% Norvig, 64.20% Hunspell, 64.00% Aspell) and Russian Cyrillic (85.67% vs. 78.50% Norvig). On 50/50 clean non-word splits with lexicon cleaning and weak-character indels, tuning yields strong gains over Norvig in Portuguese (86.97% vs. 76.75%), Turkish (81.67% vs. 79.05%), Hebrew Abjad (74.59% vs. 62.30%), and French (74.53% vs. 67.92%), while fixed weights remain optimal for English (80.37%) and Russian (85.67%).
  </details>

- **[Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16843)**  `arXiv:2611.16843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.
  </details>

- **[MedPsy: State-of-the-Art Small Medical Language Models for Efficient Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02011)**  `arXiv:2611.02011`  `cs.CL`  
  _Davide Vitabile, Alexandro Buffa, Akshay Nambiar, Amril Nazir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical language models promise expert clinical support, but the strongest open-source LLMs remain too large for private edge-device deployment, while compact medical models still trail substantially on knowledge-intensive and real-world healthcare tasks. Reasoning models that fit edge memory budgets can produce long reasoning traces, increasing sequential decoding, end-to-end latency, energy consumption, and KV-cache memory pressure. We present MedPsy, a family of text-only 1.7B and 4B medical language models designed for edge deployment, targeting this accuracy-generation-efficiency trade-off. Our recipe combines a synthetic medical data pipeline over biology, medicine, and health seeds with chain-of-thought targets from a 235B medical-focused LLM teacher; a four-stage post-training curriculum comprising two SFT stages followed by two RL stages with hard-sample mining; and an edge deployment evaluation spanning multiple GGUF quantization variants and real-device inference on laptop and smartphone hardware. On seven closed-ended medical benchmarks, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-1.5-4B by 19.34 points and matches MedGemma-27B while being 6.75x smaller; MedPsy-1.7B outperforms MedGemma-1.5-4B by 11.42 points despite being less than half its size. On HealthBench-Hard, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-27B by 15.33 points, while MedPsy-1.7B surpasses it by 11.66 points despite being ~16x smaller. Beyond accuracy, MedPsy reduces average response length by 1.7x (1.7B) and 3.2x (4B) versus its Qwen3 backbones, while Q4_K_M quantization preserves the aggregate score within 1 point of BF16 with ~69% lower disk footprint. In real-device inference, MedPsy-4B's shorter generations translate into 75% lower median end-to-end latency and estimated energy per answer than its Qwen3 backbone at matched quantization, while peak RAM remains below 5 GB across our tested laptop and smartphone configurations.
  </details>

- **[HEDGE: Gated Hypergraph Memory for Scalable Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38902)**  `arXiv:2610.38902`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amirhossein Abaskohi, Mahdi Mostajabdaveh, Zirui Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate systems require agents to retain and revisit arguments across multiple rounds, yet limited context windows and unstructured memories make relevant information increasingly difficult to recover. Existing flat and pairwise-graph memories also struggle to capture higher-order relations, including multi-agent agreement, conflicting evidence, and evolving consensus. We introduce HEDGE, a selective memory framework that represents debate trajectories as semantic hypergraphs. Hyperedges connect related claims across agents, while intra-hyperedge relational graphs preserve fine-grained argumentative structure. A learned memory selection module (MSM), trained through supervised learning and reinforcement learning, routes each memory candidate to STORE, BACKUP, or OMIT, retaining useful information while limiting redundancy. Across seven diverse benchmarks, HEDGE consistently outperforms strong single-agent, multi-agent, and memory baselines, improving accuracy by 5.0 points on average over the strongest multi-agent system with GPT-4o-mini. The gains reach 8.2 points on conflict-heavy tasks, while HEDGE reduces memory usage by 48% and the number of debate rounds by 34%. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Missing Piece for Trustworthy AI Reviewers: From Benchmarking Rhetorical Robustness to SciCore Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39027)**  `arXiv:2609.39027`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenguang Wang, Ming Li, Chenrui Fan, Jianpeng Chen, Han Chen, Tianyi Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI reviewers can assign different judgments to manuscripts that report the same science in different wording, potentially rewarding rhetorical optimization over scientific improvement. We formulate Rhetorical Robustness as the joint requirement of stability across content-preserving rewrites and discrimination across papers. We introduce RobustReview, a controlled full-manuscript benchmark with 1,260 manuscript versions, and evaluate 30 reviewer configurations. The benchmark reveals false robustness, where low rewrite sensitivity coincides with score collapse across papers, and shows that human alignment and rhetorical robustness rank reviewers differently. Moreover, the evaluated content-focused prompting protocol does not consistently improve robustness across backbones. Motivated by these findings, we introduce SciCore, a dual-branch reviewer that averages a full-manuscript judgment with a judgment based on an extracted, structured science core. This design combines manuscript-level assessment with a content-normalized view intended to reduce rhetorical sensitivity. In our primary GPT-5.5 comparison, SciCore achieves a leading joint stability-discrimination profile among the benchmarked reviewers while maintaining competitive human alignment. These results identify rhetorical robustness as a distinct evaluation target and demonstrate the potential of science-core review to improve it.
  </details>

- **[Partition, Prompt, Aggregate: Statistical Self-Consistency in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15277)**  `arXiv:2607.15277`  `cs.CL`  
  _Patrik Wolf, Thomas Kleine Buening, Andreas Krause, Celestine Mendler-Dünner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are often used to perform a form of conditional inference. In-context learning is one implicit way to do this. The prompt specifies a context and the model's output is treated as an estimate of the corresponding conditional distribution. If this interpretation holds, then LLM estimates should satisfy basic probabilistic identities. In particular, the law of total probability asserts that prior-weighted conditional distributions aggregate into population-level marginals over any valid partition of the population. In this work, we investigate to what extent LLM estimates adhere to this self-consistency principle. We use binary trees as an evaluation scaffold to recursively partition a population into increasingly fine-grained subpopulations. We then prompt LLMs with verbalized subpopulation descriptions in context, aggregate the resulting estimates back into population-level estimates, and compare them across partitions of varying granularity. Translating these comparisons into a benchmark score and evaluating frontier models across problem domains, we demonstrate widespread violations of basic consistency properties. An in-depth study of persona prompting further reveals a pattern we call the macro fallacy: estimates reconstructed from more fine-grained subpopulation responses are often better aligned with human reference data than direct population-level estimates. The effect persists across variations in partitioning, verbalization, and estimation task, suggesting that models possess relevant subpopulation knowledge but do not reliably propagate it into aggregate estimates. This gap establishes statistical self-consistency as an unsaturated, reference-free criterion for evaluating LLMs.
  </details>

- **[MosaicLeaks: Privacy Risks in Querying-in-the-Open for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30727)**  `arXiv:2605.30727`  `cs.CL`  
  _Alexander Gurung, Spandana Gella, Alexandre Drouin, Issam H. Laradji, Perouz Taslakian, Rafael Pardinas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents increasingly combine private local documents with external tools like web retrieval, creating a privacy risk: an agent's external queries may leak sensitive information from its local context. This risk is amplified by the mosaic effect, where individual queries may appear harmless but become revealing in aggregate. We introduce MosaicLeaks, a benchmark of 1,001 multi-hop deep research tasks that chain private enterprise documents and a public web corpus, forcing agents to make external queries that depend on local information. We evaluate leakage with an adversary LLM that observes only the agent's external queries and attempts to infer private information at three levels: the agent's research intent, answers to specific private questions and verifiable claims about the enterprise documents. We find that models across families and sizes frequently leak at all three levels, that zero-shot privacy prompting has inconsistent effects across models and that reinforcement learning for task performance alone worsens leakage. To address this, we propose Privacy-Aware Deep Research (PA-DR), an RL framework that combines situational rewards for task success with a learned privacy classifier to provide dense credit assignment over both per-query and mosaic-level leakage. Training Qwen3-4B-Instruct with PA-DR improves accuracy from 48.7% to 58.1% and reduces Privacy Leakage from 34.0% to 6.5%.
  </details>

- **[LongBEL: Long-Context and Document-Consistent Biomedical Entity Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13451)**  `arXiv:2605.13451`  `cs.CL`  
  _Adam Remaki, Xavier Tannier, Christel Gérardin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical entity linking maps textual mentions to concepts in structured knowledge bases such as UMLS or SNOMED CT. Most existing systems link each mention independently, using only the mention or its surrounding sentence. This ignores dependencies between mentions in the same document and can lead to inconsistent predictions, especially when the same concept appears under different surface forms. We introduce LongBEL, a document-level generative framework that combines full-document context with a memory of previous predictions. To make this memory robust, LongBEL is trained with cross-validated predictions rather than gold labels, reducing the mismatch between training and inference and limiting cascading errors. Experiments on five biomedical benchmarks across English, French, and Spanish show that LongBEL improves over sentence-level generative baselines, with the largest gains on datasets where concepts frequently recur within documents. An ensemble of local, global, and memory-based variants achieves the best results across all benchmarks. Further analysis shows that the largest gains occur on recurring concepts, suggesting that LongBEL mainly improves document-level consistency rather than isolated mention disambiguation.
  </details>

- **[Don't Lose Focus: Activation Steering via Key-Orthogonal Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06342)**  `arXiv:2605.06342`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haoyan Luo, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Mateja Jamnik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering controls LLM behaviour towards target behaviour by intervening in internal representations, yet it often degrades reasoning and retrieval performance. We argue that a primary cause of this trade-off is attention rerouting: steering vectors alter query-key matching, shifting attention away from contextually important tokens toward less informative ones. To address this, we propose Steering via Key-Orthogonal Projections (SKOP), a steering method that constrains harmful attention rerouting without eliminating steering efficacy. SKOP achieves this by preserving attention patterns on a small set of focus tokens the model relies on for reasoning and retrieval, while allowing redistribution among less critical tail tokens. Across multiple steering benchmarks, we show that SKOP achieves the best joint steering-utility trade-off, reducing utility degradation by 5-7x while retaining over 95% of vanilla steering efficacy. Our results further suggest that, in long-context retrieval settings where vanilla steering approaches are ineffective, SKOP can maintain robust performance by avoiding attention rerouting.
  </details>

- **[A minimal probe of task modeling in language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02028)**  `arXiv:2605.02028`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianxiang Dai, Jonathan Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models read long contexts and score well on knowledge and reasoning benchmarks, but neither shows whether a model can do work whose next step depends on everything that came before, as a long-running agent must. We call this ability task modeling and propose counting identical items as its simplest test: every item adds to the total and no item records it. We define counting capacity (CC) as the sequence length up to which a model returns the exact count more often than not, and measure it for 205 models. Every resolved measurement loses count abruptly, at a median of 50 items and at most 627; across release years the median rose 1.6-fold while context windows grew about 80-fold. Changing the counted symbol or the delimiter moves a model's CC severalfold, yet the model accounts for about three quarters of the variance across 16 models and 11 encodings. Reasoning leaves the median CC unchanged across 77 matched pairs. In six open-weight models the count stays readable in early layers past the boundary but does not reach the output, and an activation edit that carries the count forward also carries the state of an object-tracking task. CC tracks the METR task horizon after controlling for general capability, and rises with capacity on grid-execution tasks and, with reasoning off, on object tracking. We do not show that counting is necessary or sufficient for long-horizon work; that remains a hypothesis, which this cheap measurement can test.
  </details>

- **[Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18543)**  `arXiv:2611.18543`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Tony H. Zhang, Connor Mattson, Daniel S. Brown_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.
  </details>

- **[Physics-Informed Feature Representations and Pretrained Tabular Foundation Models for Generalizable Melt Pool Geometry Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26235)**  `arXiv:2611.26235`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Rafid Enayet, Imtiaz Ahmed, Md Habibor Rahman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing generalizable, data-efficient machine learning (ML) models for advanced manufacturing remains challenging because experimental datasets are limited and process-property relationships vary across materials and operating conditions. In laser powder bed fusion (LPBF), melt pool width, depth, and melting mode (conduction, transition, or keyhole) govern process stability and defect formation. Conventional ML models typically use dimensional process parameters, which represent laser-material interactions only implicitly and may limit cross-material transfer. This study evaluates whether two physics-informed dimensionless features, normalized enthalpy and Peclet number, combining laser processing conditions with material thermophysical properties, improve melt pool prediction over raw process parameters. We systematically benchmark seven models: four classical ML methods (Gaussian process, support vector machine, random forest, and XGBoost) and three pretrained tabular foundation models (TabPFN, TabDPT, and TabICLv2). Experiments span 316L stainless steel, IN625, IN718, and combined-material datasets, covering regression of melt pool width and depth and classification of melting modes. Dimensionless features yield comparable or superior predictive performance in most settings, with the largest gains in cross-material depth prediction (R^2 = 0.96). Tabular foundation models generally achieve stronger classification performance than classical baselines, particularly for multi-material datasets. These findings indicate that combining physically meaningful dimensionless representations with pretrained tabular models can improve data-efficient melt pool modeling and support more transferable process-window identification in LPBF.
  </details>

- **[SiliconGym: A Versatile Reinforcement Learning Environment for Agentic EDA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25658)**  `arXiv:2611.25658`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hejia Zhang, Sheng Lu, Yitong Ma, Zhongming Yu, Zhijing Wu, Wentao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) with electronic design automation (EDA) feedback is advancing large language models for hardware design and verification. Extending these gains to hardware-engineering agents requires environments that span tasks, preserve state across tool calls, and support large hierarchies alongside parallel rollouts. We present SiliconGym, the first versatile, cross-task and cross-toolchain RL environment for hardware design and verification. By decoupling versioned workspaces, budgeted execution, and acceptance-gated scoring, SiliconGym scales from module-level RTL, testbench, and PPA tasks to multi-round repository repair and full-hierarchy SoC flows. Streaming dispatch overlaps tool checks with generation, finishing a 256-answer RL batch 21.9% faster than native ChipSeek. Using interchangeable open-source and commercial backends, we show that open-source-trained functional correctness transfers cleanly to commercial simulation, while paired netlist evaluation reveals when open-source PPA gains survive commercial synthesis.
  </details>

- **[Classical Energies Learn to Reason 1,000x Faster than Looped Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17309)**  `arXiv:2611.17309`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Benjamin Hoover, Hugh Blayney, James C. R. Whittington_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.
  </details>

- **[QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513)**  `arXiv:2609.19513`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Davide Vitabile, N. Ranjan, Akshay Nambiar, Kamal K. Gupta, Amril Nazir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.
  </details>

- **[CHORUS: Coverage-Guided Agentic RL and Policy Consolidation for Testbench Stimulus Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10090)**  `arXiv:2608.10090`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hejia Zhang, Sheng Lu, Zhijing Wu, Yitong Ma, Zhongming Yu, Wentao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Testbenches written by large language models can compile and run while leaving entire datapaths idle. We present CHORUS, the first framework to combine coverage-guided agentic reinforcement learning (RL) with policy consolidation for testbench stimulus generation. We train LLM4Cov's final supervised checkpoint to draft testbenches and repair its lowest-scoring draft using simulator feedback, raising CVDP-ECov agentic Pass@1 from 69.2% to 85.5%. Applying the same training to three checkpoints yields similar overall pass rates, yet the policies succeed on different designs. Averaging their weights combines these strengths in a single dense 4B model that reaches 86.7% agentic Pass@1. When simulator rewards select the teacher for each training prompt, the distilled 4B model reaches 88.0%, 13.5 percentage points above DeepSeek-R1 (671B).
  </details>

- **[Structured Role-Aware Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07274)**  `arXiv:2605.07274`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bingqing Jiang, Guoxi Zhang, Jasper Wang, Bingning Wang, Difan Zou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), especially with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has shown strong potential for improving the reasoning capabilities of large vision-language models (LVLMs). However, in multimodal reasoning, final-answer rewards are typically assigned at the sequence level and do not distinguish the functional roles of different tokens, making it difficult to determine whether a correct answer is supported by task-relevant visual evidence. In this paper, we revisit multimodal RLVR from the perspective of role-aware token-level credit assignment, where structured responses are decomposed into perception tokens for extracting visual evidence and reasoning tokens for deriving answers from that evidence. Based on this perspective, we propose Structured Role-aware Policy Optimization (SRPO), which refines the sequence-level GRPO advantage into role-aware token-level advantages without changing the reward function. Specifically, SRPO assigns role-specific credit by using self-distilled on-policy contrasts: perception tokens are emphasized according to their visual dependency under original versus corrupted visual inputs, while reasoning tokens are emphasized according to their consistency with the generated perception. These role-specific signals are further unified through a shared trajectory-level baseline, yielding positive token weights that adjust relative update magnitudes while preserving the original GRPO reward and advantage signs, without requiring external reward models or separate teachers.
