Experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that SRPO improves evidence-grounded reasoning, highlighting the importance of moving beyond uniform sequence-level credit toward role-aware optimization for reliable multimodal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[MKA: Evaluating Multimodal Knowledge Agents on end-to-end professional tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25228)**  `arXiv:2611.25228`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Anima Singh, Suhas Yogin, Weicheng Kuo, Ivor Rendulic, Ivan Jurin, Antoine Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Multimodal Knowledge Agent (MKA) benchmark, which measures how well autonomous agents perform end-to-end professional knowledge work that depends on visual understanding. Each task requires an agent to extract and combine information from multiple visually dense files, such as CAD blueprints, electronics schematics, radiographs, complex charts and geospatial maps, and to produce a standalone deliverable in .xlsx, .pptx, .pdf, .docx, .html or .png format. Existing benchmarks leave this capability largely untested: removing image-viewing tools leaves Gemini 4 Carbon's score on GDPval unchanged, and single-format evaluations such as GDP$.$pdf and GDP$.$xlsx do not require reasoning across files or modalities. MKA contains 200 tasks across 10 economic sectors. Deliverables are graded against expert-weighted rubrics by a sandboxed agentic autorater that agrees with human domain experts on 96.2% of criterion judgments in our expert-graded study (Cohen's $\kappa = 0.91$), scored by weighted satisfaction rate (WSR) overall and on frontier criteria unsaturated by prior-generation models (Climb-WSR). Across the frontier models we evaluate (Gemini 4 Carbon, Claude Fable 5.2, Claude Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 Astra), Claude Fable 5.2 (56.6% Climb-WSR, 73.3% Overall WSR) and Gemini 4 Carbon (54.3% Climb-WSR, 71.9% Overall WSR) achieve the highest scores, while Carbon uses $5.7\times$ fewer tokens. Even for the strongest models, Climb-WSR remains below 57%, leaving substantial headroom for multimodal agents.
  </details>

- **[MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723)**  `arXiv:2610.11723`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhangbo Xu, Ruoxi Zhang, Rui Hu, Yisong Wang, Xitong Li, Jingdong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.
  </details>

- **[Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory for Graphic Design Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22086)**  `arXiv:2609.22086`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modularthis http URLskill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools. Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.
  </details>

- **[TwinLab-Bench: Evaluating AI Scientists in Agentic Twins of Real Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26248)**  `arXiv:2611.26248`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yang Wan, Linchao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are increasingly expected to act as AI scientists, which requires three increasingly open abilities: carrying out a given computational workflow correctly, choosing the next measurements from a given design space, and designing their own experiments. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate the first two abilities, in a terminal or in a closed loop that selects from predefined candidates. Evaluating the third ability requires reliably executing an experiment that an agent designs, with readings grounded in a real laboratory. We introduce TwinLab-Bench, which evaluates AI scientists on self-designed experiments in 52 tasks from laboratories in the physical sciences, the life sciences, and engineering. The benchmark supports more than 100 experimental action types, and the agent's report is scored by comparison with a hidden answer taken from real laboratory records. Each task has its own TwinLab agent, an agent that serves as the environment and executes the experiments that the tested agent designs, reading the raw materials of the laboratory and calling calculators that are built from its records. Compared with a language model that sees only the task description, the TwinLab agent almost eliminates readings that deviate from the real records and inconsistent answers to a repeated request, and closed-book probes control for answers recalled from memory. We find that these tasks remain hard for frontier models: the strongest, Claude Fable 5.2, averages a score of only 45.0, and we conduct an empirical study of where it fails.
  </details>

- **[Can Computation from Earlier Problems Help LLMs Solve New Ones?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39394)**  `arXiv:2609.39394`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jipei He, Wenhui Tan, Xiaoyi Yu, Enver Sangineto, Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Rita Cucchiara, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often solve independent problems in the same conversation. Can computation from earlier problems help them solve new ones? Preliminary experiments show that retained history can raise or lower later-turn accuracy, even within the same domain. To trace this effect, we hold each problem fixed and vary its preceding history. The resulting changes in the model's internal states contain a recurring, problem-dependent component. To improve reasoning with retained history, we introduce STAIR (Stale-Token Attention for Inter-query Reuse). It captures attention keys and values from earlier response generation in a read-only bank. STAIR learns to reflect current query vectors to redirect attention over this bank during prompt processing. The backbone and stored entries remain fixed; only 12,288 parameters are trained. Across three Qwen models and four reasoning benchmarks, STAIR improves average later-turn accuracy by up to 11.67 percentage points over the unmodified model with history. The math-trained Qwen3.5-9B controller also improves code generation on HumanEval+ without further training.
  </details>

- **[Balance of Benchmarks: Evidence Retention and Multiplicity-Aware Model Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30044)**  `arXiv:2608.30044`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jhen-Ke Lin, Hong-Yun Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark aggregation requires decisions about how much evidence to use and how much influence each benchmark receives. Curated suites control influence through selection, which can exclude informative observations. A broader pool can retain those observations. With equal weighting, repeated or closely related benchmarks gain influence as their number grows. We introduce Balance of Benchmarks (BoB) to retain eligible observations and control their influence separately. We fit benchmark-specific response curves to place different difficulty ranges on a shared ability scale and use description similarity to downweight benchmarks in crowded neighborhoods. We test retention and density weighting in separate interventions on WildScores, a sparse collection of 148 developer-reported benchmarks, using 14 Artificial Analysis benchmarks as a controlled reference. With the model population fixed and entire source-lineage families held out, suite expansion improves held-out prediction under BoB and equal-weight equating on both WildScores and BIG-bench. Under substantial reporting overlap, density weighting improves robustness to multiplicity. Complete re-listings yield smaller mean rank displacement under BoB (0.63 versus 1.95 for equal-weight equating), with benefits also in paraphrased re-listings and supported affine fits. The benefit can reverse if models report different benchmarks, because a single weight per column gives models that report only part of a group less than its intended share. We therefore compute each model's weights from the benchmarks it reports. This rule removes the reversal in controlled re-listings and keeps held-out prediction close to that of equal weighting. These comparisons separate evidence retention, benchmark influence, and reporting coverage and show why repeated entries need to be handled per model.
  </details>

- **[LM-based Personalized Travel Planner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25531)**  `arXiv:2611.25531`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Amandi Nimasha, Oshadha Wijerathne, Dushan Fernando, Nisansa de Silva, Srinath Perera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective travel planning requires handling multiple information sources, managing constraints, and tailoring recommendations to user preferences. While LLMs have introduced dynamic, context-aware decision-making, single-agent models often struggle with complex multi-step reasoning, constraint adherence, and modular execution. To overcome these limitations, we propose a hierarchical multi-agent framework where a supervising chatbot agent coordinates specialized agents for schedule validation, user query formulation, real-time data retrieval, and constraint-aware itinerary generation. By integrating LLM reasoning, external APIs, and iterative refinement mechanisms based on a deterministic, multi-dimensional scoring framework, our approach ensures adaptability, efficiency, and personalization. Evaluations on the well-known TravelPlanner benchmark demonstrate improvements in Budget Constraint planning by 10\%, wider constraints set-based planning by 25\%, and personalized planning results by 45\%. compared to the ReAct baseline. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Pro$^2$ Agent: Scientific Problem Probing for Autonomous AI R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25357)**  `arXiv:2611.25357`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yixiong Chen, Liqian Peng, Tian Tan, Ziqian Lin, Blake Jianhang Chen, Chao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can now autonomously conduct AI research by writing code and running experiments. They typically do so by iteratively optimizing a codebase, while ignoring the problem structure behind the task. This formulation is effective on simpler tasks, but on difficult problems it often gets stuck in the wrong solution region because the agent never explicitly represents its understanding of the problem. We introduce Pro$^2$ Agent, which shifts the primary state of autonomous AI research from a codebase state to a Problem State: an explicit structure of what problems prevent success and which solutions are likely to address them. Pro$^2$ Agent initializes the Problem State through comprehensive analysis of available resources and constraints. It then performs targeted research to relate the identified problems to candidate solutions. Through controlled hypothesis testing, the agent updates the importance of a suspected problem or the benefit brought by a candidate solution. On the complete MLE-Bench, Pro$^2$ Agent matches the best reported medal rate (70.7%) under the standard 24-hour budget and reaches 76.0% with a 48-hour budget, the highest medal rate reported on the complete benchmark. It also achieves competitive performance on PostTrainBench. In addition, it produces research notes for each task that make the research process transparent and reusable by human researchers.
  </details>

- **[Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25214)**  `arXiv:2611.25214`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fangxu Yu, Tao Feng, Xuying Ning, Haolin Yang, Jiaxuan You, Tianyi Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs.
  </details>

- **[Calibrated Decision Rewards: Training Language Models with a Judge That Knows How Sure It Is](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25135)**  `arXiv:2611.25135`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rudransh Agnihotri, Manasvi Kapoor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) can only teach a language model what its reward can recognize. Answer checkers are exact but need a reference answer; learned reward models and LLM judges work everywhere, but their scores are not probabilities. We study RLCDR (Reinforcement Learning from Calibrated Decision Rewards). An LLM converted into a decision model answers a typed question about a response ("is the final answer correct?", "which step is the first error?") with a probability distribution, read in one forward pass, debiased over option orders and calibrated with about a hundred labels. Training Qwen3-1.7B with GRPO on MATH, this reference-free reward raises MATH500 accuracy from 72.2% to 76.8% (+4.65 points, p < 1e-4), matching training on the answer key (76.9%), while a random reward gains only +0.65. The gain grows with difficulty, from 44.8% to 51.7% on the hardest level. On the policy's own changing samples the reward stays calibrated: its expected calibration error is half that of a softmax judge on the same 27B backbone (0.074 vs. 0.141), and it shows no sign of reward hacking. Without step training, the same model finds the first wrong step better than a dedicated 7B process reward model (ProcessBench F1 0.772 vs. 0.722, 20/20 splits). We also map where the approach stops being enough: trained only on problems the base model mostly fails, the reference-free reward still improves the policy (+1.9 MATH500 points, p = 0.03) but trails the answer key, because its probabilities become overconfident on that distribution. We state six hypotheses in advance and test each with a control designed to falsify it.
  </details>

- **[EvoSkill Injection: Red-Teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30429)**  `arXiv:2608.30429`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Sugyeong Eo, Yeo-Chan Yoon, Chanjun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agent systems increasingly adopt skill-based architectures to reduce repetitive reasoning costs and improve stable, efficient task execution. Recent studies propose self-evolving agents that autonomously generate, refine, and reuse skills from past experiences to enable continuous capability evolution. However, autonomous skill evolution introduces a new attack surface in which malicious capabilities are generated, stored, and reused as legitimate skills. In this paper, we define EvoSkill Injection as a threat model targeting the autonomous skill generation and evolution pipeline of self-evolving agents. We further propose SARGE (Red-teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in self-evolving agents), a red-teaming framework for evaluating this threat model through iterative generation, escalation, and reinforcement interactions. To support our framework, we construct EvoSkillBench, a benchmark dataset of malicious interaction trajectories for inducing malicious skill formation in self-evolving agents, and introduce EvoSkillSafetyBench, a post-attack benchmark for evaluating whether injected malicious skills are subsequently retrieved and activated as harmful behaviors. Our evaluation shows that SARGE induces malicious skill formation and that injected skills are persistently stored and repeatedly activated, highlighting the risk of persistent capability corruption.
  </details>

- **[Tokengeist: Multi-Turn Attribution Tracing in Agentic Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22610)**  `arXiv:2607.22610`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jessica Tang, Shraddha Barke, Sharad Agarwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a model answers after several turns of dialogue and tool use, an overseer needs to know which earlier turns and intermediate steps it rests on. Attribution is the natural primitive for answering this, but existing context attribution methods process the full transcript in a single pass: they recover surface-level dependencies while missing the layered structure of multi-turn traces, in which an assistant turn is itself a derived artifact that condenses or transforms earlier context. We introduce multi-turn context attribution (MTCA): given a target span in a model response, the task of tracing attribution backward across turns to identify not only which prior turns were directly relevant, but also how those turns themselves depended on earlier context. We propose Tokengeist, an attribution-method-agnostic and scalable framework that recovers full dependency paths by casting attribution as a recursive traversal of a directed acyclic graph over conversation turns. We will release MTCABench, a benchmark of 3,970 target spans across 685 multi-turn conversations, annotated with gold provenance graphs reaching depths of up to 17, across four dependency types. Across four open-weight models, flat attribution methods fail to recover multi-hop dependencies, with source recall improving from 11-38% under flat attribution to 82-94% under Tokengeist. Our results reveal a systematic failure mode of single-pass attribution, which we term provenance collapse, where attribution terminates at the nearest assistant restatement instead of the originating event.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry of Lies: Knowledge and Persona Directions for Detecting Deception in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26545)**  `arXiv:2611.26545`  `cs.AI`  
  _Md. Abdullah Al Zami, Suraiya Shanaz, Mahdi Hasan, Shoaib Ahmed Dipu, Farig Yousuf Sadeque, Md. Sabbir Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear probes are the cheapest LLM lie detectors but often fail to transfer across kinds of lie. Label-free directions from honest and deceptive role-play are untested at 70B scale and on lies about knowledge, where a lie and an honest denial can be identical. We widen a published persona bank with roles that deny knowledge they have, plus honest counterparts. At each model's middle layer the resulting persona direction ranks lies across Liars' Bench better than single-dataset probes on three of its four models; it fails on Qwen, even when Qwen stays in role far more often. On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct it beats directions from a random persona bank, but its gain over the published direction is within the range of random widenings. On lies about knowledge, persona directions mainly track a reply's wording: where lies and honest denials share their words, our three main contrasts stay inside the random-direction band on Llama and Qwen and reach only its edge on Gemma, though on Mistral one clears it. A knowledge direction contrasts questions the model can answer with ones nobody can, each followed by the same scripted denial. It separates lies from identical honest denials on two models and narrowly on a third, but not on the fourth; its other results partly depend on the benchmark's wording. On the Gender-Secret fine-tune it clears the band by a few hundredths on Llama and fails on Mistral. The sum of the two directions ranks lies better than a supervised probe at the same depth on all four models (on one, both near chance) but is level with the persona direction on Llama's average and behind that probe on held-out lies. More varied role-play questions do not help the persona direction on Liars' Bench.
  </details>

- **[LLM GerryMandering: Separating Interactions from Additive Effects in LLM Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26538)**  `arXiv:2611.26538`  `cs.AI`  
  _George Rahul, Ronit Jahilal, Bhaskara Hanuma Vedula, Ponnurangam Kumaraguru, Pushkar Shukla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intersectional bias arises when a model treats people who hold several marginalised identities at once differently from what their separate identities would predict. Auditing such intersectional subgroups remains a challenge: their number grows quadratically, and there is no agreed baseline for what independent attributes would predict. Without such a baseline, many existing audits flag a subgroup when it exceeds each attribute alone, a condition that attributes acting independently already satisfy. We test this standard criterion on 150 synthetic datasets built with no interaction. With the shuffle null, it flags 66.1 false subgroups per dataset on average, and at least one in every dataset. When we plant interactions of known size into these datasets, a repaired null lets the criterion recover only 32% of them. We introduce ADDIS (Additive Intersectional Screen), which fits an additive model over all attributes, scores each subgroup by its Additive Residual Distance (ARD) from that model's prediction, and tests it against a Freedman-Lane null. On our synthetic benchmark, ADDIS flags 0.09 false subgroups per dataset and recovers 84% of the same planted interactions. We then build IndiPersona, 10,000 synthetic Indian personas with six identity attributes, and audit 13 open language models. The standard criterion flags 7.1% of the 738 tested subgroups on average; ADDIS flags 3.0%. Gender identity crossed with sexual orientation is the largest pairing in every model, and counterfactual personas confirm it and additionally reveal a caste x class interaction in all but one model that the observational test lacked the data to detect. Much of what audits report as intersectional bias can thus arise from independent single-attribute effects alone, yet a small core of interactions survives.
  </details>

- **[FLUID: Flexible, Evidence-Grounded Situated Task Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26491)**  `arXiv:2611.26491`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunqi Zhao, Run Peng, Ze Dong, Ryan Lu, Yinpei Dai, Joyce Chai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time task guidance is inherently flexible: when an agent guides a user through a task, multiple responses and timings can be appropriate, and sometimes the best action is to remain silent. Existing benchmarks often miss this flexibility by treating synthesized dialogues as fixed targets for what to say and when to say it. We introduce FLUID (Flexible, Evidence-Grounded Situated Task Guidance), a unified framework for modeling, evaluating, and enabling flexible interaction in multimodal task guidance. Using naturally occurring human-human guidance from WTaG and HoloAssist, FLUID grounds interventions in their supporting evidence, necessity, and timing flexibility, and uses this representation both to evaluate flexible guidance and to build a real-time agent for task guidance. Our agent achieves the strongest overall balance of response correctness, intervention precision, timely guidance, and response latency among evaluated systems, while still leaving room for improvement in avoiding speech overlap. Together, FLUID moves situated task guidance beyond rigid reference matching toward more natural and adaptive human-AI interaction. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Seer: Guiding Small Deep-Research Agents with Shared Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26423)**  `arXiv:2611.26423`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhao Zhou, Yang Zhou, Yuanxi Li, Jindi Lv, Dan Si, Qing Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep-research agents repeatedly decide what to search, read, and verify, often using large cloud-hosted language models for action proposal. However, privacy and local control motivate running agents on users' devices, where limited memory and compute favor smaller models and complicate on-device training. Yet inference-time guidance faces three obstacles: exploring alternatives through real execution adds tool calls, prompted self-evaluation is bounded by the agent's judgment, and model-specific guidance risks repeated adaptation costs. We introduce Seer, an inference-time framework providing local guidance that separates the agent's action proposal from learned consequence prediction and value estimation. From a shared corpus of real research trajectories, we train two models: a tool-response simulator on observed call--response pairs and a value estimator on histories labeled with discounted terminal correctness. The simulator and estimator are trained offline and reused across agents without further training. Moreover, Seer executes only the selected action and adds only real tool responses to the committed history, avoiding tool calls on discarded candidates at additional inference cost. Across multiple deep-research benchmarks, Seer substantially improves agent performance for different language models. For instance, GAIA accuracy rises from 13.6% to 41.7% for Qwen3.5-4B and from 22.3% to 50.5% for Qwen3.5-9B. Furthermore, our analyses suggest that broader candidate coverage can compensate for imperfect simulation, while prediction and scoring errors limit the benefits of wider imagined search. These findings highlight the potential of reusable models for prediction and evaluation to strengthen small research agents through extra local computation without the real execution of the discarded candidates.
  </details>

- **[ZeroVLN: Learning to Navigate without Supervised Fine-Tuning of the VLM Brain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26414)**  `arXiv:2611.26414`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Huang, Yang Zhou, Guli Zhang, Sunzhu Li, Hongyan Xie, Zehao Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Representative vision-language navigation (VLN) methods typically couple a vision-language model (VLM) brain with a learnable action head and fine-tune the VLM brain on navigation-domain data to predict geometric goals. However, such adaptation can substantially alter base VLM weights and degrade general visual capabilities. We introduce ZeroVLN, a slow-fast navigation framework that achieves effective navigation with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain. Rather than teaching the brain to predict geometric goals, we equip it with a semantic tool-calling interface for conveying movement decisions to an action expert in natural language. We first adapt the action expert to carry out these movement requests, then jointly improve the brain's movement decisions and the action expert's continuous execution through closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). Trained on the same navigation episodes, ZeroVLN substantially outperforms representative SFT-based hierarchical VLN methods on standard VLN-CE (continuous environments) benchmarks, while inducing only minor changes to the brain's weights and largely preserving its general capabilities. To our knowledge, ZeroVLN is the first slow-fast VLN framework with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain, and the first to jointly optimize its slow and fast policies through closed-loop agentic RL in VLN-CE.
  </details>

- **[World Models Favor Learned Priors over Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26366)**  `arXiv:2611.26366`  `cs.AI`  
  _Han Xiao, Zhiyong Bao, Xiaotong Liu, Yanhao Chen, Qingqiang Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models are increasingly used to simulate physical interactions, but training a separate model for each environment limits scalability. Cross-environment prediction requires identifying the current physical rule, which appearance alone may not reveal. We therefore study visual calibration: using object motion and interactions in a short video as direct evidence to infer the current rule and predict motion under that rule from a different initial state. However, differences in data availability across physical conditions can yield a long-tailed training distribution, biasing models toward common rules even when evidence supports rare ones. To study this challenge, we introduce TailWorld, which evaluates the transfer of demonstrated rules across initial states while independently controlling training imbalance and calibration informativeness. The benchmark compares predictions with two reference futures from the same prediction initial state, one following the demonstrated rule and the other a common rule, to measure prediction bias toward common dynamics. We further propose Rule Inference for Transfer (RIFT), which learns how different rules affect object motion, infers the current rule from calibration interactions, and applies it to prediction from a new initial state. RIFT explicitly controls the influence of training frequency on rule inference, allowing sufficiently informative observational evidence to support rare rules. Experiments on TailWorld show that RIFT improves prediction accuracy and physical consistency across varying training imbalances and distribution shifts, with gains in predicting rare dynamics.
  </details>

- **[How Much Should an Agent Remember? Deployment Lessons from Self-Distilled Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26361)**  `arXiv:2611.26361`  `cs.AI`  
  _K. R. Jayaram, Vatche Isahagian, Aswath Vaithinathan Aravindan, Vinod Muthusamy, Punleuk Oum, Gaodan Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents on long-horizon tasks fail in recurring, procedural ways. They mis-paginate an API, resolve the wrong entity, or answer when no answer was asked. We built a pipeline that lets an agent learn from its own history. It first mines short \emph{lessons} from the agent's own trajectories, one to three per episode. It then consolidates those lessons into a smaller set of \emph{guidelines}, where each guideline records how many lessons it merged. At inference the agent receives either all guidelines or a selected subset. The pipeline ships in the open-source altk-evolve library and is deployed in IBM's CUGA agent and watsonx Orchestrate platform. We report what we learned building, measuring, and productizing it. Our primary instrument is the AppWorld benchmark, across models spanning 40--92\% baseline task completion. The central finding is that the right amount of memory is a property of the model, not the memory. A strong model keeps improving as guidelines are added. DeepSeek-V3.2 gains +9.5 points of task completion and +16.1 points of scenario completion with its full 191-guideline set. A weak model gains less from the same full set than from a small selected subset. gpt-oss-120b starts at 39.9\% task completion. It reaches 51.2\% with all 199 of its guidelines but 55.4\% (mean of two runs) with a selected subset of about 29. Around that result we report what went wrong: a high-support guideline that backfired, provenance silently dropped during consolidation, a swallowed API error that failed every task while producing a plausible log, and a baseline confound that nearly produced a misleading comparison with a prior method. We distill these into recommendations for teams deploying agent memory.
  </details>

- **[Context as a Resource](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26346)**  `arXiv:2611.26346`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xufei Tian, Han Hu, Wenli Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Managing concurrent tasks across separate agent sessions quickly exhausts human attention, motivating always-on personal agents behind a single continuous interface. Yet when every context shares identical frozen backbone weights, funneling interleaved dialogues and external events into one linear session induces cross-topic constraint bleed and head-of-line stalling. Conversely, router-ticket hierarchies incur lossy prose handoffs, heavy routing overhead, and blind merges - collapsing true-conflict repository merges to a 33.3% three-run mean despite high isolated branch passes. Rather than scheduling anthropomorphic roles, we introduce ClonAct, a routerless architecture that virtualizes context blocks directly through a pluggable single-pass non-generative Context MMU, in-place Context Cells, a footprint-aware Synaptic Bus with an isolated trial merge gate, and a wave-flushed Shared Trunk. Across three programmatic multi-stream benchmarks and three model families, our portable trial merge gate lifts true-conflict merges to 63.3%, while ClonAct's routerless virtualization attains this ceiling with 45.9% fewer prompt tokens and prevents compact-model router collapse. Under continuous surge streams, ClonAct matches top completion (95.0%) with the lowest median wait and zero timed-out status checks.
  </details>

- **[Training LLMs to Follow Reasoning Instructions with Minimal Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26342)**  `arXiv:2611.26342`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alexandra Yakovleva, Luat Gia Khoi Nguyen, Juho Kannala, Harri Valpola, Alexander Ilin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly rely on extended reasoning traces, yet the training signals used to shape this behavior remain coarse. Supervised fine-tuning on full trajectories forces the model to imitate an entire demonstration, overconstraining its output distribution, while trajectory-level reinforcement learning provides only weak credit assignment, rewarding or penalizing whole completions without identifying where they succeed or fail. We propose edit-based training, a general framework for learning from a model's own trajectories via localized textual corrections. Given a sampled completion, an editor identifies the first point of failure under a specified criterion and proposes a minimal replacement span. The model is then supervised only on these edited spans. By design, this concentrates supervision where the model's reasoning first breaks down and aims to preserve its existing reasoning patterns elsewhere. The method applies both offline and online, with the latter continually adapting supervision to the model's evolving failure modes. We instantiate this approach on reasoning instruction following with Qwen3-32B on the ReasonIF benchmark. Compared to full-trajectory imitation and trajectory-level reinforcement learning baselines, edit-based training substantially improves compliance with the reasoning instruction while largely preserving the model's skills on untargeted tasks. These results suggest that localized correction is an effective complement to these approaches for shaping how LLMs reason.
  </details>

- **[ClosureBench: A Benchmark for Reasoning under Closure Assumptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26335)**  `arXiv:2611.26335`  `cs.AI`  
  _Francesco Belardinelli, Flavio Corradini, Matteo Leonesi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents built on large language models (LLMs) often answer questions from incomplete records. Missing information might be interpreted as unknown or false, depending on the completeness of the source. Current evaluations fail to distinguish these two aspects, as they are usually interwoven with other reasoning demands. We present ClosureBench, a benchmark that tests whether LLMs follow the stated rules for dealing with missing information. Every base scenario is tested under OWA, CWA, and LCWA, and only the treatment of missing information changes between variants. ClosureBench provides a verified base benchmark as well as agent extensions for action choice, source scope, and updates. A model is considered correct on a base scenario only if it provides correct answers in all of its closure variants. Across 960 instances, overall accuracy can mask substantial closure mistakes. Models achieve overall accuracy from 71.18% to 99.86% across replicated runs, but their core switch accuracy, measured on the scenarios whose gold label actually changes with the contract, varies between 18.23% and 99.31%. Large discrepancies emerge under LCWA. For example, some agents choose unknown despite the prompt indicating that missing information should be treated as false. Our findings demonstrate that evaluations of AI agents should not only measure whether agents can reason over existing facts but also whether they use the appropriate assumption when information is absent.
  </details>

- **[Sage-Evolve-Preview:Adaptive Observation for Post-Training Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26132)**  `arXiv:2611.26132`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yinhao Tang, Youqing Fang, Yanan Sun, Xintian Shen, Hanyu Sun, Wenran Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training recursive self-improvement (RSI) iteratively improves a target capability, operationalized by a fixed anchor benchmark, through repeated intervention, training, and evaluation. Yet most loops use the anchor's aggregate score as their sole observation, so early gains give way to stalled progress and repetitive exploration. When this anchor-only feedback ceases to reveal actionable gaps, a plateau may reflect an exhausted observation rather than an exhausted capability. We introduce adaptive observation (AdaObs), an observation-side mechanism of harness evolution that keeps the anchor objective fixed while adapting the active observation. At a plateau, a controller refines diagnostic granularity, escalates difficulty, and adds related tasks; validated increments are fused back into training, while every checkpoint remains evaluated against the anchor. Across eight independently optimized anchors in long-context reading comprehension and instruction following, AdaObs raises the mean score from $66.4$ to $73.7$ under a matched data budget, outperforming FixedObs, the anchor-only baseline. The evolved harness further improves the seven-task average on the independent PostTrainBench across two agent backends. These results identify observation adaptation as a missing mechanism of harness evolution for sustaining post-training RSI.
  </details>

- **[ForeSeek: Foresight-Driven Reasoning for Long-Form Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26024)**  `arXiv:2611.26024`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuchen Sun, Siyuan Cheng, Shi-Zhe Chen, Yixin Qin, Jinwen Luo, Zheng Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form video understanding, as a fundamental yet crucial vision task, is being revolutionized by multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based video agents. However, the retrieval-driven nature inherent to these agents creates a significant bottleneck, forcing them to reactively fetch clips based on local saliency without a global temporal plan. To address this, we present ForeSeek, a foresight-driven video agent that transforms long-video reasoning from reactive retrieval into predictive exploration. Rather than blindly probing the timeline, ForeSeek forms visually grounded expectations over unseen temporal regions, verifies them through targeted observations, and continuously refines its search based on emerging evidence, where failed expectations serve as informative signals for principled backtracking. Furthermore, we develop a two-stage training framework that equips MLLMs with foresight-guided reasoning and efficient decision making. Extensive experiments on five long-video benchmarks demonstrate that ForeSeek consistently improves the efficiency--accuracy trade-off of long-video reasoning, achieving competitive or superior accuracy while observing $2.5$--$4.4\times$ fewer frames than the strongest search-based agent. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[From Cases to Criteria: Reusing Evaluation Knowledge for Task-Specific Rubric Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25903)**  `arXiv:2611.25903`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qiyan Deng, Long-Kai Huang, Kangfei Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-specific rubrics make LLM evaluation more fine-grained and transparent, and generating high-quality rubrics requires both identifying the target query's evaluation scope and specifying concrete checks. Historical rubrics provide reusable evaluation experiences, yet existing approaches reuse either complete query-rubric cases or aggregated criteria and dimensions without explicitly connecting recurring evaluation requirements to their criterion-level evidence and source-query context. This can preserve irrelevant task details or lose the broader evaluation structure. In this paper, we introduce RubricDuo, which organizes historical query-rubric pairs into a Rubric Knowledge Graph linking evaluation dimensions, shared concepts, local concepts, criteria, and queries, where reusable evaluation requirements across queries are abstracted by shared concepts. Given a target query, RubricDuo first combines concepts relevant to the target query with retrieved historical cases to draft a rubric with broad coverage. It then revisits each draft criterion, retrieves concept-linked historical criteria and their source queries, and adapts applicable evidence to refine the criterion. Experiments on RubricBench and HealthBench demonstrate that RubricDuo achieves the overall best performance among the evaluated automatic methods in both alignment with human-authored rubrics and downstream LLM judging.
  </details>

- **[Generalist Meets Specialists: An Evolving Multimodal Agent with Persistent Tool Orchestration for Traffic Scenario Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25868)**  `arXiv:2611.25868`  `cs.AI`  
  _Linjia Kang, Binhan Jiang, Juan Yang, Zhiqin Song, Xiangsen Wu, Qionglin Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic scenario understanding requires combining fine-grained visual evidence with spatial and temporal reasoning. Tool-augmented vision-language models (VLMs) can use specialized perception tools, but effective collaboration faces two challenges: terminal rewards provide limited guidance for intermediate decisions and error recovery, while repeated planning adds unnecessary cost across similar tasks. Echoing the fast-slow dichotomy of human cognition, we propose StarTV-Agent, which combines policy learning with experience evolution to support adaptive reasoning when needed and efficient reuse of prior experience when possible. During training, Role-Sensitive Process Optimization (RSPO) combines process advantages with terminal advantages, while Weakness-Driven Demonstration Learning (WDL) provides validated teacher demonstrations targeting the student's weak questions. During inference, Lightweight Adaptive Workflow Reuse (LAWR) enables a fast path by selecting suitable workflows and binding task-specific arguments and resource references, while a slow ReAct-based path handles tasks without suitable workflows. We further design label-free feedback based on execution completion, workflow adoption, and cost savings to guide workflow reuse and memory updates. Across three challenging benchmarks, StarTV-Agent achieves up to 8.0% relative performance improvement, while reducing token consumption by up to 52.1%.
  </details>

- **[Decoupled Knowledge Retrieval and Compact Memory Enable Reliable Lifelong Model Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25861)**  `arXiv:2611.25861`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Che, Jingze Wang, Chen Guoyi, Yujie Wu, Xuming Ran, Mingkun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lifelong model editing requires continuously incorporating new facts into deployed large language models (LLMs) without expensive retraining or compromising existing capabilities. Memory-based editors offer an appealing solution by keeping the base parameters untouched, yet their practical use is hindered by linearly growing storage requirements and deteriorating retrieval performance as edits accumulate. To tackle these problems, we introduce BiSpace, a lifelong model editing framework that decouples memory retrieval from knowledge injection across two distinct representation spaces. BiSpace routes queries and identifies relevant edits in an external semantic space, while injecting the corresponding knowledge into the internal representation space of the LLM through sparse and storage-efficient updates. Extensive experiments show that BiSpace shrinks each entry's storage to only about 10.2% of the original and shows strong resilience to long edit sequences of up to 16K edits. On a more challenging benchmark, BiSpace further improves Overall Performance by more than 23% over existing methods. These results advance memory-based editing toward reliable long-term model maintenance at scale.
  </details>

- **[Inject Where It Matters: Objective-Dependent Feature Control in Rectified-Flow Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25811)**  `arXiv:2611.25811`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nina Goncharova, Alexandra Ivanova, Aibek Alanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many prompt-driven FLUX editing methods rely on injecting source features into target-conditioned generation, creating an inherent trade-off between source preservation and the desired edit. We address this problem by controlling where source features are injected and which transformer blocks are used. First, we introduce adaptive masks that spatially gate feature injection using differences between source- and target-conditioned block outputs at matched inversion states. Second, we use REINFORCE to select Top-K injection blocks under different preservation-editability objectives. The selected blocks are fixed at inference and require no online search. On PIE-Bench with FLUX.1-dev, spatial masking controls the alignment-preservation trade-off. On 60 validation examples, a learned nine-block set gives lower whole-image LPIPS than the 18-block RF-Edit set within the same masked pipeline, with slightly lower alignment. Among 50 random nine-block sets, it has the lowest LPIPS but not the highest reward. Separate objective sweeps reveal different block preferences for preservation and alignment.
  </details>

- **[Duopra: From Full-Duplex Conversation to Full-Duplex Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25773)**  `arXiv:2611.25773`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chiyu Chen, Pengzhou Cheng, Yuezhao Ma, Xiaocheng Li, Can Liu, Haonan Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-duplex interaction models exhibit strong conversational agency, enabling natural, low-latency turn-taking, backchannels, and interruptions. However, completing tasks further requires task agency: timely tool invocation and argument revision following user corrections. Frontend-backend agents separate initiation from execution: the frontend decides whether and what to request, while the backend determines how. Execution can be offloaded, but unsent requests cannot be executed, and incomplete ones can misdirect the backend. On Full-Duplex-Bench v3 (FDB v3), most failures of Realtime-Venus-Audio + Harness in our streaming pipeline occur before a domain tool executes. We introduce Duopra, a full-duplex interaction model that learns task execution alongside conversational control. Duopra treats the backend as a tool, accepting a self-contained natural-language request. Direct calls and backend delegation share an action interface and requirements for faithful initiation and correct execution. Its three-stage training combines protocol SFT, full-duplex RL rewarding timely and complete execution, and projector-only repair of talker conditioning after thinker updates. With the same backend, Pass@1 rises from 4.7% to 44.7%; Duopra achieves 47.7% calling tools directly (26.0% after SFT) and 49.3% with a DeepSeek backend. It improves three FDB v1.0 takeover rates over the base model. These results show that task agency can coexist with key conversational agency.
  </details>

- **[CompAct-Bench: A Benchmark for Working-Context Compaction in Long-Horizon Agent Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25673)**  `arXiv:2611.25673`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayi Chen, Lizhe Chen, Yujia Chen, Shihan Dou, Ming Zhang, Chendi Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As language-model agents are applied to increasingly extended tasks, their working context continuously accumulates interaction history, tool observations, intermediate decisions, and task progress. Context compaction offers a practical way to reduce this growing history while allowing execution to continue, yet the compaction ability of modern LLMs remains insufficiently understood. We introduce CompAct-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating working-context compaction through its downstream effect on agent task completion. Starting from successful agent trajectories, we insert compaction boundaries at different stages of execution, compress the preceding working context with the model under evaluation, and let the same executor continue the original task. To enable fair comparison across compactors, we evaluate them under controlled retention ratios using a lightweight iterative ratio-adjustment procedure. CompAct-Bench covers information-seeking, software-engineering, and workspace-oriented tasks through BrowseComp, SWE-bench, and an internal CompanyBench evaluation. Our experiments show that working-context compaction remains highly lossy even for frontier models: Claude- and GPT-family models perform strongest overall, yet substantial degradation in downstream task accuracy persists under aggressive compaction. Further analysis reveals that failures are often caused not only by omitted information, but also by distorted task state, such as turning unresolved issues into apparent conclusions or forgetting previously established results, which can lead to incorrect decisions or redundant execution. These findings highlight working-context compaction as a distinct and still challenging ability for LLMs operating in extended agent tasks.
  </details>

- **[Don't Lose the Future: Latent-Aware 4-Bit Quantization of World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25614)**  `arXiv:2611.25614`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yichen Guo, Yanlong Chen, Xinyue Zhang, Mohan Liu, Song Zhang, Yawei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) predict how a scene will evolve and condition their actions on this predicted future. Deploying them on a robot's onboard computer calls for 4-bit weights, activations and key-value caches (W4A4KV4). Existing 4-bit quantizers for WAMs preserve the generated action, and estimate sensitivity one denoising step at a time. However, they fail to preserve the fidelity of the predicted future: once the action pathway is protected, the remaining action error enters mostly through a degraded future that none of them deliberately protects. We propose LAQ, a latent-aware quantization framework that preserves the predicted future where it is written, stored and read. Specifically, we introduce a reader metric, which measures future error by its effect on the action, and a read count, which weights each rounding error by the number of denoising steps that read it. Together they give the allocation of precision over activations and caches a closed-form solution. We further develop gaze-guided refinement, which gives the tokens the action attends to a second 4-bit pass in the denoising loop and in the cache. All weights stay at 4 bits, with no high-precision branch and no per-task recalibration. On the LIBERO-Plus benchmark, LAQ reaches 76.4% and 83.3% average success on Fast-WAM and Cosmos-Policy, within 1.2 percentage points of the 16-bit models and all above the baselines with 16-bit KV caches. It compresses the model by 3.8x, reduces the peak GPU memory of the whole policy by 2.3x, and runs 1.66x faster than the 16-bit model. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, it reaches 47.0% average success over three tasks, against 52.0% for the 16-bit model and 30.0% higher than QuaRot.
  </details>

- **[Learning Networked Dynamical Systems via Neural ODE-based Graph Structure Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25586)**  `arXiv:2611.25586`  `cs.AI`  
  _Peiyan Sun, Jinming Yang, Gujia Gao, Yaohui Jin, Yanyan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inferring the latent interaction graph from multivariate time series is fundamental to understanding complex dynamical systems in neuroscience, biology, and physics. Despite recent progress in neural relational inference, most methods rely on discrete-time modeling, which degrades under sparse and irregular observations and tends to entangle multi-hop dependencies into spurious direct edges. We show that a continuous-time formulation naturally decouples inference from any fixed observation schedule, allowing gradient signals to be integrated continuously between observations for both dynamics and structure learning. We propose NOGI (Neural ODE-based Graph Inference), a framework that couples a deterministic graph learner with an MPNN-based Neural ODE. The two modules are trained end-to-end via the adjoint method. Across four simulated dynamical systems at two scales, NOGI achieves the best structure recovery in 30 out of 32 evaluated cases with highly competitive forecasting performance, remaining robust under observation degradation. On the realistic Netsim fMRI benchmark, it attains an average AUPRC of 0.90 (AUROC 0.96). These results suggest that explicitly decoupling relational interactions from self-dynamics within a continuous-time vector field provides a powerful mechanism for graph structure learning under realistic observation conditions.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Multimodal Agents for Auditing 3D Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25543)**  `arXiv:2611.25543`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingbo Yang, Ziyan Jiang, Li An, Jiabao Ji, Yujian Liu, Tommi Jaakkola, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents can now build interactive 3D worlds and games, yet these worlds, like those built by people, often contain glitches such as floating objects, walls that can be walked through, or objects that change when no one is looking. An automatic auditor could check such worlds at scale and provide rewards for agents that build them, but only if it is both accurate and cheap. Auditing requires three abilities, namely navigating to the objects that may contain a glitch, triggering glitches that appear only after a specific behavior, and recognizing what is wrong. Current auditors are either accurate but slow and expensive or cheap but unreliable. We study how much of the audit small models can take on. A 4B vision language model fine-tuned end to end on verified trajectories of a frontier teacher guided by Bayesian search learns to navigate and trigger, and six such explorers together match a frontier model on both, but recognition stays far behind. We therefore build an auditor in which a swarm of small explorers navigates and triggers, and a frontier model reviews what they record and report. On WorldAuditBench, our auditor succeeds on 55.9% of 213 tasks in Unreal Engine 5 andthis http URLworlds, 13.6 points above the strongest auditor reported, at less than a quarter of its cost. It matches the frontier reviewer exploring on its own at 4.6 times lower cost and about half the latency. Without further training, it also answers embodied questions about real-world scans, matching the frontier reviewer exploring on its own and surpassing four other frontier models by 6.4 to 16.6 points.
  </details>

- **[How Feedback-Free Self-Evolution Reshapes Agent Skills: A Systematic Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25534)**  `arXiv:2611.25534`  `cs.AI`  
  _Litao Guo, Man Chen, Xinli Xu, Jinsong Zhou, Zixin Zhang, Ying-Cong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills are documents in which experts write down procedural knowledge, and models increasingly maintain them by rewriting them without feedback. Such rewriting could adapt a skill to the model that uses it or erode the expert knowledge that makes it useful, yet how skills evolve under this feedback-free self-evolution has not been studied. We let models rewrite expert skills round after round without any task, run or judgment and run agents built on the same models with the rewritten skills. Our experiments show that the skills lose value rather than improve, as every model we test performs worse with them on every benchmark, by 15 points on average on SkillsBench after thirty rounds. Sampling each model's prior with skills it writes from the topic alone, we find that rewriting pulls skills toward this prior and gives up first the knowledge outside it. Putting this knowledge back recovers much of the lost pass rate, so an expert's skill is valuable largely for what the model does not write on its own. Building on this insight, SkillDiff marks this knowledge in place and raises the pass rate on SkillsBench by 4.8 points on average without feedback or any change to the skill's text. Project repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Keystone: Judging Previews at Key Steps for Test-Time Search in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25530)**  `arXiv:2611.25530`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Chaoran Cheng, Ziwen Wang, Changyi Yang, Bangji Yang, Qinglong Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image diffusion and flow models yield a preview, their clean-image estimate, at every denoising step. When they miscount, drop or misplace objects, test-time search is in a bind: its verifiers are reward models built for finished images, so judging early misreads blurry previews, and judging late, as best-of-N does, pays to fully denoise doomed candidates. We find that the image is not the problem: the outcome is settled as denoising starts, by the seed or by a stochastic sampler's first steps, and already shows in early previews; what fails on them is the judge, as reward models read them near chance on several kinds of prompts. We introduce Keystone, a training-free judge of previews that compiles a prompt into typed questions about its objects, counts, colours and positions and has an off-the-shelf multimodal decision model answer them. Asked this way, the judge reads counts and positions from early previews better than reward models read them from finished images. Searching at these key steps on GenEval with SD3.5-Medium, Keystone's adaptive search and pruning significantly outperform best-of-8 with every other verifier at under half its generator compute, and as a drop-in verifier there, the Keystone score significantly improves best-of-N, progressive seed pruning and FK steering; without retuning, both searches stay significantly above the strongest other verifier's best-of-N on average over four base models and three benchmarks. Keystone thus moves test-time judgment to the key steps where the outcome already shows.
  </details>

- **[Why Do LLMs Help with Scientific Misconduct Requests and What to Do About It?: Increasing the Trustworthiness of AI for Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25492)**  `arXiv:2611.25492`  `cs.AI`  
  _Almene De Meran Meguimtsop, Daniel E. Acuna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) support scientific research, but some of that assistance can also undermine research integrity. Existing safety evaluations in science often measure whether models refuse misconduct requests, but rarely explain whether they even recognize the problem or why they comply. We investigate this gap across 8 LLMs using SciIntBench, an existing benchmark of 1,350 prompts about scientific research integrity. We examine the reasons behind compliance in four steps. First, when asked directly whether fulfilling a request would involve misconduct, models overwhelmingly identify such requests, even in the Most Covert cases (91%), while they refuse to carry out the request in only 34% of cases. Second, we examine which request characteristics are associated with refusal using features that capture how the request is framed, how much the assistant has been told about what it is helping to hide, and how the goal and the user's stake are stated, among others. Third, we estimate that the most significant effect was to rephrase requests as questions about permissibility rather than instructions, which increased refusal from 52% to 88%. Finally, we test two system-prompt fixes: asking models to assess the request before responding and reminding them of responsible conduct of research principles. These changes increase refusal rates on adversarial requests from 58% to 87% and 86%, respectively. Our findings show that models recognize wrongful requests but still help users violate integrity. Understanding why these failures occur and testing ways to reduce them offer promising paths toward more trustworthy AI for science.
  </details>

- **[Falsifiable Compositional Reasoning in Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25388)**  `arXiv:2611.25388`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hao Xue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models as well as the language-guided analytical agents achieve strong accuracy on standard chronological benchmarks. However, models that rank highest on historical validation splits often degrade across non-stationary regime transitions. We hypothesise that this gap arises from using historical backtesting as an open-ended search and model-selection objective instead of using it to test pre-specified compositional assumptions. Real-world time series are generated by recurring latent primitives that co-move within finite historical windows. Under strong historical co-movement, chronological holdout error cannot separate predictors that learn invariant couplings among primitives from predictors that fit transient historical correlations. Searching over large pools of architectures, hyperparameters, or chain-of-thought rules further biases selection toward historical shortcuts. Through theoretical analysis and diagnostic experiments on a controlled multi-primitive benchmark and German day-ahead electricity prices, we show that expanding validation search fails to improve out-of-regime accuracy, and that decomposing primitives without an ex-ante falsification test still breaks down when residual correlations shift. Hence, we propose formulating time series forecasting and reasoning around falsifiable compositional hypotheses that combine neural primitive encoders, explicit coupling constraints, and training-calibrated falsification tests.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Weight Updates: Evolving Agent Harnesses for Domain Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25380)**  `arXiv:2611.25380`  `cs.AI`  
  _BoHao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain specialization of LLM agents is usually framed as a weight-update problem: collect trajectories, run reinforcement learning (RL) or supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and deploy a new checkpoint. We study a complementary axis: evolving the \textbf{agent harness}---the system prompt, tool schemas, skill library, and planning scaffolding---while keeping the policy frozen. We cast harness evolution as an RL-style outer loop and instantiate it with GEPA, a reflective evolutionary optimizer. The resulting harness adapters require no GPU training, are loadable per vertical domain, and compose with weight-based RL. Experiments with Codev, a Claude Code-style coding agent, on SWE-bench Verified, OfficeBench, and Terminal-Bench show a consistent pattern: weight updates give the strongest single-path gains, harness evolution recovers most of those gains at a small fraction of the compute, and combining both paths is best overall. We also analyze a failure mode of SFT-based specialization---shortened reasoning chains and increased truncation---and argue that harness adapters avoid it by construction. Our results position harness evolution as a practical, non-destructive complement to weight updates for vertical agents.
  </details>

- **[Stop Going in Circles:Loop-Triggered Routing for System 1 Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25363)**  `arXiv:2611.25363`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zeming Yu, Huamin Ma, Yingyu Chen, Yuan Sun, Peng Hu, Yang Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System 1 decision models (represented by Jev) provide fast, low-cost action selection, but existing agent integrations do not reliably translate these local advantages into task completion. At certain points during execution, an agent may repeatedly select the same actions without making progress toward its goal. LLM-based policies offer strong reasoning capabilities that support planning within an agent, but invoking them at every step incurs substantial overhead. The effective coordination should therefore preserve Jev's fast local action selection while introducing LLM reasoning when execution stalls. We observe that recurring state-action pairs can signal stalled execution. We therefore propose LoopRoute, a simple but effective action router that uses this signal to allocate control within an agent. The decision model acts by default. When a loop is detected, the LLM provides brief, feedback-driven assistance and then returns control to the decision model. Later loops can request further assistance. The rule requires neither additional training nor model-specific confidence calibration. Across three benchmarks and seven LLMs, LoopRoute improves task success over standalone Jev with only limited use of the LLM policy. The mean gains are 21.0%, 60.1%, and 30.1% on ALFWorld, MapReader, and TextWorld cooking, respectively. Compared with full LLM control, LoopRoute also substantially reduces the mean cumulative API time and API-call cost per task. The same rule also improves the success rates of six frozen decision models.
  </details>

- **[AppSci-Bench: Can LLMs Build AI Agents and Make Them Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25360)**  `arXiv:2611.25360`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sanjari Srivastava, Manpreet Kaur, Cheng Chang, Sofía Ramós Anton, Oumaima Essamadi, Rishu Garg, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Neurosymbolic Multimodal Framework for 30-Day Diabetic Readmission Prediction: Integrating Structured EHR Data, Dense Clinical Embeddings, and Knowledge-Grounded Symbolic Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25236)**  `arXiv:2611.25236`  `cs.AI`  
  _Una Suman Kumar Patro, Sibangi Subhadarsani, Narendra Badam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unplanned 30-day readmissions among patients with diabetes mellitus impose a substantial clinical and economic burden, yet most predictive models use only structured electronic health record (EHR) data and ignore free-text clinical notes, which often contain code-mixed Hindi-English (Hinglish) phrasing and negation that general-purpose language models parse unreliably. We propose a neurosymbolic multimodal framework evaluated on the public Diabetes 130-US Hospitals dataset (101,766 encounters; 71,518 patients) with a patient-level grouped 80/20 split. Three streams are fused: engineered structured features; 32 components of Bio_ClinicalBERT note embeddings obtained by truncated singular value decomposition; and symbolic entities extracted by an open-weight clinical language model within a three-stage hybrid retrieval-augmented generation pipeline (dense retrieval, sparse lexical retrieval, and cross-encoder re-ranking) with reflective negation auditing. A cost-sensitive XGBoost classifier with a decision threshold calibrated by Youden's J statistic produces the prediction. On the held-out test cohort (n = 20,153), the structured baseline achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.6477, sensitivity of 0.5839, and Brier score of 0.1080. The full multimodal model achieved the highest sensitivity (0.6667; +8.28 percentage points), the best F2-score (0.3824), and the lowest Brier score (0.0977), at a slightly lower AUROC (0.6375) and specificity (0.5161). Shapley additive explanations showed that prior utilization dominates risk, with compressed narrative components among the top predictors. On a 500-note code-switched benchmark, grounded retrieval raised Hinglish symptom recall from 4.8% to 40.2% and precision from 3.1% to 45.2%, while reducing entity hallucinations from 1.74 to 0.00 per note.
  </details>

- **[A Verifier from the Past: Test-Time Compute for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25171)**  `arXiv:2611.25171`  `cs.AI`  
  _Arjun Choudhry, Aditya Narayanan, Raghav Mallampalli, Po-han Li, Sai Shankar Narasimhan, Raimi Shah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time compute (TTC) improves language models by spending additional inference compute to generate, verify, and select among candidate outputs, but this recipe has not transferred directly to time series forecasting. A language model can verify a proof or vote across samples, but a forecaster must be causal; it cannot use future information to score a candidate. We propose Backtest-Gated Augmentation Averaging (BGAA), a TTC algorithm for time series forecasting that builds a causal verifier from the series's own history, using rolling-origin backtests to score structured views of the context, discard those that underperform the untransformed context, and average the survivors' predictive distributions. BGAA improves every tested TSFM-benchmark combination, reducing MASE by 0.5-1.0% on GIFT-Eval, 1.3-5.0% on fev-bench, and 1.7-5.9% on BOOM, where a 313M model with TTC outperforms the 1B and 2.5B models in its family. We further show that useful views have structured, model-dependent effects consistent with a prior-transport interpretation. On the 53 cross-fittable GIFT-Eval configs, the deployed policy gains only 0.4-1.3% CRPS, versus 1.4-4.1% when choosing the best view for each dataset configuration and 17-19% when choosing the best view separately for each test window. This gap suggests that a major remaining challenge is window-level view selection.
  </details>

- **[EverydayBench: Evaluating Satisfactory Completion of Live User-Facing Digital Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25146)**  `arXiv:2611.25146`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fangrui Huang, Bingyang Wang, Chuhan Li, Yijiang Li, Arpandeep Khatua, Austin Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Everyday digital tasks, from selecting products and planning trips to conducting research and creating artifacts, require AI agents to deliver useful outcomes rather than merely retrieve web information. Yet evaluating these outcomes on the live web is challenging, as a single request may admit multiple valid results, and changing web information can alter which results remain valid over time. Although existing benchmarks address aspects of this setting, they do not fully integrate predefined success criteria, current evidence verification, and deliverable-quality assessment for agent-selected outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce EverydayBench, a benchmark of 77 human-authored tasks spanning eight domains of everyday life and work. Each task specifies success criteria before execution without prescribing an answer or workflow, allowing agents to select their own outcomes. To evaluate submissions under fixed success criteria, EverydayBench combines (1) deterministic checks of explicit constraints, (2) verification of claims against agent-cited webpages retrieved at evaluation time, and (3) rubric-based assessment of final deliverable quality. We further validate the protocol through human judgments, repeated scoring under fixed evidence, and controlled grounding failures. Across 23 reported agent-model configurations, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1 tie for the highest strict pass rate at 54.55%; a controlled GPT-6 Sol effort sweep ranges from 29.87% at low effort to 45.45% at max effort. Reliability varies substantially by task type: GPT-5.6 Sol leads lookup, Fable 5.1 leads planning, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 tie on comparison, and GPT-6 Astra and Sonnet 5.5 tie on customized deliverables. Together, these findings highlight the difficulty of delivering complete, well-supported, and usable outcomes for everyday user requests.
  </details>

- **[SailSearch: Agent-Assisted Discovery in Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25087)**  `arXiv:2611.25087`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dhriti Verma, Kanthi Sarpatwar, Vinicius Lima, Dzung Phan, Jayant Kalagnanam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present SailSearch, a human-guided workflow for algorithmic research with two language-model agents, and report results on Market Split and maximum independent set (MIS). For Market Split, our randomized algorithm searches across multiple lattice bases, dividing computation between enumeration and continued basis reduction. Across ten random seeds per instance, it found the first reported CPU-only solutions to 18 of 33 previously open QOBLIB instances, solving 130 to 133 of the 156 benchmark instances per seed with a 24-hour cutoff on two CPU cores. For MIS, our generator encodes planted XOR constraints as graphs with certified optima; on seven instances with 1,752 to 3,996 vertices, the best solutions returned by the tested solvers in eight-hour runs were 5 to 15 vertices below the optimum.
  </details>

- **[PhysScope: Agentic Multimodal Physics Reasoning with Grounded Visual Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23010)**  `arXiv:2611.23010`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyang Liu, Kecheng Liang, Xinyu Li, Weixing Chen, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can correctly interpret physics diagrams yet apply constraints outside the states or conditions in which they hold, causing premise errors to propagate through subsequent calculations. We introduce PhysScope, an inference-time visual harness that supports self-correction of these scope mismatches without updating model weights. PhysScope enables the model to link physical scopes directly to its visual observations and explicitly constructs derived views to support reasoning. The harness imposes explicit constraints on state distinctions, conditions, and physical scopes during model reasoning, and checks agent-proposed revisions against sources and scopes. Accepted revisions invalidate registered dependents, thereby maintaining scope-aware working memory. On PhysAlign, PhysScope achieves higher solving scores than direct solving across all six evaluated VLMs. With Gemini-3.8-flash, it outperforms Canvas-CoT by 9.34 percentage points and substantially improves solving accuracy on the out-of-distribution (OOD) PhysUniBench benchmark. Ablation studies support explicit scope management and dependency maintenance as design principles for multimodal physics reasoning agents.
  </details>

- **[An Augmented Lagrangian Approach to Lifted Bellman Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14183)**  `arXiv:2611.14183`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jongchan Park, Hyukjun Yang, Narim Jeong, Donghwan Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal-difference critics regress onto a bootstrapped target and usually need a target network. Lifted Bellman Linear Programming (LBLP) instead expresses the in-sample optimal values of offline RL as the solution to a linear program over the dataset. Its neural solver ALBUM relaxes the constraints into hinge penalties with fixed coefficients, and its guarantees are conditions on coefficients chosen in advance.
