We solve the same program with the method of multipliers: the coefficients become multipliers learned by dual ascent, with one penalty parameter $\rho$. In the convex case the fixed points are the KKT pairs of LBLP, and at every stationary point the applied constraint weights balance the objective exactly. The critic can serve a Gaussian actor, a flow actor, and best-of-N sampling, and for the Gaussian actor we propose an advantage-normalized DDPG+BC objective. With a single $Q$ and $V$ and no target network, our AL-LBLP with best-of-N reaches 60.1\% average success on the 50 state-based OGBench tasks. On long-horizon tasks, it reaches 81.5% on humanoidmaze-giant and 79.8% on antmaze-giant. It does not collapse up to batch size 8192.
  </details>

- **[Releasing the Inner Jev: A Simple Recipe for Open Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17205)**  `arXiv:2610.17205`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jonathan Geuter, Andrew Cheng, David Alvarez-Melis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based decision-making systems, also called System One models, have recently gained popularity, most notably through Jev (Almeida, 2026). Given a typed input state, a question, and a set of options, they return a calibrated probability for each option in a single forward pass, without text generation. We show that open instruction-tuned LLMs can achieve performance comparable to Jev without any additional training, by reading out answers from letters (A, B, C, etc.) in the model's output logits at the last token position. Next, we propose a simple supervised LoRA fine-tuning recipe that can improve their performance further. We evaluate four instruction-tuned LLMs: Gemma 4 31B, Gemma 4 12B, Qwen3.6-27B, and Qwen3.5-4B. Furthermore, we introduce OpenDecisions, a novel 2,352-question validation dataset designed to reflect the task composition of JevBench (JevBench maintainers, 2026) and which computes a Capability score based on accuracy and calibration. Without fine-tuning, Gemma 4 31B scores 79.6 on OpenDecisions, compared to Jev's 80.1. After fine-tuning, it scores 89.0, outperforming Jev by 8.9 Capability points and on par with the strongest open decision model we evaluated. The same recipe improves the three other models by 7.8 to 15.7 points. On a suite of public decision datasets, our models achieve accuracy and calibration comparable to Jev. Our results show that open LLMs already possess System One capabilities, and with simple fine-tuning they outperform Jev on a wide range of decision tasks. We release our models, training and serving code, OpenDecisions, and DecisionBank, our training dataset consisting of 261,561 decisions.
  </details>

- **[UpliftMem: Learning Set-Level Uplift for Agent Memory Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36805)**  `arXiv:2609.36805`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mengkun Liang, Haoran Qiang, Guannan Liu, Junjie Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents draw on external memory to guide new tasks, raising the question of which memories to retrieve together to improve execution. Learning set values requires outcome feedback, but evaluating each untested alternative costs an additional rollout. We introduce \textsc{UpliftMem}, which learns set-level execution uplift relative to the same executor without memory. Our analysis of how retrieval preferences limit feedback coverage motivates targeted probes. Under a correlated Gaussian surrogate, we derive a closed-form expected value of sample information (EVSI) criterion that prioritizes probes by their expected benefit to local retrieval decisions. The feedback trains a shared scorer with the executor frozen, and inference requires no probing. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and BigCodeBench, \textsc{UpliftMem} achieves best or joint-best success rates among evaluated methods. On fixed-store MemSyco-Bench evaluations, decision accuracy increases from 41.7\% to 66.5\% with Qwen3-4B and from 44.4\% to 68.8\% with Qwen3-8B. At matched probe budgets, EVSI improves decision accuracy over random probing by 10.6 and 13.9 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Real-Time Voice Agents: A Survey from Component Quality to Grounded Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30798)**  `arXiv:2609.30798`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shivam Negi, Arpit Rawat, Rashi Jain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time voice agents must decide when to speak, recover from disruption, and act only when the conversation requires it. But currently their evaluation is fragmented and is split across speech quality, turn-taking, tool calling, and task completion. We review 136 primary works published up to October 2, 2026, and code each on two axes: the capability an evaluation exercises, namely timing, recovery, grounded outcome, and, where relevant, multiparty conduct; and the strength of its evidence, namely evaluation conditions and grading instrument. The coding locates the gaps. Only 13 of 91 evaluation works check external state or action traces, only 5 of 21 grounded-outcome benchmarks use real human speech, and only 2 of 26 multiparty benchmarks verify state. Three claims follow. Architecture constrains deployment but does not determine results. It has been observed that benchmarks can verify outcomes, but few do. Evaluating an agent solely through one-on-one user interaction leaves critical gaps: it cannot confirm if the agent is speaking to the correct person, verify that person's authority, or identify the proper moments for strategic silence. From the taxonomy we derive TRG, short for Timing-Recovery-Grounded, a minimum reporting standard, and apply it to three published reports with different architectures. In one, tool-selection F1 is 82.5% while exact-call success is 33.0%, with no evidence of external task state. None of the three reports measures task state after an interruption. TRG governs what is reported and assigns no composite score. Per-source codes and tooling accompany the submission.
  </details>

- **[Measurement-Budget Allocation in Quantum Learning with Finite-Shot Generalization Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24891)**  `arXiv:2608.24891`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ferhat Ozgur Catak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On near-term quantum hardware, estimating a Born probability requires repeated circuit executions. A quantum learning experiment with a fixed measurement budget $B$ must therefore decide how many distinct training states $n$ to use and how many shots $S$ to spend on each. We study this trade-off for binary quantum classifiers whose measurement operator $M$ is learned from the training data and evaluated with fresh measurement outcomes. We prove a distribution-free generalization bound for bounded, $L$-Lipschitz losses with a sample term of order $\sqrt{d/n}$ and a shot term of order $\sqrt{(\log n)/S}$; under $B=nS$ the two terms move in opposite directions. Minimizing a closed-form surrogate of the bound gives the allocation rule $n^* = 2\sqrt{2dB/\log(4B/\delta)}$, $S^* = B/n^*$, whose bound value is within 0.1% of the exact numerical minimum on all budgets we evaluate, and a worst-case rate of $B^{-1/4}$. PennyLane simulations with 2- and 4-qubit variational circuits on nine synthetic benchmarks show that the empirical gap stays below the bound in all configurations, although the bound is vacuous at the simulated budgets and becomes non-vacuous only for larger budgets (e.g., $B \gtrsim 5000$ for $d=4$). For a loss that is concave in the score, the empirical gap is U-shaped under a fixed budget and the measured shot contribution matches its analytical value; for the absolute loss, shot noise is unbiased and no shot-limited regime appears. The rule can be used to plan finite-shot evaluations before an experiment is run.
  </details>

- **[Cautious Context Steering for Language Model Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05813)**  `arXiv:2608.05813`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gihoon Kim, Jeyoung Lee, Suhan Woo, Jangwon Oh, Sekwon Oh, Minsu Jeon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User context can personalize a language model without per-user fine-tuning, but its influence is not equally helpful at every token. Context Steering (CoS) uses a fixed steering strength throughout generation and requires two LM forward passes per step. We propose Cautious Context Steering (CCS), a lightweight adapter that learns when and how strongly to steer a frozen LM using user context. We train the adapter through cautious distillation, using oracle targets built from predictions with and without context. It learns to match the steered distribution where context helps predict the user's preferred token and the context-free base distribution elsewhere. The CCS adapter outperforms both training-free context-based methods and trained personalization baselines in preference alignment across benchmarks. We also introduce GSM8K-P to evaluate task accuracy and task-specific format preferences together. CCS achieves high format adherence while maintaining task accuracy, offering a better balance than the baselines. The CCS adapter adds less than 0.4% of the backbone's parameters. At inference, each decoding step uses one context-conditioned LM forward pass and a lightweight adapter evaluation, reducing generation time on PRISM by approximately 35-36% compared with CoS.
  </details>

- **[Post-Reasoning: Improving the Performance of Non-Thinking Models at Negligible Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06165)**  `arXiv:2605.06165`  `cs.AI`  
  _Richmond Sin Jing Xuan, Rishabh Bhardwaj, Soujanya Poria_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) accelerates, token consumption from intermediate reasoning traces increasingly contributes to inference latency and operational cost. Recent studies suggest that many real-world tasks require little to no explicit reasoning, with additional reasoning sometimes even degrading performance. In this work, we propose Post-Reasoning, a simple yet effective approach that improves instruction-tuned models by conditioning them to justify their answers after generating the final response. By design, it enables the final answer to be obtained with no reasoning tokens before the answer and with negligible latency and token cost, while still improving performance through simple instruction augmentation. We evaluate Post-Reasoning across 117 model benchmark settings spanning 13 open and proprietary models, 7 model families, and 9 diverse reasoning and knowledge-intensive benchmarks, including AMC, HMMT, GSM8K, GPQA, MMLU-Pro, and BIG-Bench Hard. Post-Reasoning improves performance in 84.62% of evaluated settings, achieving a mean relative improvement of 15.57%. Furthermore, we propose supervised post-reason tuning, which further improves performance in 91.11% of evaluated settings, and exceeds the prompt-based post-reasoning baseline by an average of 7.17%, demonstrating that post-reasoning can be effectively internalized through training. Ultimately, Post-Reasoning establishes a new performance ceiling for direct-answer capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Agent^2 RL-Bench: Can LLM Agents Engineer Agentic RL Post-Training?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10547)**  `arXiv:2604.10547`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanyi Chen, Xiao Yang, Xu Yang, Tianming Sha, Qizheng Li, Zhuo Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Agent^2 RL-Bench, a compact diagnostic benchmark for evaluating agentic RL post-training, which tests whether LLM agents can autonomously design, implement, debug, and execute post-training pipelines that improve foundation models. RL post-training increasingly drives model alignment and specialization, yet existing benchmarks are largely static, rewarding supervised fine-tuning or script generation without assessing an agent's ability to close an interactive RL loop. Agent^2 RL-Bench provides a unified agent-facing interface: each run starts from an isolated workspace containing a base model, task data, instructions, and a grading API, and agents must iterate within a fixed budget by training models and submitting artifacts for evaluation. The benchmark spans six tasks across three levels, from static rule-based training to judge-based optimization and closed-loop online RL with trajectory collection. Two diagnostic skills, namely runtime recording and post-hoc summarization, enable structured analysis of agent behavior, facilitating smooth and effective iteration of the benchmark's evaluation framework. Across five agent systems and six driver LLMs, agents show intelligent behavior but clear limitations: one RL-oriented run improves ALFWorld from 4.85 to 93.28 via SFT warm-up and GRPO with online rollouts, yet DeepSearchQA remains difficult, most successful routes rely on supervised pipelines, and interactive outcomes show large differences across agent stacks. Overall, Agent2 RL-Bench shows that current agents can sometimes engineer online RL, but stable agent-driven RL post-training remains rare under fixed budgets. It also demonstrates that our benchmark provides a strong and effective evaluation framework for future research in this direction. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AI GameStore: Scalable, Open-Ended Evaluation of Machine General Intelligence with Human Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17594)**  `arXiv:2602.17594`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lance Ying, Ryan Truong, Prafull Sharma, Kaiya Ivy Zhao, Nathan Cloos, Kelsey R. Allen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rigorously evaluating machine intelligence against the broad spectrum of human general intelligence has become increasingly important and challenging in this era of rapid technological advance. Conventional AI benchmarks typically assess only narrow capabilities in a limited range of human activity. Most are also static, quickly saturating as model capabilities improve. We propose that a more promising way to evaluate human-like general intelligence in AI systems is through a particularly strong form of general game playing: studying how and how well they play and learn to play all conceivable human games, in comparison to human players with the same level of experience, time, or other resources. We define a "human game" to be a game designed by humans for humans, and argue for the evaluative suitability of this space of all such games people can imagine and enjoy -- the "Multiverse of Human Games". Taking a first step towards this vision, we introduce the AI GameStore, a scalable and open-ended platform that uses LLMs with humans-in-the-loop to synthesize new representative human games, by automatically sourcing and adapting standardized and containerized variants of game environments from popular human digital gaming platforms. We generated 100 such games based on the top charts of Apple App Store and Steam, and evaluated seven frontier vision-language models (VLMs) on short episodes of play. The best models achieved less than 10% of the human average score on the majority of the games, and especially struggled with games that challenge world-model learning, memory and planning. We conclude with a set of next steps for building out the AI GameStore as a practical way to measure and drive progress toward human-like general intelligence in machines.
  </details>
